#170. A Mexican Artist Sang Some of His Hits. Authorities Stopped the Show, Then Arrested Him.

A concert by the singer Gabito Ballesteros was suspended after he sang songs that supposedly glorify cartel culture. The action has renewed a debate over culture and free speech.

A popular Mexican singer’s concert was halted on Friday night because he performed songs considered to be glorifying cartel culture, making him the latest and most high-profile artist to be ensnared in a government crackdown on the genre known as narcocorridos.

He was later arrested by the federal authorities in relation to an unrelated investigation.

Gabito Ballesteros, 27, whose songs have billions of streams, was performing at a fair in Querétaro State in central Mexico when authorities escorted him off the stage.

Before the show, his set list was reviewed and approved by state officials and event organizers, according to a statement issued by the fair. Songs that glorified criminal activities were explicitly excluded, in accordance with state regulations, the fair said.

Last year, the state joined several other states and municipalities throughout Mexico that effectively prohibit the public performance of so-called narcocorridos, a version of traditional Mexican ballads that celebrate drug cartels instead of historic events and everyday life. Violations were punishable with fines or even jail time.

The actions — and the genre itself — have sparked much debate in Mexico, which has been wrestling with both the effects of organized crime, particularly amid pressure from the Trump administration to crack down on cartels, and its identity as defined through popular culture. The discussion also has became a referendum on free speech.

On Friday night, Mr. Ballesteros performed an unauthorized song and local authorities took him offstage to remind him of his prior agreement, the fair said. When he returned, the fair said Mr. Ballesteros started to perform another unauthorized song, “AMG.” The 2022 hit, which references the luxurious lifestyles and fancy cars of cartel members, has nearly one billion streams on Spotify.

#166. Trump’s justice department warns Elizabeth Warren over Democrats’ plans for future investigations – live

Letter to likely chair of Senate Banking Committee is latest warning to Democrats about their potential plans after the party’s midterms success

Hello and welcome to the US politics live blog.

Senator Elizabeth Warren, the likely incoming chair of the Senate Banking Committee, was warned by the US Department of ⁠Justice of potential “significant legal consequences” if she fails ⁠to preserve records related to ⁠investigations into ​president Donald Trump.

It is the latest warning sent by the justice department to congressional Democrats about their potential plans to investigate the ⁠administration or Trump associates, Reuters reported.

Trump last week ordered the department to look into Democratic lawmakers for “threatening” people, and US attorney-general Todd Blanche subsequently indicated that ⁠his agency was prepared to battle congressional Democrats.

In a letter to Warren, US attorney Aaron ​Reitz in Houston told the Massachusetts Democrat ‌she appeared “eager to wield your ‌future gavel like a tomahawk, hunting for the scalps of president Trump’s private associates”.

Reitz told ‌her to preserve materials “relevant to your intent to weaponize congressional investigations and deprive citizens of their core constitutional rights”.

Notices to preserve records can presage investigations. Warren’s office did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Reitz shared the letter on social media and Trump reposted it to his Truth Social account several hours later.

Warren and other Democrats have signaled ‌that when their party takes control of Congress in January, they plan to investigate large corporations, White House chief of staff Stephen Miller and Elon ​Musk, the world’s richest person, among others.

Donald Trump is preparing to depart for Asia on Monday, turning his focus to economic deals and historic three-way nuclear talks with Russia and China as his administration adjusts to a new political reality in Washington. Trump’s departure comes after the often-omnipresent president had remained largely out of public sight for the past several days.

Democratic US senator Chris Van Hollen on Sunday escalated his bid to lead his party’s incoming Senate majority rather than Chuck Schumer, asserting that he has a path to victory amid a dedicated campaign that includes a series of television appearances. Van Hollen, a Maryland progressive, appeared on Sunday on CNN, ABC and Fox News as he continues to make his case for why his peers in the Senate should reject Schumer, who has led the Democratic caucus for nearly a decade.

US air travel disruptions continued for a third day with more than 10,000 flights affected on Sunday, but transportation secretary Sean Duffy predicted a return to normal by Monday even as airlines scrambled to get back on schedule following a systemwide ground stop.

After recently becoming the first Mississippi Democrat elected to the US Senate in more than four decades, Scott Colom pledged to try to ensure Trump “follows through on the commitments” he made while successfully running for a second presidency in 2024. Colom, while being interviewed by CNN host Dana Bash on Sunday, alluded to various campaign promises made by Trump – including lowering consumer prices, halting the seemingly endless wars waged under other presidential administrations, and fully releasing files related to the prosecution of Jeffrey Epstein, the late accused sex trafficker whose social circle encompassed the US and global elite.

Elon Musk and a host of other tech bosses have called for Jensen Huang’s reinstatement at Nvidia, amid reports the former chief executive is already plotting a return to the chip company just four days after his resignation.

An open letter that has more than 165,000 signatories demands that the company’s board members “bring Jensen back while an independent investigation establishes the facts”.

Huang’s resignation on Thursday ended a 33-year run at the helm of the AI company he co-founded, after reports he had at least two extramarital affairs, one with a woman accused of spying for China and another with a subordinate.

According to the Financial Times, Huang has discussed a possible return to the company with people close to him. One Huang ally told the paper they were putting together ⁠a “war room” of Silicon Valley executives to lobby ​for the former Nvidia ‌CEO’s return ‌and would try to garner support from ‌the White House.

“Everyone that matters in tech outside the frontier labs loves Jensen,” the person said, adding that the efforts had Huang’s support.

One quick note on the US Senate race in Alaska. According to the state’s election laws, if no candidate manages to cross the 50% threshold, that triggers a ranked-choice run-off where the candidate with fewest votes is eliminated but their vote goes to their second choice (which they mark on their ballot). This happens in rounds until the candidate with the most votes wins.

Donald Trump will begin his day in Washington, before traveling to Manila, Philippines for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) summit.

Trump’s first order or business will be his first meeting with the “Quad” alliance since he returned to the White House last year. This group includes Indian prime minister Narendra Modi, Australian prime minister Anthony Albanese, and Sanae Takaichi, the Japanese prime minister.

Then, later this week, Trump will take part in the first trilateral summit between China’s Xi Jinping and Russian president Vladimir Putin.

Here’s more about what we can expect this week from my colleague Robert Tait:

Democrat Mary Peltola is leading Republican incumbent Dan Sullivan in the nail-biting race for US Senate in Alaska.

With 97% of the ballots counted, Peltola – a former congresswoman – has 49.9% of the vote and only needs more than 50% to win.

At present, Sullivan only has 46% of the vote. This would be the Democrats’ ninth flip of the midterms cycle and the first time the party has won a US Senate since 2009.

On Sunday Trump continued to blast Ana Reyes, the federal judge appointed by Joe Biden, who has temporarily blocked construction on the historic Washington public golf course that the president wants to turn it ⁠into a championship-level course.

“Why would a judge want to delay taking a terrible and very unsafe golf course, one that is mocked all over D.C.(and far beyond!), instead of quickly turning it into a great and admired one?” Trump wrote on Truth Social, noting that his administration plans to appeal the ruling. “Time is of the essence, or golf seasons, jobs, prestige, and much money, will be lost!”

US air travel was slowly returning to normal on Monday after three days of widespread disruption following an extraordinary no-takeoff order issued by US aviation authorities.

The ground stop issued on Friday afternoon was only the second time such a directive had been issued nationwide in the busy US airspace since planes were grounded following the 9/11 attacks and was blamed by the Trump administration on a faulty upgrade to communications equipment.

On Monday morning, more than 250 US-related flights – those traveling within, into or out of the United States – scheduled for the same day were cancelled, according to industry tracker FlightAware.

That marked a dramatic shift from the more than 2,300 flights cancelled on Sunday.

There were delays to more than 700 US-related flights scheduled for Monday, compared to more than 10,000 the previous day, AFP reported

A progressive and onetime presidential candidate, senator Elizabeth Warren is a vocal Wall Street critic and has repeatedly condemned the Trump administration and its policies.

She is the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee, and is widely expected to become chair when Democrats ⁠take over Congress, Reuters reported.

The US Department of ⁠Justice on Sunday warned her of potential “significant legal consequences” if she fails ⁠to preserve records related to ⁠investigations into Trump.

Reitz’s letter to Warren cited her statements on investigating corporate leaders. “The ​context sends a strong ​smoke signal: you intend to target private ​individuals close to president Trump,” he wrote.

Other Democratic lawmakers who have ​received similar letters include ‌senators Richard Blumenthal ​and Elissa Slotkin ​as well as representative Robert Garcia, who is in line to lead the House Oversight Committee.

Political experts and Trump’s opponents have raised concerns about efforts by the Republican president and his administration to target their own critics and political opponents.

Donald Trump is embarking on his first overseas trip since his Republican party’s thumping midterm repudiation by US voters, in what may herald a renewed focus on foreign policy for the remainder of his presidency.

The US president will meet his Chinese and Russian counterparts, Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin, for their first trilateral summit, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) forum in the Chinese city of Shenzhen. Before that, he will land in Manila in the Philippines on Tuesday for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) summit.

At the Asean summit, Trump is expected to sign a trade agreement with Thailand, push deals with other countries and lobby fellow leaders to favour the US over China on artificial intelligence.

He will also take part in the first leaders’ meeting of the Quad partnership – consisting of the US, Japan, India and Australia – since he returned to the White House in January 2025.

White House officials said the president would be accompanied to Apec by Elon Musk, the Tesla and SpaceX entrepreneur who spent almost $500m (£380m) unsuccessfully supporting Republican candidates in this month’s midterms. The reason for Musk’s presence has not been explained.

The China leg of the trip will take place amid the continuing fallout from the Nvidia spying scandal, which prompted the company’s chief executive, Jensen Huang, to resign amid revelations he had had an affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Trump is under pressure to raise the matter with Xi when they hold a bilateral meeting on the sidelines of Apec.

The US president has said he does not plan to bring up the subject; administration officials insist he will not do so and that the meeting will be dedicated to implementing a trade agreement between the two superpowers.

Democratic US senator Chris Van Hollen on Sunday escalated his bid to lead his party’s incoming Senate majority rather than Chuck Schumer, asserting that he has a path to victory amid a dedicated campaign that includes a series of television appearances.

Van Hollen, a Maryland progressive, appeared on Sunday on CNN, ABC and Fox News as he continues to make his case for why his peers in the Senate should reject Schumer, who has led the Democratic caucus for nearly a decade.

On CNN’s State of the Union, he reaffirmed that he believes he has a path to victory, citing support and a widespread openness to change within the Senate Democratic caucus.

“This really is a response to the call of the voters, who clearly rejected Donald Trump and his awful policies, but they haven’t fully hired the Democrats,” Van Hollen said, alluding to how the US president’s Republican party lost control of Congress during the 3 November midterm elections.

“They’ve given us a chance, and I think we need to earn their trust. And I think that begins by not going back to business as usual.”

Van Hollen criticized reports that Schumer’s team may move the Senate caucus leadership election date earlier than 1 December. He contended that the caucus should have a debate about the group’s direction as the Democrats assume control of Congress in January for the second half of Trump’s second presidency.

#165. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#163. In Europe, an Emboldened Iran Contributes to a Sense of Anxiety

Prosecutors and government officials believe Iran or its proxies are behind a series of security breaches and threats in Britain and Europe, unsettling the region.

On Veterans Day this past week, President Trump declared that his decision to go to war with Iran had been a complete success.

“When this conflict is over, and it will be soon,” he said, speaking on a blustery morning from Arlington National Cemetery outside Washington, “the entire world will be a much safer and better place, more peaceful place.”

A growing number of attempted terrorist attacks across the continent and in Britain, including a foiled plot against a major American military base, arson attacks on Jewish community sites and cyberattacks that raised temperatures in refrigerators and blood banks are rattling national security officials and unnerving the public. Many have been attributed to Iran or to proxies acting on its behalf.

The sense of global unease was heightened when the U.S. government grounded all air travel for hours on Friday in what officials later said was a technical issue.

European residents had already grown frustrated before the most recent round of turmoil. The economic consequences of the war that Mr. Trump and the Israeli government started against Iran in February have piled up: Gas prices are higher because of disruptions of oil deliveries through the Strait of Hormuz. Groceries cost more. Heating homes in winter has become far less affordable.

And in recent weeks, the effect has grown darker as security officials have accused Iran or its proxies of conducting a series of terrorism-related plots. In September, U.S. prosecutors say, an Iraqi man, an Iranian operative and a senior operative for an Iran proxy group in a New York jail plotted to attack R.A.F. Fairford, an air base in England used by American bombers. They did not succeed, but the effort prompted a major security incident.

#162. Mamdani disbands controversial NYPD unit accused of using excessive force

Mayor axes strategic response group that cracked down on protesters and announces new community safety division

Zohran Mamdani, New York City’s mayor, announced Monday that he was disbanding a controversial city police unit that has faced criticism over the years from civil rights groups who have accused the unit of using excessive force and unduly aggressive tactics against protesters.

In a video released Monday morning, Mamdani said the move stemmed from a campaign pledge he made to disband the New York City police department’s strategic response group (SRG). “Today, we are keeping that promise,” Mamdani said in the video.

An accompanying joint statement from Mamdani and NYPD commissioner Jessica Tisch said the overhaul would “transform how the city prepares for and responds to protests, placing communication, de-escalation and civilian coordination at the center of our approach, while protecting New Yorkers’ constitutional right to protest”.

Along with eliminating the SRG, which was founded in 2015 ostensibly to focus on counter-terrorism, Mamdani and Tisch’s statement also said that the city planned to establish a “first-of-its-kind, civilian-led Community Dialogue and De-escalation Division (CDDD)” within the mayor’s office of community safety.

That division will not have weapons or arrest powers, unlike the SRG, according to the announcement.

The city will also establish what they described as a “specialized dialogue team” that will be known as the “First Amendment Support Team” (Fast) within the NYPD’s community affairs bureau, Mamdani and Tisch’s statement said. The creation of Fast is meant to “improve the NYPD’s protest response,” and it will consist of 30 uniformed officers trained in communication and de-escalation, the mayor and police commissioner said.

Additionally, their statement said, the NYPD will create a new patrol support division (PSD) within its patrol services bureau.

Plans call for the unit to be staffed with vetted officers who will primarily perform patrol-related duties and receive specialized crowd management training to support responses to demonstrations.

The reforms also introduce “significant new restrictions on the tactics and weapons” officers can use during protests and demonstrations – banning irritant gas, kettling and long guns. Responding officers will no longer be allowed to deploy pepper spray “as a crowd-management tool” – and “long-range acoustic devices” meant to affect protesters’ hearing “will be limited to 90 decibels,” Mamdani and Tisch said in their statement.

Under the new structure, the mayor said, the SRG’s counter-terrorism functions – “including heavy weapons deployments at critical infrastructure” – will be consolidated within the NYPD’s intelligence and counter-terrorism bureau.

The statement said that helped ensure a complete separation between the city’s counter-terrorism operations and protests protected by the rights to free speech and assembly guaranteed by the US constitution’s first amendment.

Tisch said that the new structure was “designed to consolidate our counterterrorism capabilities, provide greater support to our patrol officers and ensure we continue to respond to protests consistent with public safety and the first amendment”.

“These changes will make us a stronger, more effective department” she said.

The administration said that the SRG-related reforms come at “no additional cost and without increasing or reducing NYPD headcount”.

While civil rights groups hailed Monday’s announcement, the New York Police Benevolent Association’s president, Patrick Hendry, criticized it in a statement on social media.

“Today is not a good day for New Yorkers who just want a safe and orderly city,” said the statement from Hendry, whose organization is a labor union representing more than 21,000 active NYPD officers. “Mayor Mamdani is showing his true colors: he’s willing to put your safety and police officers’ safety at risk to appease his radical base.

“So the next time your life is disrupted by demonstrators who are blocking bridges and roadways or causing chaos on our streets, remember: Mayor Mamdani is on their side, not yours.”

Mamdani in January had reiterated promises to disband the SRG after it arrested dozens of demonstrators occupying a hotel lobby in protests opposing Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE), the federal agency at the forefront of the mass deportation campaign which Donald Trump has pursued throughout his second presidency.

Furthermore, after 2020 protests over a Minneapolis police officer’s murder of George Floyd, New York City’s department of investigation recommended that the NYPD “reevaluate the central role” of the SRG “in response to large protests given their orientation to handle counterterrorism, riots, and other serious threats, and better calibrate their use to circumstances that require such specialized force”.

#160. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#158. Is Italy the Model for Europe’s Future?

Over more than a quarter-century, I’ve watched Europe’s politics become more like Italy’s, with all the resulting instability, as the far right came in from the cold.

Jason Horowitz, previously bureau chief in Rome, is now bureau chief in Madrid. This article is adapted from his recent book, “The Italian Job: A Memoir of an American Reporter in Rome.”

When I first moved to Rome, I spent my time living in the past.

At the turn of the century, I drifted through a vintage postcard of postwar Italy: old women sitting around tables they had dragged into the street, old men playing cards in caps, cats arching their backs on the hoods of snub-nosed Fiats.

As newspapers filled with talk of Silvio Berlusconi, a media mogul with ambitions and appetites worthy of a Roman emperor, my meanderings inevitably took me toward Tiber Island, a sliver of land in the middle of the river that divides Rome.

An ancient bridge on the island’s left bank had a strange statue of weather-beaten faces positioned back-to-back as if they couldn’t stand one another. The heads rose out of the shared torso of a square pillar and represented Janus, Rome’s god of the past and future, the deity of transitions and change.

Back then, I didn’t see the change around me, but now, more than a quarter-century later, after spending decades reporting on Italian politics and changes across Europe, I have come to understand that I was living in the future.

In the time since, I have seen an Italianization of politics all around the continent, with coalitions of small parties resulting in instability, with fed-up voters desperate for new saviors. But more than anything, I have seen — in Britain, France, Germany — countries following the Italian model by allowing the far right into the political mainstream.

#157. Beckham firm sues after fragrance found for sale in Poundland

Brand reacted with ‘shock and dismay’ after products were also found in a ‘bargain bin inside a gas station’

David Beckham’s company reacted with “shock and dismay” after discovering his branded fragrance range was on sale in petrol stations and in Poundland, court documents have revealed.

The former England football captain’s brand, DB Ventures, has launched a lawsuit against the distributor of the products, arguing it allowed them to be sold in stores that had not been agreed upon.

DB Ventures claimed to have discovered the perfumes for sale in the troubled UK budget retailer and in a “bargain bin inside a gas station” at an unspecified location, according to court papers filed in New York.

Lawyers acting for Beckham’s company said it reflected “desperation and greed” by the perfume company, Coty.

Coty, which is headquartered in the Netherlands, has worked with Beckham’s brand for more than a decade and has produced more than 30 different fragrances bearing his name. In the UK, they sell at about £20 per bottle.

Lawyers claimed that Coty sold at least $24m (£18m) of Beckham products through “unapproved distribution channels”.

In the court filing, they said: “DB Ventures, the licensor of the David Beckham brand, ensures that all of its brand licensing partnerships maintain the lifestyle and luxury image of the brand that Sir David has carefully cultivated for decades.

“Imagine, then, the shock and dismay experienced when DB Ventures discovered that David Beckham products were being offered for sale in a gas station. How could this possibly have happened? Desperation and greed.”

The legal documents, filed at the supreme court in New York and first reported by the Daily Mail, allege that Coty had “distributed David Beckham fragrances through the budget retailer Poundland, allowing the product to be sold for dollar store prices and associating the David Beckham brand with cheap, discount products.”

The lawyers asked for compensation and damages totalling $41m. They also claimed Coty under-reported net sales by $123m, which would mean that DB Ventures lost $6m in unpaid royalties.

Coty filed its defence, asking for the case to be dismissed. It denied that it had ever sold the fragrances in Poundland, but argued that it had the right to decide where it wanted to sell its David Beckham range.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

Beckham, who retired from professional football in 2013, has built a multimillion-pound business empire, DRJB Holdings, around his brand. The group is made up of three divisions, the largest of which is DB Ventures, which covers most of his commercial partnerships with brands such as Hugo Boss.

The Seven Global division contains licensing agreements with companies such as Adidas, and the remainder of the business is Studio 99, the production studio that made Netflix documentaries about the lives of himself and of his wife, the former Spice Girl and fashion designer Victoria Beckham.

This year David Beckham received a $51m dividend from the group, after a series of lucrative brand deals for the Fifa World Cup. He appeared in about a dozen ad campaigns during the tournament this summer, working with brands such as Lenovo, McDonald’s and Bank of America.

The Beckhams are together worth an estimated £1.2bn, according to the most recent Sunday Times Rich List.

DB Ventures and Coty were approached for comment.

#153. Under-threat lidos get lifeline as Andy Burnham sets up fund to rebuild pools

Move reflects growing demand for outdoor swimming as Britain heats up and follows campaign by MPs

Andy Burnham is setting up a public fund to rebuild the UK’s lidos after a revival in outdoor swimming.

Outdoor pools were built across the UK in the 1930s. The often beautiful, art deco constructions were made as a cheap way to provide access to healthy activity during the summer.

In the following decades the lidos fell into decline and by the end of the 1980s nearly two-thirds were derelict or demolished. Britain had more than 300 lidos in the 1930s but only about a third were still open in 1990. Some survivors are still at risk of closing down today and communities are fighting to keep them open.

Now, the Department for Digital, Culture, Media and Sport has committed to creating a dedicated national fund to reinvigorate the UK’s lidos, after a campaign by MPs.

The move reflects the growing demand for outdoor swimming as summers in Britain heat up, and the fact that many communities do not have a place for cooling off. Better, the biggest leisure centre group in the country, which runs 10 outdoor swimming centres, said 2026 was by far its busiest year for outdoor swimming, with trips to its lidos between June and August rising by 28% to 692,471 visits.

Lisa Nandy, the culture secretary, said the investment would be “the first dedicated fund for lidos that this country has produced” and would form part of a wider public leisure facilities programme.

MPs who have campaigned to keep lidos open in their cities welcomed news of the fund.

Andrew Pakes, the Labour MP for Peterborough, who campaigned to keep the city’s lido open after the council’s budget plans suggested it could be mothballed, said the announcement was welcome news.

This year, Peterborough’s Grade II-listed art deco lido celebrated its 90th anniversary and its best ever season, which Pakes said echoed the experience at outdoor swimming facilities in many other cities. “This makes protecting and supporting our pools even more important,” he said.

Our morning email breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

He has been leading a group of MPs who have been calling for funding to build or reopen lidos in their local areas. “For places like Peterborough, the lido is far more than somewhere to swim. It is a much-loved part of our city’s heritage, bringing people together across generations,” he said.

The Carlisle MP, Julie Minns, who founded the campaign to save the city’s Grade II-listed Victorian and Turkish Baths, said: “These are places that bring communities together, support physical and mental wellbeing and form an important part of our shared heritage. In Carlisle, our Victorian and Turkish Baths are one of the city’s most distinctive historic buildings and have served generations of local residents.”

#152. Mushroom lovers in UK told to avoid grow kits owing to invasive species risk

Popular edible golden oyster classed as high-risk invasive species because of negative impact on woodlands

They have been viewed for years as the ideal Christmas gift for the culinary-minded, but experts have now warned that grow-your-own mushroom kits should be blacklisted from a spot under the tree.

An international group of mycologists, including experts from the Royal Horticultural Society, have said many of these kits, which allow people to easily grow mushrooms at home as a delicious addition to stir-fries and other dishes, contain invasive species.

The edible golden oyster mushroom, a favourite option, has been found growing in UK forests at breakneck speed. It has now been officially classed as a high-risk invasive species because of its negative impact on woodlands.

Scientists have said the golden oyster has established itself in the wild in at least two UK forests, with the most likely pathway of introduction being escape or release from grow kits.

Native to east Asia, the mushroom species has been found to be invasive on other continents as it has wind-dispersed spores that can travel thousands of miles, making it impossible to stop its spread. It arrived in the US in 2012 and has spread across 25 states and approximately 772,200 sq miles (2m sq km). Because it establishes itself so successfully, the species has been shown to as much as halve the beneficial wood-inhabiting native fungi.

Despite its invasive nature, it is still legal to sell the golden oyster commercially, with grow kits widely available online and through large retailers. The RHS no longer exhibits the golden oyster at events, nor does it sell the mushroom in its shops, instead spotlighting native species that can be grown at home including lion’s mane.

Dr Jassy Drakulic, a senior plant pathologist at the RHS, said: “The growing public interest in fungi is something to celebrate, but as they become more widely grown and sold, we must also recognise the biosecurity risks that can accompany them. The escape of the golden oyster mushroom in the UK highlights a significant gap in how invasive fungi are managed.

“Fungi are difficult to track in the wild, living mostly unseen until they fruit, so we need stronger policies to prevent introducing species that can permanently alter native ecosystems. Developing a dedicated strategy for invasive fungi is essential if we are to safeguard biodiversity for the future.”

Invasive species are one of the world’s leading causes of biodiversity loss. A famous example in Britain is the grey squirrel. It was imported from the US in the 1800s and spread a fatal disease known as squirrelpox to the native red squirrels. As a result, red squirrels have become extinct in many parts of Britain.

Aishwarya Veerabahu, a mycologist at the University of Wisconsin-Madison in the US, said: “Every golden oyster mushroom kit sold gives the golden oyster a chance to establish in nature and spread from there. Mushroom kits helped the golden oyster rapidly spread across the US and Canada; in less than a decade, it colonised vast areas of North America, and its full ecological impacts are still unfolding.

“Fungi are the foundation of forests and we’re beginning to understand how invasive species are impacting local biodiversity and disrupting their complex relationships. Golden oysters haven’t spread too far in the UK yet, so there’s a fighting chance of preventing it from becoming invasive. We’re asking people to hold off on buying mushroom kits to protect the UK’s fungal biodiversity and favourite native edibles.”

#151. Iran Attacks Bahrain for the Second Time in Days

The resumption of attacks after a two-month lull comes as peace talks between the U.S. and Iran appear to have stalled.

Vivian Nereim reported from Riyadh, Saudi Arabia, and Aaron Boxerman from Jerusalem.

Iran launched a drone attack against Bahrain on Sunday for the second time in days, as peace talks with the United States have faltered.

The Bahraini Defense Ministry said the Gulf Arab country’s armed forces had intercepted several “Iranian aerial threats” on Sunday before they struck their targets. Bahrain’s Interior Ministry also issued two alerts to residents on Sunday morning advising them to seek shelter.

There were no immediate reports of injuries or damage. Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps claimed that Iranian forces had targeted a radar system at a Bahraini air base.

Bahrain separately hosts a major U.S. naval base. The American military did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Sunday’s attack came two days after the U.S. military said it had shot down two drones fired by Iran toward Bahrain, without causing any injuries or damage.

The Revolutionary Guards said that Friday’s attack was retaliation for a previous American strike on a telecommunications tower in Iran.

The attacks were the first to target Bahrain in about two months. After the U.S. and Israel attacked Iran in February, Iran launched thousands of retaliatory missile and drone attacks at Gulf Arab countries that host American military installations. But those attacks had largely petered out as the two sides entered a stalemate.

#149. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#147. Brand statement from prison claiming his innocence ‘appalling’ and could breach rules, says No 10 – UK politics live

Brand’s wife Laura read out a message from him on their YouTube show

Peter Walker is a Guardian political correspondent.

Downing Street has described the broadcasting of a message from Russell Brand from prison claiming his innocence as “appalling”.

As the BBC reports, at the weekend Brand’s wife Laura read out a message from him on their Sunday Service show on YouTube in which the convicted rapist claimed he was “not guilty” of the offences for which he was jailed.

Asked about this at the daily lobby briefing, the PM’s spokesperson said:

double quotation markThis is clearly appalling and is being urgently investigated. We expect action to be taken if he is found to have broken the rules.

Prisoners are strictly banned from making statements that cause distress to victims and speaking to the media is only permitted in exceptional circumstances. Prisoners cannot get around these rules by having someone else publish their comments.

It is understood that, if a prisoner provides an offending statement of this kind to a relative knowing that it will be published, they are deemed to have broken the rules.

The Labour MP Stella Creasy, chair of the Labour Movement for Europe, asked if the UK could learn from Switzerland’s relationship with the EU.

Falconer did not reply in detail, but he said the government was “really ambitious for a, deeper, closer, stronger relationship with the European Union”.

Explaining the EU’s relationship with Switzerland, Šefčovič said:

double quotation markThe model we have in Switzerland is comprehensive. It means that it engages with the EU on most aspects of the internal market. And, in exchange for this access, Switzerland contributes financially to our cohesion policy, with many cohesion projects supported directly with the Swiss funds.

So Switzerland also accepts free movement of persons and participates in the Schengen area.

Where Switzerland does participate in the internal internal market, agreements rely on dynamic alignments and the uniform interpretation and application of the EU law which ensures, in turn, the level playing field for all participants in the internal market.

Šefčovič said it was up to the UK to decide what relationship with the EU it wanted.

Falconer and Šefčovič are now taking questions.

In response to a question from Mika Aaltola, a Finnish MEP, about whether Andy Burnham’s EU reset could lead to the UK rejoining at some point, Falconer replied:

double quotation markThe question you’re asking me is really, are we prepared to have a big, broad discussion in the UK about the future relationship between the UK and the European Union? And the answer is yes.

I won’t get ahead of the prime minister who’s going to give a speech this evening when he’ll say a little bit about this.

What he will often say to me is you cannot think about the UK’s path ahead over the next 10 years without thinking about our relationship with our home continent, and that is very much the spirit in which he comes to this. So I think you’ll hear more from us soon. And he is prepared to have a broad discussion about that.

Maroš Šefčovič, who as the European commissioner for trade is the EU’s lead negotiator with the UK on post-Brexit issues, told the UK-EU parliamentary partnership assembly that he hoped agreements would be reached on Friday in relation to three areas: sanitary and phytosanitary standards, emissions trading and youth experience.

He said the UK and the EU would also continue negotiating on UK participation in the EU’s internal electricity market. This would involve the UK making a contribution to the EU’s cohesion funds because this was a condition of particpation in the internal market, he said. He said these agreements would not be ready this week.

He said the youth experience scheme was particularly important because “young people are always the best ambassadors of our countries”.

double quotation markWe also want to build a closer, more resilient partnership between the EU and United Kingdom. And this partnership will put cooperation on economic security at its heart. That is why the summit will discuss how to cooperate more closely whether we can protect joint supply chains and under what conditions.

Hamish Falconer told the UK-EU parliamentary partnership assembly that the TCA (trade and cooperation agreement – the post-Brexit trade deal with the EU) has now been in force for five years.

The rest would provide an opportunity to” cut costs, support industries and create more opportunities for young people”, he said.

double quotation markAt the heart of what our teams have been doing are the detailed negotiations on the three new agreements we committed to last year – an agrifood trade deal, an agreement to link our emissions trading systems and a youth experience scheme.

These important deals will boost our respective economies. They will cut red tape, help our businesses grow and provide opportunities to young people denied since Brexit.

Over the course of this week, Maroš [Šefčovič] and I will be working hard to conclude those agreements and we hope to have good news for you come Friday summit.

The UK government will then bring forward primary legislation to implement those agreements.

We will also discuss a shared approach to protecting our economic security to ensure we do not unintentionally harm each other, and instead tackle shared economic challenges together.

That should be the floor, not the ceiling, of our ambition.

We want to go further to restore a higher level of growth and prosperity.

So beyond this summit, as the prime minister said, we will begin to look at options for the future and opening a national discussion on these options.

And that is obviously also a conversation we will want to have with our EU partners and friends about how Britain and the EU can work more closely together for our common security, prosperity and opportunity.

Hamish Falconer, the minister for EU relations, and his EU counterpart, Maroš Šefčovič, are both speaking and taking questions at a meeting of the UK-EU parliamentary partnership assembly in the House of Commons today. There is a live feed here. Falconer and Šefčovič have been heavily involved in the EU deals being announced on Friday, although it is not clear how much they will want to reveal this afternoon. Falconer started by saying he was appointed to his job while he was on honeymoon. He said he would not describe himself as married to Šefčovič, but they spend so much time together “it sometimes feels like the relationship is indeed very close,” he joked.

Downing Street has not ruled out the UK joining the European Union’s pilot scheme to send rejected asylum seekers to Rwanda, the Press Association reports. PA says:

double quotation markSeveral EU countries are reportedly set to announce a deal to send rejected asylum seekers to the east African country.

The government will watch the scheme “with interest” as part of efforts to find “innovative approaches” to kerb illegal migration, No 10 said.

The scandal-hit Rwanda deportation plan introduced under the Conservatives was axed by Keir Starmer immediately after he became prime minister.

It cost Britain £700m despite only four volunteers being sent to Kigali, then-home secretary Yvette Cooper said, as she branded the policy the “most shocking waste of taxpayer money I have ever seen”.

But the new EU pilot is said to differ from the Tory plan because it will only apply to asylum seekers who have exhausted all of their options, rather than all asylum seekers who arrive.

Asked about the scheme, the PM’s spokesperson said: “I’ve seen the reports on this. Obviously, it’s for the EU to make any announcements. I’m not going to get ahead of that.

“More broadly, though, you’ve got our position. The UK, like other European countries, is exploring innovative approaches to tackle illegal migration, including returns hubs, and will continue working with international partners to strengthen returns, remove those with no right to be here and restore control to our borders.”

Asked to rule out the UK joining the plan, the spokesperson said the UK would be watching the new initiative “with interest, learning from what went on in the past”.

After education questions, there is one urgent question in the Commons and two ministerial statements. They are, with rough timings:

3.30pm: A Home Office minister responds to an urgent question about planning permission being granted for the proposed camp for asylum seekers near Piddington.

Around 4.15pm: Ian Murray, the online safety minister, gives a statement on Friday’s Microsoft 365 outage.

Around 5.15pm: James Frith, a health minister, gives a statement on “digital capability for health and care”.

While Andy Burnham may be nervous about making the debate about the future relationship with the EU a simple one about whether or not to rejoin (see 10.59am), his opponents are happy to have this argument. Brexiters have already launched a campaign called Stay Out. It is led by Oliver Lewis, who was deputy to David Frost when Frost was Boris Johnson’s chief Brexit negotiator.

Lewis was on the Today programme this morning. Making the case for the UK to stay out, he focused more on the argument that this would be a breach of promises made to the public (including by Andy Burnham during the Makerfield byelection campaign) than on the notion that Brexit has been a great success. In this, he was broadly aligned with what Kemi Badenoch has been saying.

But Lewis also went further than the Conservative party (see 12.20pm), explicitly criticising the deal set to be announced by Burnham on Friday. He said:

double quotation markIt’s always a good idea to promote closer cooperation with all of our allies, and there’s always more that you can do to facilitate trade with friends and neighbours. However, what I disagree with is a settlement that will involve us paying hundreds of millions of pounds into the EU and involve the imposition of huge amounts of red tape on all sorts of businesses across the country … I think it’s a really bad step backwards.

Lewis said that at the general election Labour had “very firm red lines” about not joining the single market or the customs union. But the deal to be announced on Friday would be “an attempt to water this down”, he claimed. He said that was “deeply concerning”.

At the lobby briefing, the PM’s spokesperson also played down the prospect of the government introducing a staggered age of consent. (See 11.30am.) Asked about the proposal, the PM’s spokesperson said the government would “always look at what more can be done to protect young people” but that changes in this area of law were “complex”. He also referred reporters to what Louise Haigh, the first secretary of state, said on this over the weekend. She told the Sunday Times:

double quotation markIt clearly would be a massive step to change the age of consent, but it deserves proper consideration and scrutiny.

Peter Walker is a Guardian political correspondent.

Downing Street has described the broadcasting of a message from Russell Brand from prison claiming his innocence as “appalling”.

As the BBC reports, at the weekend Brand’s wife Laura read out a message from him on their Sunday Service show on YouTube in which the convicted rapist claimed he was “not guilty” of the offences for which he was jailed.

Asked about this at the daily lobby briefing, the PM’s spokesperson said:

double quotation markThis is clearly appalling and is being urgently investigated. We expect action to be taken if he is found to have broken the rules.

Prisoners are strictly banned from making statements that cause distress to victims and speaking to the media is only permitted in exceptional circumstances. Prisoners cannot get around these rules by having someone else publish their comments.

It is understood that, if a prisoner provides an offending statement of this kind to a relative knowing that it will be published, they are deemed to have broken the rules.

At her press conference, Kemi Badenoch faced various questions about Brexit, and Andy Burnham’s reset deal with the EU. She dismissed Burnham’s plan to debate a closer relationship as a “distraction”. Here are some of her arguments.

Badenoch said Burnham’s deal with the EU was just a “big nothingburger” until the details were published, and she repeatedly described his call for a debate on the future relationship with the EU as a “distraction”. She said:

double quotation markAndy Burnham is not the one who started this EU reset. Quite a lot of what Keir Starmer went and did at the last EU reset has not come to pass … People have forgotten, but all he managed to do was give away our fish. Everything else is still unsettled.

So the fact is Labour going to the EU and talking about a big reset moment is a big nothingburger until we see what’s come back and when it’s actually implemented.

Until then, it’s just a talking point and a distraction from the day to day issues we need to deal with now.

(Badenoch may be keen to dismiss the debate about the future relationship with the EU as a distraction because, given polls show most people now regard it as a failure, the Conservatives, who implemented Brexit, feel awkward being forced to defend it.)

Badenoch backed David Cameron’s argument that the problems facing Britain have nothing to do with Brexit. She said:

double quotation markAndy Burnham still has not learned the lessons of the past. He is still living in 2016.

The problems that this country faces today have nothing to do with being in or out of the EU. France was literally on fire last month. It’s in the EU. It has a small boat crisis, as do many other European countries. They are in the EU.

We need to stop obsessing about Europe and whether to be in or out, and actually get on with doing the things that we are elected to do – sort out the economy, fund our defence, cut welfare, get jobs for people, make sure businesses can create those jobs. We don’t need to be in the EU to do any of that.

This is again a distraction from a Prime Minister who doesn’t know what to do.

She said Burnham wanted the opposition to talk about Brexit “because if we’re talking about that, then we’re not talking about the government’s failures”. She added: “I’m not falling for that trick.”

She said she agreed with Boris Johnson when he told the Daily Telegraph that, in a second referendum, people would again vote to be out of the EU. Asked about his comments, she said:

double quotation markI wasn’t an MP when we had the referendum. And I remember everyone assuming that remain would win and all the polls and stats showed remain winning. And then something different happened.

So all of the people bringing out stats and polls saying people would vote to go back into the EU – I would ask them to go and look at the news from ten years ago and familiarise themselves with that.

She refused to say whether she would support or oppose the deal Burnham is set to agree on Friday. She said:

double quotation markI speak as a former trade secretary. [Burnham] laid out all of his negotiating tactics and all of his asks [before agreeing the EU deal]. You never, ever do that when you’re negotiating because the other party can squeeze out more concessions.

Take something like youth mobility, for instance. I support youth mobility depending on the conditions. I negotiated youth mobility for countries like Australia … The issue is not youth mobility. It’s what are the terms that are going with it?

It’s the detail of the deal that we will be looking at. So simply saying, ‘Yes, we’re going to reverse it, no, we’re not going to reverse it’, let’s actually see what he brings up.

What we will always do is what is right for the country. I’m not interested in, you know, attacking bits and pieces of a reset deal that I haven’t seen. The bottom line is that Andy Burnham is not the one who started this EU reset.

Following the Russell Brand conviction, and the revelations about David Walliams, there have been calls for the government to introduce a “staggered” age of consent, which would make it illegal for adults over a certain age to have sex with 16 and 17-year-olds. In an interview yesterday Pat McFadden, the work and pensions secretary, said the government would look at this idea.

In her press conference this morning, Kemi Badenoch said that, while she could see the case for this, she did not think it was very practical.

double quotation markWhen I was 16, I remember reading the Peter Stringfellow case, a 55-year-old man dating a 16-year-old, and I thought it was the most disgusting thing ever.

I do think ... that there’s some common sense there in terms of a staggered age of consent. My issue is with how we will enforce it.

Badenoch also suggested it would be odd for the government to be raising the age of consent in some circumstances while also lowering the voting age to 16. She went on:

double quotation markPoliticians need to focus on building capacity, building prison spaces, renting spaces abroad, which is one of our policies, rather than creating more laws that cannot be enforced …

[The government is] all over the place. Let’s have an age of maturity and stick to it, and let’s have some coherence in policy.

When Andy Burnham told the Labour conference that he wanted to start a debate on a new relationship with the EU, because Brexit had done more harm than good, he raised the possibility that this could lead to Labour proposing to rejoin at some point after the next election.

But some ministers and Labour MPs are deeply unhappy about that prospect, believing that there would have to be a referendum and that this would be deeply divisive, and there are some signs that Burnham (who is on record as saying he would like the UK to rejoin in his lifetime) is starting to scale back his ambitions.

In his speech to the Welsh Labour conference on Saturday, Burnham repeated the argument he made in September about Brexit having done more harm than good. But he focused on making Brexit better, not overturning it. At one point in the speech he said:

double quotation markI don’t believe Brexit and the deal that they got has been good enough for Britain. I think we’ve lost more than we’ve gained over the last 10 years.

And actually, I think the Conservatives gave Britain a harsher and harder Brexit than people thought they were getting when they voted in 2016.

double quotation markPeople’s jobs depend on working together and collaborating.

And that’s our approach, and it will always be our approach. People working together in unions, trade unions, but also unions of nations, and that’s what makes us who we are.

And yes, working with the European Union, as I said, but at the same time, respecting the red lines in our manifesto and respecting the referendum, the referendum result.

The reference to “respecting the referendum result” could have just meant not rejoining before the next election. But given that this was a manifesto red line, and that he had already spoken about respecting that, he seemed to imply something more.

And today Burnham has announced three tests for his Brexit reset. (See 9.15am.) Although these are only tests for the deal he is announcing on Friday, not for the Brexit policy he will announce at the next election, the focus on improving the Brexit deal and bringing it “closer to what people thought they were voting for in 2016” might be a sign of where his thinking is heading.

In interviews this morning, Ed Miliband, the foreign secretary, also focused on the government wanting to make Brexit work better for the economy. He told Sky News:

double quotation markLook, here’s my starting point. My constituents, people across the country, voted for a range of reasons for Brexit, but one of them was one of the biggest, certainly for my constituents, was to make our economy better, positive economic change. We haven’t had that. The economy has got worse. And nobody really thinks this Brexit deal is a good deal.

On ITV’s Good Morning Britain, Ed Balls did not ask Miliband about Russell Brand but he did ask him repeatedly if he wanted the UK to rejoin the EU. (See 9.15am.) Miliband and Balls worked very closely together for many years and Balls was clearly assuming that the honest answer would be “Yes”. But Miliband said that he would not give his own personal view because he was abiding by collective cabinet responsibility. Balls said that Andy Burnham has been happy to say he wants the UK to rejoin in his lifetime, while John Healey (who is 10 years older) has said that he does not expect that in his lifetime. Balls asked Miliband what he expected to happen in his lifetime. Miliband still refused to answer. Balls urged him to be braver and less restrained – “Let your hair down, have a good time” - but Miliband still would not engage with the question.

The Tories have pledged to halt “screen-based” exams and cut coursework in a bid to limit the use of AI by schoolchildren if the party returns to power, the Press Association reports. PA says:

double quotation markNew guidelines for teachers would seek to ensure that laptops and tablets are not the default in classrooms, the party said.

Shadow education secretary Laura Trott said Britain had “sleepwalked into a situation where nearly a million children spend almost every lesson” on a device despite health professionals warning of the dangers of excessive screen time.

Under Conservative proposals, the amount of coursework in school assessment will be “minimised” to make sure the next generation of workers are “AI-resilient”, the party said.

“Screen-based” exams would also be stopped, with screen time for secondary school pupils limited to a maximum of two hours a day, including during homework.

In primary schools, devices would only be used at a “minimum” and where relevant in the curriculum, such as for computing, the party has said.

Kemi Badenoch and Trott will be speaking about this at their press conference starting shortly.

Ed Miliband has said he will not resign from the cabinet if the government approves the Rosebank oilfield, despite his previous condemnation of the scheme.

In an interview on the Today programme, it was put to Miliband that it would be logical for him to resign given that he is on record as saying in 2023 that Rosebank would be “climate vandalism” and a colossal waste of taxpayers’ money.

Asked if he would resign if Labour approves the project, Miliband replied: “No. Definitely not.”

double quotation markI obey collective responsibility. Miatta Fahnbulleh [the energy secretary] will make the decision on this. I know it’s a quasi-judicial decision. So she looks at all the evidence, and then reaches a conclusion. I’m not going to second-guess [her].

Miliband said he supported the decision she made to allow the Jackdaw gasfield to go ahead.

Pressed again on Rosebank, he would not say whether he had changed his mind since he condemned the proposal in 2023. But he said: “I’m a member of the government and I will abide by collective responsibility on this.”

As Ben Quinn reports, around 75 Labour MPs have already signed a private letter saying they are opposed to Rosebank going ahead and it is thought that number could rise to more than 100.

Good morning. There are two issues that will dominate the political news this week – the EU reset, and the Alan Milburn report on youth unemployment – and this morning there are new lines on both of these issues.

Andy Burnham has set out “three tests” for the deal that he is due to agree with the EU at a summit on Friday. Tonight he is giving the traditional prime ministerial speech to the lord mayor’s banquet in London, which by convention focuses on foreign policy, and, according to the overnight briefing from Downing Street, he will announce three tests. No 10 says:

double quotation markAhead of this week’s UK-EU Summit, the prime minister will set out three tests for any agreement:

-First, it must be consistent with the manifesto and the government’s red lines.

-Second, it must improve the Brexit deal and bring it closer to what people thought they were voting for in 2016, rather than the hard Brexit that no one ever seriously argued for in the referendum.

-Third, it must pass the Makerfield test by delivering real, tangible improvements in people’s everyday lives, particularly in communities that have felt neglected for too long.

That means cutting costs for businesses, easing pressure on prices for consumers and creating more opportunities for young people.

If the agreement delivers against these tests, the prime minister will sign it, demonstrating a new approach based on cooperation rather than confrontation.

There is no real doubt about whether the Friday agreement – which will largely focus on food export rules, but which will also cover energy policy (emissions trading rules) and a youth mobility scheme – will pass these tests. Much of it was already agreed when Keir Starmer was PM, and it is more or less a done deal. But Burnham’s “three tests” are interesting because they provide some clues as to how he will approach his plan to have a wider debate about a closer relationship with the EU.

In his speech, Burnham will also restate his view that Brexit has not worked – using a new simile. He will say:

double quotation markIt’s like the Emperor’s new clothes – where no one is prepared to say the obvious truth. Well, I say it as I see it. And to be frank, the Emperor has nothing on. The Brexit we were given has not worked. We have lost more than we gained.

Charlie Moloney has more on the Burnham speech here.

Alan Milburn is expected to say that employer national insurance for workers under the age of 25 should be cut in his report published tomorrow, the BBC is reporting. Many of Milburn’s recommendations have already been made public, but this is new, and it is likely to be welcomed by Kemi Badenoch, who is holding a press conference later, because the Tories have repeatedly been saying that Rachel Reeves’s decision to raise employer national insurance has been bad for jobs.

As Alexandra Topping reports, in an article for the Guardian, Burnham has pledged in a Guardian article to intervene as early as childhood to tackle youth unemployment.

Ed Miliband, the foreign secretary, has been giving interviews this morning. It is relatively unusual for Miliband to be put up for a full interview round and, on Europe and the Milburn report, his remarks were not especially newsworthy. But, on the Today programme, Nick Robinson did get a good line out of him when he asked him about Russell Brand, and Miliband’s decision to do a high-profile interview with Brand during the 2015 election campaign – when Brand had not yet been accused of rape, but when he was already widely viewed as disreputable.

Last week Ed Balls, who was shadow chancellor at the time, twisted the knife by telling his podcast that he had warned Miliband not to go ahead with the “ridiculous” interview. Balls is now a presenter on Good Morning Britain and this morning he was interviewing his old boss and colleague. Sadly, though, he did not ask about Brand.

On the Today programme, when Robinson asked about Balls’s comments last week and put it to Miliband that in 2015 he should have listened to his shadow chancellor, Miliband replied:

double quotation markLet me first say, Russell Brand’s crimes are heinous and appalling, and all of my sympathies are with the victims of Russell Brand, who have shown incredible bravery.

On the question of that interview, I’ve said … that if I had known of these allegations against Russell Brand, the allegations which he’s now being convicted [of], I would never have done that interview, and I deeply regret it.

Miliband claimed that he did not know at the time about Balls’s reservations about the interview going ahead, but he said Balls was “obviously right” about Brand being weird and untrustworthy.

But Miliband went on to defend doing the interview. He said:

double quotation markLet me just say one thing, because I do want to one thing you said.

Russell Brand was going around telling people not to vote in the general election.

I was not endorsing Russell Brand. I was taking him on and saying he was completely wrong in saying that people shouldn’t vote, that democracy didn’t matter and all those things. But as I say, I deeply regret doing that interview.

10am: Kemi Badenoch and Laura Trott, the shadow education secretary, hold a press conference.

11.30am: Downing Street holds a lobby briefing.

2.30pm: Lucy Powell, the education secretary, takes questions in the Commons.

#145. Dogecoin jumps after Elon Musk replaces Twitter bird with Shiba Inu

Twitter’s traditional bird icon was booted and replaced with an image of a Shiba Inu, an apparent nod to dogecoin, the joke cryptocurrency that CEO Elon Musk is being sued over.

Musk addressed the change Monday afternoon, tweeting, “as promised” above an image of a year-old conversation in which another user suggested that Musk “just buy Twitter” and “change the bird logo to a doge.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

The doge logo appeared on the site two days after Musk asked a judge to throw out a $258 billion racketeering lawsuit accusing him of running a pyramid scheme to support the dogecoin, according to Reuters.

Lawyers for Musk and Tesla called the lawsuit by dogecoin investors a “fanciful work of fiction” over Musk’s “innocuous and often silly tweets.”

It wasn’t clear whether the logo change was permanent. Musk has been known to use Twitter to troll both his fans and his critics.

The price of dogecoin, which is typically volatile, was up more than 20% over the past 24 hours, to about 9 cents. It was trading just under 8 cents Monday morning.

Dogecoin was created December 6, 2013, by a pair of software engineers — as a joke. The name is a nod to the “doge” meme that became popular a decade ago. Its Shiba Inu mascot mimicks that meme: a dog surrounded by a bunch of Comic Sans text in broken English.

#143. Saudi-Backed Forces Retreat in Yemen, Leaving Key Strait in Houthi Control

It’s a setback for the Yemeni government and its ally Saudi Arabia, as analysts said the momentum in country’s civil war was tilting toward the Iran-backed Houthis.

Forces backed by Saudi Arabia in Yemen said on Sunday that they were withdrawing from the country’s Red Sea coast, where they have been fighting for weeks to retake strategic territory from the Iran-aligned Houthi militia.

A group of fighters called the Southern Armed Forces, who are allied with the government of Yemen, said in a statement that they were “redeploying” to protect the interim capital, Aden, citing recent battlefield developments and “threats targeting” the city.

Aden has been the seat of the internationally recognized government for more than a decade, since the Houthis seized control of Sanaa, the Yemeni capital.

The retreat is a blow for the Yemeni government and Saudi Arabia as the balance of power in Yemen continues to tilt toward the Houthis, analysts said.

“The Houthis are very determined to go to Aden,” said Ahmed Nagi, a senior analyst for Yemen at the International Crisis Group. “They don’t want to see any area controlled by the government or the Saudi side, so they want to take over everything at this moment.”

#142. Two charged after man was run over by van after flags incident in Midlands

Pair due to appear in court in December in relation to incident that took place in May in south-west Birmingham

Two men have been charged following an incident in Birmingham, where a man was run over by a van after flags were removed from lamp-posts.

A man, in his 30s, was taken to hospital with leg injuries after the incident on Mayfield Road in the suburb of Stirchley, south-west Birmingham, on 14 May.

Mark Keating, 49, from Tamworth, has been charged with four counts of assault by beating and will appear before magistrates in Birmingham on 9 December, West Midlands police said on Monday.

Ryan Bridge, 45, from Bromsgrove, is charged with two counts of using threatening/abusive words/behaviour likely to cause harassment, alarm or distress. He will appear before the same court on 10 December.

Another man, 53, from Sutton Coldfield remains on bail after being arrested on suspicion of wounding in May.

In a statement, West Midlands police said the van was recovered nearby, shortly after the incident.

“Officers have been carrying out a range of thorough enquiries to establish the full circumstances around what happened,” the statement read. “We took reports of four assaults relating to the same incident, but we understand that no one suffered serious injuries.”
