#117. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#115. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

Scan the QR code to download the CNN app from the Apple Store.

#113. Eisenkot condemns Netanyahu’s ‘racist’ campaign against Israel’s Arab citizens

Prime ministerial hopeful calls Arab minority ‘an integral part of the Israeli story’ as he seeks to form government

Israeli prime ministerial hopeful Gadi Eisenkot condemned a “racist and aggressive campaign of delegitimisation” against the country’s Arab citizens, after the caretaker prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, accused Arab parties of electoral fraud without evidence.

Netanyahu has repeatedly targeted Arab parties and Palestinian citizens of Israel after his party received a disappointing electoral showing, accusing them of manipulating the election and being linked to Hamas. Israeli electoral authorities have rejected these claims as not being backed by evidence.

Arab citizens of Israel, the vast majority of them of Palestinian descent, comprise more than 20% of the country’s population.

Shin Bet, Israel’s internal security branch, sought to question four Arab lawmakers, including the Arab-majority Ra’am party head, Mansour Abbas, leading to accusations that the security agency had become politicised.

“Arab citizens of Israel are an integral part of the Israeli story. The attempt to marginalise them and vilify them collectively is a profound violation of the principles of statesmanship,” Eisenkot said in a statement on Friday.

Eisenkot’s statement was a rare instance of a prominent Jewish political leader taking a strong stand in defence of Arab Israelis.

The statement also reflected political realities in Israel, as Arab parties found themselves kingmakers after the latest election. The two Arab-majority parties, Ra’am and the Joint List, secured 19 seats in the October election.

They have the potential to help Eisenkot form a governing coalition and become Israel’s next prime minister. If Eisenkot cannot form a coalition within 28 days, with a possible 14-day extension, the mandate to form a government would pass to another lawmaker, potentially Netanyahu.

Eisenkot has to balance the competing interests of several parties if he hopes to form a coalition. Rightwing elements of the opposition have pledged to drop their support of the prospective PM if he cooperates with Arab parties.

His statement on Friday portrayed a more statesman-like approach to ruling than his more rightwing, demagogic opponents who have used Arab parties and citizens as political scapegoats over the last three years.

“[Arab citizens] have been subjected to a racist and aggressive campaign of delegitimisation, fuelled by the outgoing prime minister and those around him for political purposes,” Eisenkot said in his statement.

“The prime minister of Israel is the prime minister of all Israeli citizens, including those who did not vote for him,” he added.

Netanyahu and his allies have repeatedly criticised leaders of Arab parties for refusing to describe Hamas as a “terror group” after its unprecedented 7 October 2023 attack on Israel.

All leaders of Arab parties have condemned the attack, in which Arab Israelis – descendants of Palestinians who remained in what became Israel in 1948 – were also among those killed.

In a speech, Netanyahu falsely claimed that the two Arab lists sought to establish either an “Islamic state” or a “nationalist Palestinian state”.

During the same ceremony, the national security minister, Itamar Ben Gvir, heckled Joint List’s leader, Yousef Jabareen, during his oath of allegiance, accusing him of having “pledged allegiance to Hamas”.

“It is entirely legitimate to disagree with representatives of the Arab parties, even to strongly disapprove of some of them. But it is neither legitimate nor acceptable to malign an entire community, and I will not allow it,” Eisenkot said.

President Isaac Herzog tasked Eisenkot with forming a new government on Sunday after consultations in which a majority of political parties, including the two Arab lists, recommended him for the role.

Israeli media has reported that Eisenkot has also been reaching out to the country’s ultra-Orthodox parties for a potential alliance. The issue of the ultra-Orthodox not serving in the Israeli military was one of the hot button issues during the October election, with Eisenkot previously saying they should also be included in the country’s mandatory draft.

#112. American Roosters Fight in the Philippines Despite U.S. Crackdown

It’s illegal in the United States to transport game fowl for fighting. But investigators say breeders are sending birds to the Philippines, where cockfighting is legal and lucrative.

It’s illegal in the United States to transport game fowl for fighting. But investigators say breeders are sending birds to the Philippines, where cockfighting is legal and lucrative.

Men preparing roosters for a cockfight at an arena in Talisay, Philippines, in July.Credit...Ezra Acayan for The New York Times

Francesca Regalado reported from Bacolod City in the Philippines.

Jerry Jalandoni navigated hundreds of chickens running loose on his farm in the Philippines before stopping at a row of tall cages that held his latest acquisitions: a dozen cockfighting roosters from the United States.

On top of one cage was a cardboard box, poked with holes that had allowed a hen to breathe during her solitary journey from Texas to Bacolod City. “She’s been through a lot,” Mr. Jalandoni said.

The hen arrived alone, he said, because her mate had been held back by exporters wary of attracting the attention of animal rights groups. Such birds come from game fowl breeds developed in the United States that are renowned internationally for their sturdiness and aggression — qualities valued in cockfighting, a legal and highly profitable industry in the Philippines.

Those birds were not supposed to be on Mr. Jalandoni’s farm, according to U.S. federal laws. But they are part of a trade in which breeders in the United States send fighting roosters to the Philippines.

While the trade has long been an open secret, the channels through which American fighting birds arrive in Asia are drawing fresh scrutiny from U.S. officials.

Cockfighting — the practice of tying metal blades, or gaffs, to the legs of roosters that fight each other to the death — has been illegal in the United States since 2008, when Louisiana became the last state to outlaw it. Transporting animals across state lines or out of the country for the purpose of fighting was banned in 1976.

#111. Saudi Arabia, Qatar, Jordan, Egypt condemn Iranian attack on Bahrain - as it happened

Iran has targeted airbase in Bahrain in latest resumption of strikes since september

The state-run Bahrain news agency is also carrying the statements of each country in the region responding to Iran’s actions targeting their Gulf neighbour this morning.

Jordan, Egypt, Saudi Arabia, and Qatar have all strongly condemned the attack and expressed solidarity with Bahrain.

The statements are very similar, I’ve pulled out a few below:

Saudi Arabia’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs issued a statement published by the Saudi Press Agency:

double quotation markThe Kingdom affirms that the Iranian attacks constitute a violation of international law and the principles of good neighborliness. The Kingdom stresses the importance of an immediate halt to the blatant Iranian attacks on the countries of the region, and of taking serious steps towards achieving security and stability for the countries of the region and their peoples.

Qatar’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs said in a statement on the Qatar news agency (QNA):

double quotation markThe continuation of these attacks represents a dangerous escalation that will complicate efforts to contain the tension and undermine political and diplomatic endeavors aimed at achieving security and stability in the region.

I’m going to wrap up coverage for now. Here’s a summary of what you need to know as Iran steps up strikes against US allies in the Gulf again.

Iran launched a six-drone attack on a US airbase in Bahrain early on Sunday, prompting fears of a return to full-blown fighting after a two-month lull in strikes

Bahrain said it had intercepted “several aerial attacks” and destroyed the drones before they reached their targets, and officials did not immediately disclose any damage or injuries

However the tiny Gulf kingdom, which hosts a major US naval command, was forced to send out public warnings and sound missile warning sirens ahead of the incoming strikes

Residents were rattled by the attacks starting up again; the second since Friday. “I was hoping this period was behind us and wouldn’t happen again”

Iran said it had targeted a radar facility at the Al-Rifaa helicopter base. The US is yet to respond, but said there had been no damage from the first attempted strike on Friday.

Iran said its attack was justified retaliation for recent US aggression. The US has been carrying out low-level strikes on Iranian naval sites along the country’s south coast and also on Qeshm Island, in the Strait of Hormuz, to support oil vessels traversing the dangerous waterway

Iran says ships traversing the chokepoint without permission are hitting sea mines and sinking, and are legitimate targets to be fired upon.

On a different front, in civil war-torn Yemen, the Iran-backed Houthis are putting pressure on the government’s control around Aden, the interim capital.

The Saudi-backed Yemeni government forces say they’ve been forced to withdraw from their Bab el-Mandeb counter-offensive to focus on securing Aden.

Iran’s Revolutionary Guards have claimed the sinking of an oil supertanker which hit a mine in the Strait of Hormuz. There’s no independent verification of this yet.

The US military had claimed recently that it had cleared all mines from the main routes through the waterway.

But the IRGC said Sunday that two ships had hit its mines while travelling through the strait, “resulting in massive explosions and fire”.

It said the super tanker had been carrying more than two million barrels of oil when it sunk in the Gulf. Iran has made several announcements in recent weeks about ships hitting mines.

Tehran has heavily restricted shipping traffic through the strait since the start of the war, demanding vessels seek permission and travel via designated pathways. It has fired on ships trying to circumvent Iranian detection.

The US has been supporting ships going through the strait. In the past 24 hours, Centcomm posted on X a picture of F-16 fighter jets and said that more than 1.7 billions of crude oil “have been shipped through the Strait of Hormuz by Gulf partners with U.S. protection.”

As I mentioned earlier, Iran’s Revolutionary Guards have said its strikes this morning were retaliation for US aggression.

Foreign Ministry spokesman Esmaeil Baghaei said at a press conference in Tehran on Sunday that Iran would continue to respond to any military aggression by the US or other states.

He was asked whether the strike on Tehran, the second this weekend, showed a change in Iranian direction as negotiations with the US have stalled.

Mr. Baghaei said that Iran was “not waiting for anyone” and was focused on its security.

The US has launched strikes on Iranian naval bases in the Strait of Hormuz in recent weeks, as well as along Iran’s southern coast. This has been seen as efforts to try and degrade Iran’s striking capability as well as offering cover for commerical ships still venturing through and out of Hormuz.

President Trump has refrained from major military escalation, describing those sporadic strikes as minor. However, American media have reported that both the US and Israel have discussed plans for a fuller resumption of strikes.

Here’s a map showing Bahrain, the smallest Gulf state, in relation to Iran and the rest of its Gulf neighbours. Tehran sent a flurry of drones across the Gulf in the early hours of Sunday morning to target a US air base south of Manama.

Bahrain is home to a major US military base, the NSA Bahrain (or Naval Support Activity Bahrain), which are headquarters for the US Fifth Fleet and US Naval Forces Central Command, and also acts as key hub of support for all operations in the region.

However the base was struck heavily during Iran’s retaliatory strikes at the start of the war, and it’s unclear whether it’s fully recovered.

American media have reported major damage to its headquarters, barracks and radar facilities.

Iran’s drones this morning were aimed at a different target- the US air base in Al-Rifaa, south of the capital.

The US has formal bases in Kuwait, Bahrain, and Qatar, and access arrangements to military facilities in the United Arab Emirates, Oman, and Saudi Arabia.

In light of Iran’s latest strike attempts on US allies in the Gulf, it’s useful to look at what the US commander-in-chief has been saying in recent days, as my colleague Patrick Wintour reports. Donald Trump has said in a series of media interviews since Thursday that he will attack Iran only if necessary, claiming that Gulf countries, such as Saudi Arabia, have urged him to hold back from planned large-scale military strikes.

Trump said he had asked the vice-president, JD Vance, to end his interest in negotiating a new deal with Iran and instead focus on corruption and fraud in Democrat-run cities and states.

Vance had been seen by the Iranians as the most trustworthy figure close to Trump, but they had rejected his request for a personal meeting with their negotiators to discuss the terms of a peace deal.

Vance has been at the forefront of Republicans saying the war was the cause of the widespread Republican losses in the midterms. Trump said he had been disappointed in the American public because higher diesel prices were a small price to pay for preventing Iran from acquiring a nuclear weapon.

I reported earlier that Yemeni government forces are falling back to Aden to try and secure their interim capital which is coming under pressure from Houthi forces.

A Houthi attack on a checkpoint near Aden has killed five people and injured eight others near Aden, AFP is reporting.

The missile targeted a checkpoint in Khor Omeira, Lahj province, AFP says, citing three unnamed military sources and a government source.

The state-run Bahrain news agency is also carrying the statements of each country in the region responding to Iran’s actions targeting their Gulf neighbour this morning.

Jordan, Egypt, Saudi Arabia, and Qatar have all strongly condemned the attack and expressed solidarity with Bahrain.

The statements are very similar, I’ve pulled out a few below:

double quotation markThe Kingdom affirms that the Iranian attacks constitute a violation of international law and the principles of good neighborliness. The Kingdom stresses the importance of an immediate halt to the blatant Iranian attacks on the countries of the region, and of taking serious steps towards achieving security and stability for the countries of the region and their peoples.

Qatar’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs said in a statement on the Qatar news agency (QNA):

double quotation markThe continuation of these attacks represents a dangerous escalation that will complicate efforts to contain the tension and undermine political and diplomatic endeavors aimed at achieving security and stability in the region.

Below is the full statement from Bahrain’s foreign ministry on the Iranian drone strikes today, which were intercepted and shot down. This has been published in Bahrain’s various state-controlled media outlets.

double quotation markThe Ministry of Foreign Affairs expressed the Kingdom of Bahrain’s strongest condemnation of the continued Iranian aggression against its territory, including the attack launched today, Sunday, November 15, 2026, by drones that were intercepted and destroyed by air defense systems in the Kingdom’s airspace.

This comes two days after a similar attack at dawn on Friday, November 13, 2026, by two drones that were also intercepted. The Iranian Revolutionary Guard explicitly claimed responsibility for these attacks. The Ministry praised the efficiency, vigilance, and capability of the Bahrain Defense Force in confronting today’s attack and its complete readiness to deter any aggression. The Ministry considered these attacks a resumption of Iran’s heinous aggression against the Kingdom and countries in the region, disregarding the provisions of international law and the principles of the UN Charter.

The Kingdom views them as a blatant attack on its sovereignty and territorial integrity, a flagrant violation of Security Council Resolution 2817, which condemned Iranian attacks on the Kingdom of Bahrain and neighboring countries and demanded their immediate cessation, a targeting of innocent citizens and residents, a sabotage of de-escalation efforts, and a serious threat to the region’s security and stability. The Ministry affirmed the Kingdom of Bahrain’s categorical rejection of the flimsy pretexts and baseless claims put forward by the Iranian Revolutionary Guard, emphasizing that no pretext justifies aggression against the sovereignty and territorial integrity of states.

The Ministry of Foreign Affairs added that the Kingdom of Bahrain affirms that its security is a red line, that its sovereignty is non-negotiable, and that it will remain, as it has always been, a proponent of good neighborliness, respect for the sovereignty of states, and non-interference in their affairs, and is committed to the security and stability of the Arabian Gulf.

Iranian media are reporting that their foreign minister Abbas Araqchi will be in Oman on Tuesday for talks with his Omani counterpart.

Oman, along with Qatar, has been one of the key negotiators in ceasefire and peace talks between Tehran and Washington.

As both sides have settled into a strategy of exerting continued economic pain on the other, we’re yet to see any movement on a deal though.

The US is maintaining a highly expensive military operation in the Gulf, with a naval blockade that aims to stop Iranian oil ships leaving the Persian Gulf, thereby hurting the Iranian economy, and through a missile and drone operation targeting Iran’s southern coastline aimed at protecting and helping commercial ships get out. Oil flows are said to be back near their prewar level.

But Iran hasn’t strayed from its demands, and the conditions it wants to end the war: a measure of control over Hormuz, removal of the blockade and economic sanctions, having its assets unfrozen, and reparations.

A lightning-strike offensive back in September saw the Houthis sweep almost the entire Red Sea coast and almost seize control of the critical Red Sea strait, which has become another chokepoint in the flow of oil and and energy supplies from Gulf producers.

Younis had been one of the senior Hamas figures who helped plan and oversee the October 7 attacks. He had only been the Rafah brigade leader role for two months, after Israel killed his predecessor, Nael Abu Obeid.

In their statement announcing his death, Hamas said Younis was “one of the most important commanders involved in planning and overseeing the 7 October attack”.

Israel is still striking Gaza on a near daily basis despite the the US-brokered ceasefire having come into action since October last year. More than 1,620 people have been killed in Gaza since the ceasefire, and more than 74,650 people have been killed in Gaza since the war began.

Here’s a recap of what the various sides have said this morning regarding the latest Iranian strikes targeting a US air base in Bahrain.

double quotation markThe US air defence radar system at the Al-Riffa helicopter base in Bahrain was targeted by six drones belonging to the IRGC Aerospace Force.

I’m yet to see a response from the US, but can expect Centcomm (which oversees all American military operations in the Middle East) to comment at some stage, like it did for Friday’s attack. Those strikes also targeted a radar site.

After two months of calm, the explosions have started again for Bahrain citizens.

Locals reported hearing blasts about 4am local time, and then a second round at 6.30am.

double quotation markIt’s exactly what I was hoping wouldn’t happen: Iranian attacks starting again, with all the worry and uncertainty that go with them”, Hayat Ibrahim, 65, told AFP news agency.

Hello and welcome to our live coverage of the fighting in the Middle East.

Iran has targeted an airbase in Bahrain in the latest resumption of strikes since September, prompting public warnings and sirens to ring out in the Gulf kingdom this morning.

#109. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#108. Iran launches drone attacks against US military base in Bahrain

Strikes cast further doubt on peace talks between Tehran and Washington with increasing risk of escalation

Iran has launched drone attacks against a US military base in Bahrain, casting further doubt on peace talks between Tehran and Washington and raising the spectre of escalation.

Blasts sounded over the Gulf nation on Sunday in the second Iranian drone attack in three days in retaliation for US strikes in Iran.

Meanwhile, in Yemen, Iran’s Houthi allies advanced against pro-government forces in the south, opening a potential route to Aden. Iran also claimed that a mine sank an oil supertanker in the strait of Hormuz.

The developments deepened gloom about the chances of any diplomatic breakthrough in the region.

Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps said they launched six drones targeting a US air defence radar at the Al-Rifaa helicopter base in retaliation for “hostile US actions”. Authorities in Bahrain said air defences intercepted the drones before they reached their targets.

At a press conference in Tehran the foreign ministry spokesperson, Esmaeil Baghaei, said Iran would continue to respond to any military aggression by the US or other states. Asked if the latest strikes indicated a change in Iranian direction as negotiations with the US have stalled, he said Iran was “not waiting for anyone” and was focused on its security.

US forces have conducted a series of strikes against Iranian targets in recent weeks, including Qeshm Island in the strait of Hormuz.

Donald Trump last week called them minor strikes and said Saudi Arabia and other Gulf states had cautioned him against large-scale combat operations.

However, the president also said Washington was no longer seeking a deal with Iran and that he had instructed the vice-president, JD Vance, to switch his focus to alleged corruption and fraud in Democrat-run cities and states.

Vance – who blamed the Republicans’ loss of Congress in midterm elections on the Iran war – had requested a personal meeting with Iranian negotiators to discuss peace terms.

A conflict that began with joint US and Israeli attacks against Iran in February remains at an impasse, with the US maintaining a naval blockade and Iran its chokehold over the strait of Hormuz.

In a separate development, Iran’s Revolutionary Guards claimed that two ships hit mines in the strait of Hormuz and that one, an oil supertanker with 2m barrels of oil, sank. The ships suffered “massive explosions and fire”, said the statement.

There was no immediate independent verification. The US military recently claimed it had cleared all mines from main routes through the waterway.

The strikes against Bahrain suggested Iran has decided on a tit-for-tat response to US attacks.

Bahrain hosts significant US bases, including a forward base that was home to the US Fifth Fleet and US Naval Forces Central Command, operating as a central support hub for military operations in the Middle East.

Tehran repeatedly targeted the kingdom until September, when there was a lull in attacks. That ended on Friday when Iran launched drones against a US radar facility. US Central Command said it shot down two drones without any injury or damage.

The attacks on Sunday prompted sirens and alerts from Bahrain’s authorities urging residents to seek shelter.

“It’s exactly what I was hoping wouldn’t happen: Iranian attacks starting again, with all the worry and uncertainty that go with them,” resident Hayat Ibrahim, 65, told Agence France Presse.

Bahrain’s foreign ministry called the resumption of attacks “heinous aggression” and a flagrant violation of international law.

The kingdom rejected Tehran’s “flimsy pretexts and baseless claims” to target its territory, it said in a statement. “The kingdom also rejects the transformation of its territory into an arena for settling scores and holds Iran fully responsible for these attacks and their consequences.”

Jordan, Egypt, Saudi Arabia, and Qatar condemned the attacks and expressed solidarity with Bahrain.

In Yemen, Saudi Arabia’s campaign against the Iranian-aligned Houthi rebels reeled when forces fighting on behalf of the Yemeni government retreated from the Bab el-Mandeb strait to defend ⁠Aden on the southern ​coast.

“The Southern Armed Forces announce the implementation of ‌a redeployment operation for its forces from Dhubab Airport and the Bab el-Mandeb area, west of Taiz Governorate,” the main military force fighting for Yemen’s government in the south said in a statement.

The admission signalled the apparent failure of a six-week government counteroffensive to drive the Houthis from the mouth of the Red Sea to secure a key oil route.

A Houthi missile strike near Aden killed people and wounded 12 near Aden, AFP reported, citing military and government sources.

Additional reporting by Agence France Presse and Associated Press

#106. In Banff, a Grizzly Bear Known as ‘the Boss’ Rules the National Park

Bear 122 has survived a train, cars and rival bears. Some fear the biggest threat to his life now is his own fame.

“The Boss,” the near mythical grizzly bear king of Banff National Park.Credit...All in the Wild Photography

Reporting from Banff National Park, Canmore and Dead Man’s Flats, Alberta.

To the scientists and wardens of Banff National Park he is grizzly bear 122. But to everyone who lives in or near Canada’s oldest national park, and many of its 4.5 million annual visitors, he is the Boss.

He has been struck by a freight train without apparent ill effect. He has shrugged off being hit by cars. Scientists have confirmed that he killed and snacked on a black bear at least once, and he is believed to have fathered many, if not most, of the bears in his 2,000-square-kilometer territory.

Most of all, the Boss has become Canada’s 700-pound celebrity bear king. When he strolls along the Trans-Canada Highway looking for dandelions, “bear jams” swiftly follow. His battles with a rival bear known as Split Lip are legendary. (He caused the split lip.) His annual awakening from hibernation, usually in March, is reported by news outlets across Alberta.

Galleries in Banff and neighboring Canmore ship giant photos of him worldwide. His image appears on fridge magnets, T-shirts and tote bags. A local restaurant is named for him, its main dining room dominated by a scrap metal sculpture of the bear.

But despite his fearsome demeanor, enhanced by four-inch claws, few bear experts consider the Boss a threat to park visitors or residents. Rather, they fear his celebrity and the legions of fans it have created, may be the biggest threat to his life and his dominance.

#105. Revealed: Israel uploaded millions of photos to Amazon’s facial recognition platform to track Palestinians

Upload came as the Israeli military was increasing its reliance on Amazon’s cloud platform to meet the technological demands of its bombardment of Gaza

The Israeli military uploaded 3.5m photos to Amazon’s cloud platform in order to use the US company’s facial recognition technology to analyse images of Palestinians, the Guardian can reveal.

Leaked documents show the expansive catalogue of images was moved into Amazon’s cloud in the early months of Israel’s devastating aerial bombardment and ground offensive in Gaza.

The images were uploaded by a department of the Israeli military that holds a vast collection of photos covering the Palestinian populations in Gaza and the West Bank.

The department informed Amazon of the decision to upload the images in early 2024 so it could use Rekognition, the company’s AI-powered image analysis platform for a facial recognition project.

The documents obtained by the Guardian form part of an investigation into the Israeli military’s reliance on the technology and infrastructure of Amazon’s cloud computing business to monitor Palestinians.

According to the files and interviews with military sources, Israel has in recent years migrated huge volumes of data – including sensitive intelligence material and information about Palestinians living under occupation – into Amazon’s datacentres.

The large set of images was moved into an Amazon datacentre near Tel Aviv by a department commonly known as Rolling Stone, which sits within a military software unit.

The department takes its name from a system widely used by the Israeli military that serves as a central repository of information about Palestinians living in Gaza and the West Bank.

At the time, the department was exploring using facial recognition technologies to analyse the extensive catalogues of images of Palestinians it holds, several sources with knowledge of its work said.

After Israel’s ground invasion of Gaza in October 2023, its military routinely collected images of Palestinians as they passed through checkpoints, often after they had been forcibly displaced by the bombing campaign.

It’s not clear whether the 3.5m images uploaded to Amazon’s Rekognition were captured at these checkpoints, which effectively served as pop-up surveillance posts.

However, a military source familiar with facial recognition efforts at the checkpoints said Amazon’s cloud infrastructure was used due to the “very large scale” of images collected at the sites.

Israel has long used facial recognition technology, particularly in its occupation of the West Bank. Face scanning is routine at checkpoints, while Israeli soldiers use custom-built smartphone apps to collect images of people living in the Palestinian territory.

In the early stages of its campaign in Gaza, Israel used Rekognition to identify hostages captured by militants in the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023. The military also deployed facial recognition technology to identify people it suspected were militants.

The New York Times has previously reported how the military experimented with commercially available technology in Gaza to detect faces in crowds and in drone footage, at times wrongly flagging civilians as militants.

The new documents reveal the Israeli military’s use of Amazon technology, both for experiments with facial recognition and for a more far-reaching project to move its data on to the servers of the computing arm of the conglomerate founded by billionaire Jeff Bezos.

They also shed light on what the Israeli military is moving into the cloud and how it uses Amazon Web Services (AWS) infrastructure to support the collection and processing of Palestinians’ personal data, typically gathered indiscriminately, through intrusive means and without their consent.

The files suggest the ties between AWS and the Israeli military deepened during a period in which Israel has been accused of committing grave crimes in Gaza, including genocide – a charge denied by Israel, but supported by a UN commission of inquiry, Israeli human rights groups and genocide experts.

In early 2024, a small group of Israeli soldiers worked from AWS’s offices in Tel Aviv, located on the 28th floor of one of Israel’s tallest skyscrapers.

The soldiers were exploring ways to use the company’s cloud infrastructure to more effectively operate a facial recognition programme underway in Gaza, several military sources said.

It’s not clear whether their work was made operational or if it related to the 3.5m images uploaded to Rekognition. The presence of the group at AWS’s offices, however, underscores the close relationship between the Israeli military and the cloud provider.

An AWS spokesperson said its customers “regularly visit our offices for briefings and meetings”. They added: “Any suggestion that these visits are anything other than routine customer engagement is wrong and unsubstantiated.”

The foundation of Israel’s relationship with Amazon is a sweeping 2021 contract known as Project Nimbus. Under the multibillion dollar deal, AWS and Google provide cloud services to a wide range of government and military clients.

The Guardian revealed last year that the Nimbus contract contains unusual terms that prohibit the US companies from restricting or suspending Israeli government and military use of their cloud services, even if Israel breaches their terms of service.

This would prevent the kind of unilateral action taken by Microsoft last year when it disabled the Israeli military’s access to technology used to operate a mass surveillance system uncovered by the Guardian and +972 Magazine.

As part of the Nimbus deal, AWS opened new datacentres in Israel in early 2023, allowing the Israeli government and military clients to migrate data from their own servers, according to documents. A large proportion of data was also moved from AWS datacentres in Ireland.

A central component of the Nimbus contract, files show, was an obligation on AWS to provide the Israeli military with access to a segregated area within its Tel Aviv datacentres, fortified with enhanced digital and physical security measures.

This isolated area within the AWS cloud – used to store sensitive material relating to military operations and intelligence collection – was described by the company and its client as a “walled garden”.

The Israeli military’s reliance on AWS reflects its embrace in recent years of leading US tech companies to meet the technological demands of war with rapidly scalable storage, computing power and AI-driven services.

The role of AI in Israel’s offensive in Gaza has come under fresh scrutiny particularly after the release of NAZA, a Guardian-produced documentary about the remote surveillance and killing of Palestinian civilians.

The Rolling Stone department is among AWS’s most significant Israeli military clients, documents suggest. Its use of Rekognition in early 2024 to analyse 3.5m images came after it had expanded its use of AWS products and services to store and analyse its data.

Files show the department migrated significant volumes of the data it holds into the so-called “walled garden” in AWS’s cloud throughout 2023. A military source involved in this effort said it had planned to move all of its databases from the military servers into AWS’s cloud.

The source, who spoke on the condition of anonymity, said: “The plan [at Rolling Stone] was to migrate pictures, videos, all the confidential intelligence information – everything.”

A spokesperson for the Israel Defence Forces (IDF) said it “does not intend to comment on engagements or services related to operational needs”.

The Rolling Stone department oversees several expansive databases that collate data on Palestinians, including the system from which the branch takes its name.

Used by the military for decades, the Rolling Stone system is constantly updated and includes data such as basic personal and family details, property holdings, and information on checkpoint crossings, work permits, and criminal activity.

A soldier who provided testimony to Breaking the Silence, an Israeli NGO, in 2016 described Rolling Stone as “a computerised system where all the information relevant to the control of the Palestinian civilian population is centralised”.

As a result of Israel’s control of the population registries covering Gaza and the West Bank, the Rolling Stone department has amassed millions of Palestinians’ photographs taken for the identity cards issued to every resident when they turn 16 years old.

Sources familiar with its work said the images were routinely used for facial recognition programmes. These include Blue Wolf, a smartphone app Israeli soldiers use in the West Bank to scan Palestinians’ faces and access information about them, including whether they’ve been flagged as suspects for arrest.

In Gaza, at temporary Israeli checkpoints established after the ground invasion, cameras trained on Palestinian people were used to capture their images as they approached the sites. Facial recognition was then used to match people to images held in Rolling Stone’s systems, sources said.

The checkpoints became notorious in Gaza as places where people were pulled aside and detained. Many were taken into Israeli custody and held incommunicado, amounting to enforced disappearances, according to several UN bodies and rights groups.

Documents suggest AWS’s cloud technology was used at times during 2024 to support the work of the checkpoints. Military insiders said the checkpoints were referred to by Israeli soldiers as Nakaz – a Hebrew word for drainage.

Harry Davies is one of the Guardian producers on NAZA, which Yuval Abraham co-directed. NAZA is now in cinemas worldwide and will screen for free on the Guardian’s website from early December. Sign up here to be the first to watch it

#104. Monday briefing: The many stages of the UK-EU relationship – and what’s next

In today’s newsletter: From situationship to a possible makeup, a look at the evolving relationship, and the bloc’s big feelings about the future

Good morning. Ahead of the hotly anticipated UK-EU summit this Friday, Andy Burnham is expected to make the reset of our relationship with Europe the pivotal theme of his white-tie speech at the lord mayor’s banquet in London tonight.

The prospect of closer ties – and the possibility of going “all the way” to reversing Brexit – has been met with jubilation and relief in some quarters and vehement opposition in others, while around Westminster and across the media the psychodrama has been spiralling for weeks.

At this point, I’m reminded of that Robert Burns couplet: “O wad some Pow’r the giftie gie us / To see oursels as ithers see us!” What does the EU think about all this? Are they ready to reverse what leading figures have repeatedly described as a colossal act of self-harm? Or are they viewing this latest iteration with bombastic side-eye? I posed these questions to Jennifer Rankin, the Guardian’s Brussels correspondent. First the headlines.

UK news | A woman who alleges she was raped by Russell Brand when she was 16 has waived her right to anonymity to campaign for a staggered age of consent, which would make it illegal for adults over a certain age to have sex with 16- and 17-year-olds.

Youth unemployment | Andy Burnham has pledged to intervene as early as childhood to tackle youth unemployment before a watershed review, promising “a root-and-branch rethink of how we support childhood in the modern world”.

Environment | The strongest El Niño in centuries has added urgency to UN climate talks in Turkey as vulnerable countries face “compounding” crises. In other Cop31 news, queer and gay protesters defied a crackdown on LGBTQ+ rights in Turkey to lead a civil society climate march.

UK news | Two people aged 22 and 23 have died after their car was hit by a BMW being pursued by police. West Yorkshire police said four people had been arrested and appealed for witnesses to come forward.

Air transport | The Trump administration says Friday’s four-hour nationwide Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) ground stop that delayed or cancelled thousands of flight stemmed from routine maintenance. The US transportation secretary, Sean Duffy, blamed prior presidents and Congress for the stoppage.

Ahead of the summit, the pro-Brexit polity has been in broadcast mode: headlines from the past few days include: “Burnham’s plot to rejoin the EU would leave the average UK household £6,500 poorer” (Express); “Taxpayers funding pro-EU university courses” (Sunday Telegraph); “Staggering cost of PM’s EU reset” (Daily Mail).

Stay Out, a new campaign against rejoining the European Union, launched last week. While the guy you really want to hear from right now – David Cameron, the prime minister who lost the 2016 Brexit referendum – has insisted the UK’s problems have nothing to do with its exit from the EU.

Even within the Labour fold, there are rumblings of unease from MPs and unions.

But the one-day summit in Brussels this Friday does not include any formal discussion of rejoining: instead, the EU and UK hope to sign deals on food and drink, emissions trading and the youth experience scheme (a work and study exchange programme).

Still, the mood music is upbeat. Back in September, when Burnham first opened the door to a Brexit reversal, French president Emmanual Macron responded: “welcome back.” while Spanish prime minister Pedro Sánchez said: “Spain would welcome Britain’s return with open arms.”

The enthusiasm is genuine, says Jennifer. Indeed, some European diplomats have told her the technicalities could be wrapped up within a year because the UK remains closely aligned with the bloc in so many areas. “It would be such a vote of confidence in the European project for a country to decide to return after voting to leave”.

Situationship: what’s at stake at this week’s summit?

Though Burnham has predicted “some long nights” of negotiation this week, Jennifer says there’s optimism on each side as the first two deals on food and drink and emissions trading were largely agreed.

There are two sticking points with the youth exchange deal. First, the Home Office wants to limit numbers to the UK to keep migration figures low. Second, there’s disagreement over whether those students would pay UK domestic rates of tuition fees or the international rate, which is four or five times higher for some courses.

The UK is offering concessions on tuition fees if it can join Made in Europe, the EU legislation designed to protect domestic manufacturing from cheap Chinese competition. But the EU says it can’t make a deal on Made in Europe this week, because the law is still at a draft stage.

And here’s where it gets complicated. The European Commission has proposed delaying an agreement on tuition fees for six months, which could allow time to get the UK into Made in Europe, a diplomatic win for Britain. But there’s pushback from member states who want to pin down the UK on tuition fees this week. “This is a real priority for the EU, in almost emotional terms,” Jennifer says. “Lots of EU officials and diplomats studied in the UK, or their children did, and they felt this was one of the big losses of Brexit, that these person-to-person links were being eroded.”

On-again, off-again: what else could get in the way of closer working?

In play at Friday’s summit will also be a future deal on Britain’s return to the EU’s internal electricity market and a joint migration pact, with the UK pushing for access to EU crime databases to tackle the small boats crisis.

Member states are already working hard to smooth things out on the electricity front, to stop it becoming a sticking point. A couple of weeks ago, Jennifer reported on leaders from Germany, Denmark and elsewhere calling for deeper cooperation on clean energy – which she reads as an attempt to head off a row about how much the UK should contribute to EU cohesion funds for the evident privilege of cutting red tape, easing trade and, ultimately, reducing costs.

A similar row over UK contributions scuppered negotiations last year on our entry into a €150bn EU defence fund.

It could also be a challenge for the UK to realign with the EU on farming, after changing to a payment model that puts more emphasis on delivering environmental benefits. And past UK governments have been lukewarm about EU AI regulation, devised post Brexit.

Conscious partnership: will the EU welcome future closeness this week?

I learned long ago there’s no such thing as a stupid question, so I ask mine now: will there be a moment on Friday when Ursula von der Leyen offers the equivalent of Macron’s “welcome back”? Will there be a “just give us six months to sort out the paperwork Andy” vibe?

The European Commission president is, by nature, a cautious politician, Jennifer tells me, so she doesn’t expect anything so specific. “I’m sure she – and António Costa, the European Council president – will get asked about it at the press conference, and they’ll want to send a signal about the UK being welcome to rejoin.”

But behind closed doors, in Brussels and other capitals, what Jennifer hears repeatedly is that it’s up to the UK to decide what it wants. And, while the prospect of Brexiteer Nigel Farage in Downing Street has faded in recent months, there remains a deal of caution about the vicissitudes of UK politics. “Now the Burnham government is exploring the options, but there’s also an awareness that you could have a very different government in a few years’ time,” she says.

It’s not you, it’s me: what else is Europe worrying about?

The EU and its member states are preoccupied with far weightier problems: the war in Ukraine (Volodymyr Zelenskyy attends every EU summit), the rise in support for the far right, street protests, hybrid threats from Russia, a trade deficit with China that has reached a staggering €1bn a day in 2026.

“There are so many other issues crowding on the agenda,” says Jennifer. So it’s not all about us? “That’s actually been the theme of my decade of reporting from Brussels,” she says with a laugh.

There’s profound concern about the prospect of Marine Le Pen winning the French presidential elections next year, which could tip the bloc into a crisis if she pursues her resolutely anti-EU agenda, which includes reducing France’s substantial contribution to the EU budget.

Beyond that, there’s a broader question about increasingly divided and unstable politics across member states. “Every single member state has to agree to the UK’s re-entry,” Jennifer reminds me. “Who knows what the EU will look like in 2030 when Britain could be having a rejoin referendum?”

Grand Theft Auto VI: the cultural event of the decade?

This week, after a 13-year wait, gamers will finally get hold of Grand Theft Auto VI, the latest instalment of the most successful console computer game in history. How did a game of stealing cars and shooting strangers become such an enormous cultural phenomenon? A bit of good news to remind you that the world’s not all bad

Some travellers seek out ancient landmarks when they visit a new place. Hidde Burgmans, from the Netherlands, looks for ancient trees – and he’s created an app of the same name to help people find remarkable old trees around the world, from Malaysian baobabs to the Fortingall yew in Perthshire, Scotland, thought to be an astounding 5,000 years old.

Users can review remarkable old trees around the world, and upload their favourites to the Ancient Trees app. “On Tuesday somebody photographed a tree in Edinburgh. Today, someone in Freiburg,” said Burgmans. “That just makes me happy. I thought it was quite a niche, geeky thing, but apparently more people are interested in ancient trees than I thought.”

#102. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#100. Israel Kidnapped Iranian Generals in Hunt for Ron Arad

Israeli officials were convinced that Tehran was to blame for the airman’s disappearance. Alternative theories got little attention, until now.

Israeli officials were convinced that Tehran was to blame for the airman’s disappearance. Alternative theories got little attention, until now.

At a protest in 2025 in Tel Aviv, supporters of Israeli hostages held captive by Hamas held pictures of the hostages and of Ron Arad, who has been missing since 1986.Credit...Itai Ron/Reuters

By Adam GoldmanRonen BergmanFarnaz Fassihi and Hwaida Saad

Ronen Bergman, Farnaz Fassihi, Adam Goldman and Hwaida Saad have spent years investigating the disappearance of the Israeli airman Ron Arad.

Gen. Abdullah Saberi, an Iranian commander, stepped out for a jog early one morning in an upscale diplomatic quarter of Syria’s capital. Israeli intelligence officers waited nearby. Posing as movers, they had slipped past the neighborhood’s security checkpoints.

As he headed for a nearby park, General Saberi passed two men who carried an oversize refrigerator box. In a flash, the men restrained the general, drugged him, threw the box over his head, shoved him into a white van and left as if nothing had happened.

He was whisked to Israel for interrogation, according to numerous officials in the Middle East, including senior Iranian officials close to General Saberi and a Syrian official who reviewed video of the September 2021 kidnapping.

As a veteran of Iran’s powerful Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps, General Saberi knew many secrets. But his interrogators were interested in only one.

Mr. Arad, an Israeli airman, vanished decades ago, and the Israeli military says he is the only service member classified as missing in action. The government’s long search for him has captivated the public and has come to symbolize Israel’s commitment, rooted in Jewish tradition, to bringing its soldiers home, dead or alive. Articles, books and documentaries have been produced about the hunt for Mr. Arad.

The Israeli government was so determined to find him that, even four decades after his disappearance, it took the risk of kidnapping General Saberi in the shadow of his own embassy.
