#99. Andy Burnham to say Brexit ‘has not worked’ and he will sign new deal with EU

PM to meet European leaders at summit this week aiming to secure agreements that ‘could raise GDP by 0.4%’

Andy Burnham will say on Monday that Brexit “has not worked” and that he will sign a new deal with the EU.

It is the latest in a string of comments by the prime minister criticising Brexit, after he told the Welsh Labour conference on Saturday that the UK has “lost more than we gained” through leaving the EU.

Burnham is due to meet European leaders at a summit this week and secure agreements that he says could raise GDP by 0.4% – about £17bn – including on food and farming, emissions trading and a youth mobility scheme.

Speaking at the lord mayor’s banquet in the City of London, Burnham is expected to argue that Boris Johnson’s promise to take back control was not delivered, and that the country was left with a needlessly hard Brexit that created barriers for businesses and damaged its economic potential.

“It’s like the Emperor’s New Clothes – where no one is prepared to say the obvious truth. Well, I say it as I see it. And to be frank, the emperor has nothing on,” Burnham will say. “The Brexit we were given has not worked. We have lost more than we gained.”

Burnham is to say he will sign an agreement with the EU, so long as it:

Is consistent with the Labour manifesto and the government’s red lines.

It improves the Brexit deal and brings it closer to what people thought they were voting for in 2016.

And it passes the so-called “Makerfield test” by delivering real, tangible improvements in people’s everyday lives.

“This is a win-win,” Burnham is expected to say. “It illustrates our new approach. Not adversarial. No longer burning bridges – but being constructive and positive, showing that we can come together to confront shared challenges.”

However, the EU relations minister, Hamish Falconer, told the BBC on Sunday that the UK could have a “voice in the room” under a new deal with the bloc, but no vote on EU food policies.

He added there could well be an “exit clause” in case a future UK government reversed the closer alignment, explaining that the EU was “worried” about the possibility of a future Reform or Conservative government undoing the agreement.

The exit clause, previously nicknamed the “Farage clause”, is designed to guard against any the cost of potentially reversing any reset on Friday should the UK’s political landscape change.

The cost of infrastructure at the borders has largely been paid for by private companies, including Eurotunnel, but French staff the food checks there, at Calais port and at Boulogne-sur-Mer, where all fish checks take place.

Meanwhile, Johnson, the former Conservative prime minister who resigned over Partygate in 2022, has said Britain would “absolutely” vote for Brexit again if Burnham forced another vote on the issue.

He told the Telegraph: “It is madness to escalate the Brexit issue as your political priority. He will impale himself and his party on this point, and he needs to move off it because he won’t win – because the reality is that people are not fools.”

#98. ‘It’s like the 16th-century version of doomscrolling’: the Book of Miracles may fetch £1m at auction

Exclusive: Public institutions get first chance to bid for rare ‘wonderbook’ of apocalyptic visions and extreme weather

In one illustration, five suns blaze down on Leipzig in 1551. In another, blood rain falls on Rome in 1456. In a third scene, dated 1552, monstrous hailstones batter the Dutch city of Dordrecht during a storm so fierce people feared Judgment Day had come.

Created during the mid-16th century, when earthquakes, storms, floods and other extreme weather events were often interpreted as ominous warnings from God, the Book of Miracles contains 167 paintings of apocalyptic visions, celestial wonders and meteorological phenomena.

It is the only known copy. To this day, no one is sure why it was created.

Now, public institutions will have their first chance to bid for the Book of Miracles since scholars learned it existed. It is expected to fetch between £700,000 and £1m when it is auctioned in London on 10 December, as the star lot of the Christie’s valuable books and manuscripts sale.

Members of the public will also have a rare opportunity to see it in person for six days before it goes under the hammer.

For nearly half a millennium, scholars were unaware of its existence. Found in the library of a small baroque castle in southern Germany nearly 20 years ago, the manuscript, which is from 1550s Augsburg, was put up for auction alongside the castle’s furniture, carpets and tankards in 2008 and sold for just over £100,000.

Shortly after the sale, the manuscript entered the private collection of Mickey Cartin, one of Connecticut’s biggest contemporary art collectors.

Eugenio Donadoni, Christie’s international specialist in medieval and Renaissance manuscripts, said the book was an “exceptionally rare” German wonderbook.

“German wonderbooks functioned as the sensationalist mass media of their day,” he said. “They were used by Lutheran theologians such as Martin Luther and Philip Melanchthon to further their Protestant eschatology, but crucially – with all the depictions of comets, cosmological events, natural disasters, all verified by witnesses, with exact dates and geographic coordinates – they laid early foundations for empirical observation and reporting. They mark a shift from medieval to Enlightenment.”

He expects the sale will attract collectors of both historic and modern art because some of the scenes, which are painted in watercolour and gouache, are “super surrealist … almost Dalí-esque”.

“If it weren’t for the little captions, which are obviously 16th-century German, some of them look so vividly modern … it’s really jaw-dropping,” Donadoni said.

The scenes depicted include a plague of locusts arriving in Poland on a “terrible wind” in 1527 and the North Sea flooding the coast of Frisia – in what is now the Netherlands and Germany – in 1230.

Other pictures are of fiery comets and eclipses, while scholars think an illustration of “wondrous dragons” flying across the sky over Bavaria in 1533 depicts the northern lights.

Underneath each scene, a German picture caption describes what is happening and where and when the event took place.

Your weekly art world round-up, sketching out all the biggest stories, scandals and exhibitions

“All of these events are portentous,” said Donadoni. “The artists of the Book of Miracles, relying on contemporary broadsheets, chronicles and histories, have condensed into pictorial form the concerns of an age that was gripped by an anxiety – and learned curiosity – about the future.”

The book records how uneasy people felt about climatic anomalies at a time of doctrinal disunity, when there was growing interest in documenting and cataloguing visible, tangible scientific phenomena, he said.

“It’s really fascinating … it is a window into how Europeans were dealing with the impending end of the world, political uncertainty and extreme – and strange – weather events.”

The five suns over Leipzig, for example, is thought to depict the optical illusion of a parhelion, more commonly known as a sun dog. “You see in the sky various different rings of light and it looks like suns,” Donadoni said.

“Blood rain” occurs when high concentrations of red-coloured dust particles, usually from the Sahara, mix with water droplets in rain clouds. “Many of these things have actual rational scientific explanations to them,” Donadoni said.

He said he imagined that leafing through the pages of the book in the 1550s was similar to bingeing on bad news on social media today: “I think it could be a little like the 16th-century version of doomscrolling.”

In this way, the book straddles a momentous turning point in history. “We’re right at that period where the superstition of the middle ages turns into the age of scientific discovery,” Donadoni said.

Till-Holger Borchert, the director of the Suermondt Ludwig Museum in Aachen, has studied the manuscript and said the first time he saw it, his reaction was: “Wow”. “It’s one of a kind,” he said. “It grabs you by your heart or your soul or your belly.”

He said he thought £1m was a “very conservative estimate” of its value. “It [the final price] could possibly be much more,” he said.

A facsimile of the manuscript was published in 2013 that got people “really excited” about it, Borchert added, and since then the Book of Miracles has become “a kind of icon for people who are interested in apocalyptic things”. It would be wrong to value it merely as an historical document: “It’s more than that. It speaks to us, to our generation.”

#94. International Students Are Turning Away From U.S. Universities

For a second year, Trump administration policies appear to be scaring away international students, disrupting the finances of the American colleges that depend on them.

The enrollment of new international students at American universities dropped once again this fall, fueled by deep uncertainty about visas and job prospects.

A survey released Monday found that new international student enrollment plunged by 15 percent, a figure that was particularly worrisome for higher education executives following a precipitous 18 percent decrease last year.

Including preexisting students, international student enrollment declined 9 percent this fall, according to the survey, adding to last year’s drop of 3 percent.

For years, American colleges and universities had attracted growing numbers of international students, who often paid full tuition, indirectly subsidizing tuition for domestic students. But President Trump’s crackdown on immigrants, which has occasionally swept up foreign students, along with confusing visa policies and a trade war with China, one of the top suppliers of international students, have stoked fears that the United States is no longer a welcoming place to study.

Ted Mitchell, president of the American Council on Education, a large education trade organization, characterized the combined two-year downturn as “likely historic,” noting that international students had been a growth sector for higher education.

He pointed to departures among international students who were already enrolled as a worrying sign. “These are people who’ve already started, and they’ve said, ‘No, the risk is too high. I’m not going back,’” Dr. Mitchell said.

#93. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#91. Gérard Depardieu drops appeal against sexual assault conviction

French actor apologises to two women he was convicted of assaulting on a film set in 2021

The French actor Gérard Depardieu has dropped his appeal against a conviction for sexually assaulting two women on a film set, and has for the first time apologised to the victims.

“I was found guilty. I hear it and I accept this conviction,” Depardieu, 77, said in a written statement. “I address my deep and sincere apologies to those who suffered.”

The actor, who has made more than 200 films and TV series, wrote: “For a long time on film sets, I hid behind provocation and ribald humour.”

He said he accepted that remarks and “some of my gestures” were not “without consequences”, particularly because he held a powerful position in the entertainment industry.

Depardieu said he had never intended to hurt the women he encountered but had “come to understand that intention changes nothing about the harm caused”.

He said times had changed and that he belonged “to a world that no longer exists”.

At a trial last year, Depardieu was found guilty of sexually assaulting a 55-year-old set dresser and a 35-year-old assistant director during the shooting of the feature film Les Volets Verts (The Green Shutters) in Paris in September 2021.

His suspended sentence will now stand and his name must be added to France’s sex offender register.

The set dresser, named in the media as Amélie, said outside the Paris appeal court on Monday: “I waited for years for this apology.” She said she would have preferred if Depardieu had attended court to deliver it in person.

The assistant director, named in the media as Sarah, said: “The monster of cinema has fallen.”

Depardieu’s conviction last year was a turning point for the #MeToo movement in France. He became the biggest figure in the French film industry to be convicted of sexual assault after years of criticism of the country’s reluctance to take women’s claims of abuse seriously.

Depardieu had previously denied all allegations of sexual assault. He told last year’s trial that the media had used allegations against him to damage his reputation.

He attacked the #MeToo movement as well as women who had staged protests outside venues during his concert tour at the time of the allegations. “This movement is going to become a terror,” he said.

The trial also set an important legal precedent when the judge ruled that Depardieu’s defence lawyer, Jérémie Assous, had been “excessively harsh” to the two women in court, saying they were not real victims and were liars.

Assous had said the women were working for the cause of “rabid feminism” and called their lawyers hysterical, “abject and stupid” and described them as having an “unbearable voice”.

The judge awarded the complainants compensation for being further victimised by Depardieu’s defence during the trial, ordering Depardieu to pay each woman €1,000 (£835).

The court had heard there was a “system of impunity” around Depardieu, with film industry insiders staying silent to keep cashing in on his name.

Depardieu has appealed against a separate decision requiring him to face trial on charges of raping and sexually assaulting the actor Charlotte Arnould at his Paris home in 2018. Appeal judges will rule this month on whether to uphold his appeal or allow the trial to go ahead next year.

In an open letter to Le Figaro in October 2023, Depardieu denied all allegations and said any encounter with Arnould had been consensual. “Never, ever, have I abused a woman,” he wrote.

In December 2023, when Depardieu was under formal investigation for rape and sexual assault in the Arnould case and also facing scrutiny over sexist comments revealed in a TV documentary, the French president defended him, saying “he makes France proud”.

Asked at the time about stripping Depardieu of a state award, Emmanuel Macron said: “You will never see me participate in a manhunt … I hate that type of thing.”

#90. Canada’s Leader, Once a Curiosity at Asia Summits, Is Now a Star

Prime Minister Mark Carney is courting Europe, but the bigger payoff might be in China, where he will share summit stages with Xi Jinping, Vladimir Putin and President Trump.

Reporting from Phnom Penh, Cambodia; Manila; and Dublin

The importance of Europe to Prime Minister Mark Carney’s plan to diversify Canada’s economy and politics away from its reliance on the United States was underscored last week by his travel schedule.

Mr. Carney added a stop in Dublin, joining a meeting of European leaders before the start of a previously planned trip to Asia.

Last month Mr. Carney announced that Canada is officially seeking to become the first “associate member” of the European Union. But experts say that Asia offers the greater economic opportunity.

“Prime Minister Carney certainly seems to have a strong focus on Europe, which gives an impression that he is not looking toward Asia,” said Kazuto Suzuki, a professor at the University of Tokyo’s graduate school of public policy. “Nevertheless, if Canada is to avoid dependence on the U.S. market and form a coalition of middle powers, cooperation with Asia is essential.”

Last year, Mr. Carney was something of a curiosity at the meetings of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations and the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation forum. But his speech in January at the World Economic Forum in Davos, Switzerland, and President Trump’s addition of energy security threats to his trade war with the world have made Mr. Carney and his energy-rich country a marquee attraction at both.

#89. Ukraine hits Kerch bridge between Russia and occupied Crimea

Traffic temporarily restricted on key connection after bridge targeted during overnight drone barrage by Ukraine

Ukraine has hit the Kerch bridge connecting occupied Crimea with Russia, in a symbolic blow that temporarily paralysed road traffic and left thousands of drivers stranded.

A Ukrainian kamikaze drone crashed into one of the bridge’s arches at 5.38am local time, local channels reported. Earlier drones could be heard buzzing above the structure, as Russian air defence units opened fire.

The extent of the damage was unclear. One report said the arch was listing at a 15-degree angle and “looked tired”.

The Kremlin-appointed governor of Crimea, Sergey Aksyonov, confirmed the “enemy” had launched an attack. “As a result, traffic will be restricted, likely until the afternoon,” he wrote on Telegram.

Russian officials said the crossing would remain closed while experts assessed its condition. About 2,000 vehicles were reportedly stuck in traffic jams on both sides.

In a call with reporters, the Kremlin spokesperson, Dmitry Peskov, downplayed the incident. He said: “Minor damage is being assessed. ⁠Traffic across the bridge continues.”

Ukraine says the bridge is a legitimate target for its armed forces, and has made at least two previous attempts to blow it up.

In 2022 a truck laden with explosives damaged part of the road section. A second strike using a “sea baby” drone took place the following year. Russia has subsequently strengthened air defences and installed metal barges and boom barriers.

Overnight Ukraine launched 1,336 drones, in what the Kremlin says was one of the biggest attacks so far this year. About 600 were intercepted on their approach to Moscow, claimed the capital’s mayor, Sergei Sobyanin.

The barrage took place days before a summit in Shenzhen between China’s president, Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Putin. US officials say the talks will not focus on the war in Ukraine, where Russia’s military offensive has largely stalled.

A digest of the morning's main headlines from the Europe edition emailed direct to you every week day

Ukraine says its retaliatory strikes are justified retribution for Russia’s own missile and drone barrages of its cities and energy grid – attacks that have killed hundreds of civilians and regularly knock out power and heating for millions. In recent weeks Russia has repeatedly hit two critical bridges in Kyiv over the Dnipro river.

Diplomatic efforts on ending the almost five-year war have so far gone nowhere. After a fresh round of talks in Abu Dhabi last week, Putin reiterated his hardline territorial demands and said any deployment of foreign troops would be seen in Moscow as a “continuation of war”.

The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy, said on Friday the Trump administration had proposed that Kyiv gives Russia the eastern Donbas region – including towns and villages Russia does not control – in exchange for Nato membership or Nato-like guarantees.

Zelenskyy ruled out handing over territory. The details of possible US security guarantees were vague and did not feature American troops on the ground, he said, citing conversations with Trump’s son-in-law Jared Kushner and the real-estate developer Steve Witkoff. The emissaries last week saw Putin in Moscow.

#88. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#86. MP Helen Haines’s first words since leaving hospital after alleged attack led to double amputation

Independent MP says she is ‘overwhelmed’ by thousands of warm wishes and thanks Australians for charity donations

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

The independent Australian MP Helen Haines has been discharged from hospital, four weeks after she was allegedly attacked by a man outside her electorate office in Victoria resulting in the amputation of both her legs.

In a statement, Haines said she had moved to the rehabilitation stage of her recovery, which would continue in Melbourne.

“This is a really positive moment, and I am full of gratitude for the expert and compassionate care I received at the Alfred [hospital] and am receiving in this next stage,” Haines said.

“I appreciate the thousands of warm wishes and supportive messages from people across Indi, throughout Australia and around the world. They are giving me strength as I focus on this next phase of healing.”

The prime minister, Anthony Albanese, said his thoughts were with Haines.

“We continue to provide support to Helen, her family, community and staff as she undertakes her rehabilitation,” Albanese said in a statement.

Haines said she was “overwhelmed” by the many Australians who had donated to the Save the Children charity fundraiser.

Her husband, Phil Haines, had made the request to the public on her behalf to donate to the charity in lieu of flowers in the aftermath of the alleged attack.

As of Monday afternoon, more than $1.46m had been raised for the charity through Haines’s fundraising appeal.

“I am overwhelmed by the generosity of Australians in the form of donations to Save the Children and I look forward to working with them in due course on applying these funds to improve the lives of children.”

Haines, the federal member for the Victorian seat of Indi, had been in a critical but stable condition after she was repeatedly struck by a vehicle in Wangaratta on 19 October. Her condition was then downgraded to “serious but stable” on 24 October.

Police will allege the 65-year-old was standing by the boot of her car outside her electoral office when a car did a U-turn and struck her repeatedly.

Tarrawingee man Brendan Nugent, 43, has been charged and the incident continues to be investigated. He is known to her family.

Police charged Nugent with dangerous driving causing serious injury, intentionally cause serious injury and the commonwealth offence of causing harm to a commonwealth public official.

Police have also charged him with two additional offences – cultivating and possessing cannabis – according to court documents.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

The foreign affairs minister, Penny Wong, paid tribute to Haines in the Senate on Monday and on behalf of the parliament wished her strength through her challenging rehabilitation.

“It says a great deal about her character that even as she faces these challenges, in her statement she looks ahead to the work she wants to do to improve the lives of children following the donations to Save the Children that Australians made at her request. It is an example of her determination and her generosity that is so admired,” Wong said.

The leader of the opposition in the Senate, Michaelia Cash, added that Haines’s discharge from hospital was warmly welcomed across the parliament.

“She has demonstrated extraordinary courage and resilience. And on behalf, in particular, of my Coalition Senate colleagues, we wish Helen every strength during her continued recovery.”

Haines has been given leave of absence until 26 November, the last sitting day of the year.

MPs across parliament have expressed concern for their safety and that of their staff since the alleged attack, and prompted conversations about the temperature of political debate.

Albanese, during the last parliamentary sitting, warned against the rise in anger “that distorts and corrodes our public discourse”, and urged voters and politicians not to ignore it.

“Enabled by the anonymity and speed afforded by social media, we are seeing rage and aggression increasing, not just in politics, but towards workers in hospitality and retail, in schools and in hospitals, towards police, paramedics, and other officials, we are seeing a normalisation of the language of grievance and violence.”

The independent MP Allegra Spender said it was “wonderful” news that Haines was making good progress in her recovery.

“She is a remarkable person and her strength and positivity after everything that has happened inspires me.”

#85. Searching for the Last Stoats of Waiheke Island

Can the tourist destination become the first urban island to rid itself of the stoat — an introduced predator that has ravaged New Zealand’s endemic wildlife?

Can the tourist destination become the first urban island to rid itself of the stoat — an introduced predator that has ravaged New Zealand’s endemic wildlife?

Frank Lepera, stoat operations manager at Te Korowai o Waiheke, installing a trap where a stoat was recently reported, on Waiheke Island, New Zealand.Credit...

Reporting from Waiheke Island, New Zealand. She did not see a live stoat on the island.

To catch a stoat, you need to think like one. For Tilde Sorensen, that means imagining having tiny legs and picturing which route the weasel-like creature might take as it haunts the wilderness of New Zealand, preying on unique birdlife.

“We’ve caught all of the ones that will happily go into a trap. So now we’ve got to figure out how to get the really shy ones,” Ms. Sorensen said of the stoat population on Waiheke Island, a 40-minute boat ride from New Zealand’s largest city, Auckland.

“We’re trying to work ourselves out of a job.”

Ms. Sorensen is a stoat-detect-and-respond coordinator for a project called Te Korowai o Waiheke, a Māori name that means The Cloak of Waiheke. It has captured more than 280 stoats since 2020 and, while it is not known how many remain, testing suggests the population has dwindled.

The campaign to eradicate stoats, and many other introduced species, has widespread support in New Zealand, where introduced predators, including rats and possums, kill about 25 million native birds each year.

Waiheke Island, which covers about 36 square miles, is home to 4,300 people, a number that swells to about 45,000 in the summer.

#83. NSW halts new law to crack down on underquoting by real estate agents at 11th hour

Backdown is one of several premier Chris Minns has announced in recent weeks as state prepares for election in March

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

The Minns government has mothballed laws intended to protect homebuyers from underquoting after the premier said real estate agents in his Sydney electorate had raised concerns about their effect amid a housing market downturn.

Changes due to come into effect on Tuesday would have banned agents in New South Wales from lowering the advertised price of a property beneath a written offer previously rejected by a seller, with fines of up to $110,000 or three times an agent’s commission, whichever was greater.

But the laws and fines will be paused for up to a year after a last-minute intervention by the premier, Chris Minns, who flagged on Monday morning that concerns had been raised by agents in his south Sydney electorate of Kogarah.

“It has come across my desk from real estate agents in my electorate,” he told reporters, after he was asked about fears the laws could prevent sellers from advertising at a lower rate now that the market has fallen. Home values in Sydney have dropped by 10.1% from their 2026 peak, according to data from Cotality.

“I do think it’s [an] unforeseen [consequence], but it is something we’re going to have to deal with because it’s not the seller or the agent’s fault if the price of real estate is dropping, and the legislation at least intuitively believed that the prices would always rise,” said Minns.

The changes, which were passed in June, were modelled on laws already in effect in Victoria. Under additional transparency measures which came into effect in Victoria last month, sellers must reveal their reserve price at least one week before a sale, and agents must publish sale prices after a transaction is completed.

The decision is a potential embarrassment for NSW fair trading and corrections minister, Anoulack Chanthivong, who has previously been overruled by Minns on rental and prison reforms.

The minister faces a preselection battle in his seat of Macquarie Fields, but Minns would not say on Monday if he would intervene on Chanthivong’s behalf.

As recently as 1 November, Chanthivong had praised the laws as “important reforms to put the hammer down on underquoting and stop homebuyers being taken for a ride”.

On Monday afternoon, a spokesperson for the Minns government provided a statement from Chanthivong, who said the laws had been passed “at a different time and in a different economic and housing market”.

“We introduced these laws to crack down on underquoting, protect homebuyers from misleading practices and ensure a fair and transparent property market for buyers and sellers,” he said.

“People are already doing it tough, and the last thing we want is for laws designed to protect homebuyers to inadvertently make it harder for them to buy a home.

“That’s why the government has made the decision not to proceed with the implementation of these changes today.”

It is the latest of several reversals in recent weeks by the government, under pressure ahead of the March state election from One Nation and the Coalition, with the cost of living and pressures on small businesses among the key issues.

Last month, the government dropped parts of plastics legislation which would have required cafes to offer customers who dine in the option of mugs and glasses instead of takeaway cups.

Polling conducted internally by the Labor party has suggested that One Nation could win more than 20 seats in the lower house. The party was polling at 26% of the primary vote in NSW according to a Sydney Morning Herald Resolve poll in October, behind Labor on 30%, but ahead of the Coalition on 23%.

One Nation announced four more candidates on its six-person upper house ticket on Sunday, but has so far only announced one lower house candidate, NSW party leader Mike Newman.

#80. They Defeated a Strongman. But Can They Run a Country?

Serbia’s student movement was deliberately leaderless and made group decisions to unseat Aleksandar Vucic. Now no one seems quite sure what happens next.

They had no leader, no political party and no conventional campaign. They made decisions by raising their hands after hours of debate in university classrooms, a slow and cumbersome process billed as a unique experiment in direct democracy.

That free-form approach to politics of Serbia’s students proved so effective that, in a general election on Oct. 25, they achieved what established opposition parties had failed to accomplish for more than a decade: routing the well-oiled political machine of Serbia’s strongman leader, Aleksandar Vucic.

Mr. Vucic, nearing the end of his second term as president and barred from running again, had resigned as president a month before the October vote for a new Parliament, gambling that his governing party would win and that he could keep his grip on power by becoming prime minister. He had held that post twice before.

His plan was upended by the election victory of a slate of parliamentary candidates selected by students in freewheeling campus meetings. Their selections and electoral successes marked the culmination of a striking exercise in collective decision making. That approach, some believe, could hold lessons for democratic movements beyond Serbia.

But the principles that carried the students to victory may not, on their own, be enough to build a functioning, coherent and durable government.

The diverse list of candidates chosen by the students, nearly all political novices with a wide range of sometimes contradictory views, reflected a belief that everyone should have a say in a leaderless movement united by the single objective of getting rid of Mr. Vucic and the system he created.

#79. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#76. Albanese treads tightrope in upcoming Trump meeting, wanting Indo-Pacific engagement and end to Iran and Ukraine wars

Quad leaders’ summit in Philippines is Trump’s first, as Australia joins US and Japan to protect AI technologies in face of China efforts to dominate

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

Anthony Albanese will attempt to walk a tightrope in his relationship with Donald Trump when the pair meets on Tuesday, arguing for a speedy resolution to the wars in Iran and Ukraine while urging the US to engage in the Indo-Pacific.

The Quad leaders’ summit in the Philippines’ capital of Manila will be Trump’s first, in either of his presidencies. The US president, fresh from a crushing midterms result, will take centre stage in gatherings of Asian leaders and prepare for historic three-way talks with Xi Jinping of China and Russia’s Vladimir Putin this week.

Underlying the diplomatic swing are efforts by the federal government to avoid angering China, as the Quad leaders gather for the first time since 2024 – and after Australia joined countries, including the US and Japan, in an agreement for new protections around key AI technologies.

Part of the Pax Silica international accord, officials said the agreement would help ensure Trump worked with partners to set standards in AI, rather than relying on others for leadership. Signatories include the UK, India, Israel, South Korea and Singapore.

“The Quad isn’t a military alliance, and Washington has gone out of its way to say it isn’t an anti-China coalition.”

Jennifer Parker, a non-resident fellow at the Lowy Institute, said criticism of Albanese’s involvement in the annual international summit season was shortsighted.

#74. Birth keeper told mother of baby who later died to ‘go home and rest’, NSW inquest hears

Sita Kali was involved in care for two of four babies who died between 2022 and 2024 in Lismore after free births or home births

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A self-described birth keeper advised a pregnant woman whose baby later died to “go home and rest” after she was given urgent medical advice to present to an emergency department, the New South Wales coroner’s court has heard.

Sita Tara Kali was involved in the care of two of four babies born in free births or home births who died in the northern rivers region between 2022 and 2024. An inquest into the deaths began in the Lismore local court on Monday. The families of the four babies were not present.

The inquest will examine whether the care was reasonable in the circumstances and whether care by an unregistered support worker was reasonable and appropriate before, during and after the birth.

Baby R’s mother had a distrust of hospitals in general, the court heard. She was aligned with and trusted Kali. She felt that having a home birth would give her a stronger connection to the baby. In a police statement she said she felt threatened by her experiences at the hospital.

At 43 weeks, an ultrasound confirmed that the baby was extremely compromised. The obstetric advice was urgent. She was urged to attend emergency and strongly advised against a home birth, the inquest heard.

She called Kali, who said in a police statement, “she was quite upset and anxious. I advised her to go home and rest. Calm yourself down and consider what you want to do.”

Baby R was born outside into his father’s hands at 9.51am. Two doulas were in attendance; neither had medical training. The baby was white in colour. He never latched on to feed.

About 14 hours later, the ambulance was called. The umbilical cord and placenta were still attached. Baby R died on the way to a waiting helicopter at 12.22am.

The cause of his death was determined to be unascertained. But an expert neonatologist surmised that it was caused by hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy resulting from severe post-maturity – being born at 43 weeks.

Prof Hannah Dahlen, a midwifery academic who has attended thousands of births, told the court that this was a baby “I would have been watching closely” as a midwife attending a birth.

She said the difference between a doula and a birthing midwife “is the theoretical knowledge that we add on to that clinical experience, which gives us an understanding and an ability to interpret signs and symptoms and to identify what pathology is going on, or what physiology is not actually working at this time and making a decision”.

The counsel assisting, Ragni Mathur, told the court women were choosing unregulated free birthing because of “traumatic or coercive experiences, fragmented care, lack of trust in clinicians, lack of available clinicians, lack of services in regional NSW, and including child protection notifications and regulatory actions against new lives”.

#73. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#71. How telecoms upgrade work sparked US flight chaos

Watch: US ground stop caused by issue during copper wire update, transport secretary says

An upgrade to a decades-old telecoms network in the US caused the longest nationwide grounding of flights since the 11 September 2001 attacks.

Friday's stoppage occurred when part of a key system that allows air traffic controllers and pilots to communicate was switched to fibre from copper wiring so old it has been discontinued, said US Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy on Saturday.

US planes were grounded for more than four hours, affecting thousands of flights and passengers.

Earlier Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said there was no indication of a cyber-attack and that everybody was safe.

On Friday afternoon, a government contractor called L3Harris was continuing work on upgrading an aviation telecoms network.

As it swapped out copper lines for fibre at a remote location in the US, L3Harris installed a router - a piece of equipment which basically directs traffic across the network.

According to Duffy, that router started flooding others in the air traffic control system, sending 1,000 connection attempts every four to five seconds.

As a result, 17 out of 22 air route traffic control centres in the US experienced "instability" on voice and ground communications, said Bryan Bedford, administrator of the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA).

It took the aviation authority 45 minutes to identify where the problem was because maintenance is logged manually on paper.

This meant the FAA had to "pore through" the physical documents "to figure out who's working on what", Bedford admitted.

As a precaution as it tried to track down the cause, Bedford said the decision was made to ground all flights.

Aviation analyst Miles O'Brien described the US system as "a house that's about to be condemned".

He told the BBC: "I think most of us could look at this and go 'this is a system in the United States that we have allowed to decay'. It is worse than a fixer-upper."

Duffy said the system needed a complete overhaul, estimating a $50bn price tag.

In the meantime, he said authorities would work to determine whether it was "incompetence" or faulty equipment that led to the outage.

He also said there would be "re-evaluation" of allowing upgrades to be done during the day.

In a statement on Saturday afternoon, L3Harris confirmed it was working with the FAA to modernise the airspace communications infrastructure and said it was supporting the agency with its investigation.

"L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travelers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously," the firm said.

Juliette Kayyem, US assistant secretary of Homeland Security under President Barack Obama, questioned why an upgrade was done on one of the week's busiest days for travel.

"You don't do it on a Friday when there's lots of air traffic controllers," she said

"You try to find ways to minimise its upgrade so you're not maybe doing it across the board in a single moment but you also have to have systems that are ready to step in if the upgrade in any way begins to threaten the system," Kayyem added.

A spokesperson for L3Harris said is it "supporting the FAA in its investigation to understand the conditions that led to the 13 November ground stop of our nation's air travel".

He added: "L3Harris takes the nationwide ground stop and its impact on travellers, airlines and air traffic operations seriously."

The ground stop led to more than 8,500 delayed and nearly 8,000 cancelled flights on Friday, according to FlightAware.

The FAA's initial announcement at 15:54 EST (20:54 GMT) said all flights departing within the US would be grounded until further notice.

More than 3,500 airplanes were in the air at the time. Some were diverted to other airports and others flew on to their destinations.

Hours later, some flights were allowed to take off from San Francisco International airport.

Have you been affected by the flight disruption? Get in touch

'We'll get home one day' - US travellers impacted by nationwide ground stop

All US-bound air traffic from Eurocontrol member states was suspended.

There are 42 Eurocontrol member states, including dozens of EU nations, the UK and Turkey, as well as Israel and Morocco.

Passengers travelling from the UK have been warned of possible disruption on Saturday morning.

A number of flights to the US from Heathrow airport were cancelled and delayed, but Manchester airport said no significant disruption was expected. Flights were operating as normal from Gatwick airport.

Australia's largest international airport, Sydney, had grounded all US-bound flights, but scheduled flights are now expected to depart as planned.

Air Canada also cancelled flights between the US and Canada.

Duffy said on Saturday: "We are back at full operations."

But, as he predicted on Friday, it is taking time to unwind what happened.

FlightAware said more than 5,900 flights were cancelled and more than 7,000 delayed in the US on Saturday.

Delta Air Lines and United Airlines said they were resuming operations but they both cancelled 20% of their flights on Saturday. American Airlines cancelled 520 journeys.

Meanwhile, JetBlue cancelled 40% of its scheduled journeys.

By Sunday morning, the situation had improved, but the impacts of the stoppage were still being felt with more than 2,400 flights delayed and 1,400 cancelled.

Luggage from cancelled flights at Ronald Reagan airport

In the US, UK and the EU, airlines may be liable for compensation if the reason for a delay is deemed within their control, such as a fault with the plane or pilot sickness.

As such, airlines do not have to provide compensation to passengers for air traffic control problems.

But passengers are still protected to a certain degree under US federal - and country-specific - laws.

Under the FAA Reauthorization Act of 2024, cancelled or "significantly delayed flights" - defined as three hours for domestic flights and six hours for international routes - must be offered alternative travel or a full refund of the fare if the passenger chooses not to fly.

It also requires all air carriers to issue refunds for tickets purchased with credit cards within seven business days of the request, and 20 days for tickets purchased with another form of payment. Passengers accepting a new flight cannot receive a refund.

In the US, there is no requirement for airlines to provide meal vouchers or accommodation, but some may offer complimentary support to customers.

However, if passengers are travelling from the UK to the US or flying with a UK carrier, you may have additional rights.

Under UK law, airlines have a duty to look after you when flights are delayed or cancelled for whatever reason.

a reasonable amount of food and drink (often through vouchers)

a way for you to communicate (often by refunding the cost of calls)

free accommodation, if you have to stay overnight to fly the next day

If your airline is unable to arrange assistance, you have the right to organise this yourself and claim back the cost later.

The Civil Aviation Authority advises people to keep receipts and not spend more than necessary.

It was only the second such nationwide stoppage since the immediate aftermath of the al-Qaeda plane hijackings of 11 September 2001.

"This looks like the worst since 9/11, but clearly not on that scale," Dr Guy Gratton, an associate professor of aviation at Cranfield University, told BBC Verify.

Gratton pointed to a US grounding in 2023 due to a failure in the Notice to Air Missions (Notam) system.

"That lasted 1 hour and 52 minutes, delayed about 9,500 flights and cancelled about 1,300," he said.

The 2023 outage meant air traffic safety notices could no longer be published and flights were grounded until that system was restored.

#70. South Korea to Examine How K-Pop Industry Treats Underage Performers

The announcement came after a New York Times investigation about the restrictive contracts of the group NewJeans, signed when its members were 14 to 17.

South Korea’s government is planning to study how the K-pop industry treats underage performers, who are central to the industry, and to re-examine the standard template for contracts it released nearly two decades ago.

The Ministry of Culture, Sports and Tourism will start an industrywide survey sometime next year, a ministry official said in a telephone interview on Monday. The government already studies the K-pop industry, checking whether record companies follow its standard contract and how much money is invested in training young stars. The new effort will focus specifically on how underage artists are treated, but its methodology, scope and exact timeline have not been decided on.

The announcement came just over a week after The New York Times published an investigation into the record contracts signed by the members of NewJeans, one of K-pop’s biggest girl groups, when they were ages 14 to 17. The investigation found that the contracts removed language included in the government template that guarantees rights for minors, such as the rights to education and sleep.

It was a rare look into the business of K-pop, which has long been dogged by allegations of exploitation. Record companies recruit children who are aspiring stars, put them through years of intensive training and then often bill them for those costs. Several stars have died by suicide.

Two years ago, NewJeans tried to quit their label in a dispute that turned into one of K-pop’s most public fights, with the members saying they had been mistreated. A court later held them to the contracts they had signed, which run until 2029.

The Seoul Broadcasting System reported the ministry’s plan earlier.

In 2009, after a boy band sued its label over restrictive work agreements, the authorities created a standard contract. It outlined protections such as capping agreements at seven years, taking into consideration an artist’s physical and mental well-being and transferring trademark rights to the artist after the terms end.

The template has been amended several times over the years but still largely remains legally nonbinding. Record companies often alter clauses and add their own conditions. Some do not use the template at all, a government survey found.

Jin Yu Young is a reporter for The Times, based in Seoul, covering South Korea and international breaking news.

Sarah Hurtes is a Times reporter working on international investigations from Brussels.

#68. Proposed NSW laws to allow coalmine to increase production pose ‘serious corruption risk’

Bill to authorise expansion at Mount Pleasant coalmine, subject to a landmark high court decision, will be debated this week

Get our daily political email, free app or daily news podcast

A New South Wales government bill to increase coal production at a Hunter Valley coalmine creates a “serious corruption risk” for the state due to a clause that would give the resources minister “king-like” powers, the barrister and anti-corruption campaigner Geoffrey Watson says.

The state parliament will this week debate legislation to allow MACH Energy to increase coal production at the Mount Pleasant mine in Muswellbrook by 32% to 16.5m tonnes per year.

The Minns government announced the bill last month after the high court ruled that an expansion of the mine was invalidly approved. The government has said the legislation is necessary to prevent 400 job losses at the site before Christmas.

Watson, the Greens and some members of the crossbench have raised concerns that a clause in the bill would give the resources minister of the day “unusually broad” powers to override other state laws.

Known as a Henry VIII clause, it would allow the minister to make regulations that could modify laws of the state as they apply to the Mount Pleasant development.

Henry VIII provisions are sometimes used by parliaments and draw their name from King Henry VIII and his style of governing by royal proclamation.

Watson is a director of the Centre for Public Integrity and Grata Fund and a former counsel assisting the NSW Independent Commission Against Corruption (Icac).

In a legal analysis of the bill, he raised concerns that Clause 17 “explicitly places” wide powers to “make new laws (or to repeal or modify existing laws) in the hands of one person – the mining minister – and for a single coal mine owner”.

“There are real problems with this as it is known that the concentration of this kind of power in the hands of one person creates a serious corruption risk,” he wrote.

He said the risk was “heightened” because the Mount Pleasant bill had been created for a single private company. He wrote that while he imputed “no bad faith at all to the current mining minister”, past experience in the state “has shown that not all ministers have acted in a manner free from improper motives”.

“Historically, we’ve seen this concentration of power into the hands of a single decision maker as posing a serious risk of corruption,” Watson told Guardian Australia.

“Now, corruption in that sense may not mean money passing hands. It also includes undue influence, decisions made in [circumstances] other than the public interest.”

Elaine Johnson is the director of Johnson Legal, which represented the Denman Aberdeen Muswellbrook Scone Healthy Environment Group (Dams Heg) in the high court proceedings. In a separate legal opinion, she raised similar concerns there was “a very obvious corruption risk arising from Clause 17”.

She wrote that the clause gave the resources minister of the day “unfettered power to make regulations to change the application of any law of the state” to the mine, and said it could potentially affect many laws that applied to the mine, such as those that regulate noise and dust or worker safety.

Sign up for Guardian Australia’s Politics, really newsletter here

The lower house is expected to debate the legislation on Tuesday, followed by the upper house later this week. The Minns government is hoping to pass the bill in what is likely to be the final sitting week before the March 2027 election.

Both Labor and Coalition lower house seats are under pressure from One Nation in the Hunter, where the mine is located.

One Nation’s NSW leader and candidate for Cessnock, Mike Newman, has described the Minns government’s bill to increase production limits at Mount Pleasant as a “Band-Aid on a bullet-wound”.

The Coalition resources spokesperson and Nationals MP for Upper Hunter, Dave Layzell, said the opposition would not oppose Labor’s legislation “in any way”. Asked about the Henry VIII clause, he said the Coalition would follow advice on the bill from the parliament’s legislation review committee which is expected on Tuesday.

Our political team brings you up to speed on the stories that really matter and what they mean for you

“We recognise [Labor’s] argument about the need to protect the number of jobs they are quoting.

“We agree that it is important that we fix the situation with the Mount Pleasant Mine extension.

“We cannot allow green environmental lawfare to continue to delay projects that have the support of the majority of the community, and are approved on a technical basis by the planning system.”

The Greens climate spokesperson Sue Higginson said it was “the broadest” Henry VIII clause she had seen during the current parliament and the party would seek to remove it from the bill or limit its scope.

“Placing that much power in the hands of a single minister is a corruption risk the Labor government should know well,” she said.

“A tightly limited Henry VIII provision can be necessary to give effect to parliament’s decision over time, as with the recent Great Koala National Park bill, where it allowed more land to be permanently protected in the public interest as it becomes available.”

A spokesperson for the resources minister, Courtney Houssos, said the government had been clear that the purpose of its bill was to protect all permanent workers at Mount Pleasant. They said the bill had been presented transparently to parliament.

“We welcome constructive engagement on the Bill from crossbenchers and will engage with good-faith amendments as we always do,” they said.

“Any proposed regulation would be submitted to the Executive Council for approval and published transparently in the usual manner, and also subject to disallowance in the usual manner.”

They added that Henry VIII provisions had been “routinely” used in legislation for decades, including in bills the Greens had supported in this term of parliament.

The independent MP Alex Greenwich, who intends to oppose the bill as a whole, said the provision was “dangerously broad and quite unprecedented”.

“It’s especially concerning, given parliament won’t sit for six months, meaning regulations issued during that time won’t be able to be disallowed,” he said.
