#66. My mother told me I would die if I sought treatment for Ebola - I am proof you can survive

When Dr Raymond Kasereka Kamwanga told his family he suspected he had Ebola and was going to a treatment centre in the city of Butembo in eastern Democratic Republic of Congo, his family were horrified.

They regarded the specialised unit as a "death house" as not many patients do recover - especially those admitted in the late stages of the illness. His 50-year-old mother was in tears, pleading with him not to go there.

"Why are you choosing to die at the Ebola treatment centre?" Masika Ndungo had asked him.

But as a doctor at Butembo's Katwa Hospital, he knew that if stayed at home, he was unlikely to survive.

This was in July, two months after the World Health Organization (WHO) had declared the Ebola outbreak in DR Congo a public health emergency of international concern. Since then 5,400 people have died and 11,000 have been infected, making it the second-most deadly outbreak ever recorded.

Butembo is in North Kivu province, the epicentre of the crisis, with the highest number of reported cases, according to the Congolese health ministry.

Dr Kamwanga is one of 3,031 people who have survived - and is evangelical about sharing his story in an effort to help stop the spread of infections devastating his community.

After staring death in the face and overcoming ostracisation, he has even started writing a book about his experience.

"As someone who has recovered, I am living proof that the disease exists, but also that it is possible to recover from it," he tells the BBC outside Katwa Hospital where he still works.

The 30-year-old medic believes he contracted the virus through contact with a patient in a setting where protective measures are not always adequate.

Raymond Kasereka Kamwanga uses his book to help convince sceptics that Ebola is real

The first symptoms appeared gradually: a bitter taste in his mouth, headaches and a general feeling of malaise. He contacted the Ebola response team.

After an initial consultation and some tests, he returned home, but his condition did not improve.

Butembo does not have the equipment to quickly test for Ebola - samples need to be sent to the city of Beni, 54km (33 miles) north.

This delays diagnosis as the condition of roads in this war-torn and underdeveloped part of DR Congo means it can take between 48 and 72 hours to travel even this relatively short distance.

After three days at home, Kamwanga felt extremely weak and experienced muscle pain. On the fourth day, he began vomiting and had diarrhoea.

This is when he gathered his family members - including his mother - to tell them he had to go to the Ebola treatment centre.

"She had started crying, along with my sisters. I told them everything would be alright, that I was a doctor and that I was confident. But they weren't reassured," he says.

Around 18:00, he set off on foot to the nearby centre but refused to use the main entrance.

He called his colleagues and asked them to put on their protective gear before they approached him and isolated him. The virus spreads from one person to another by contact with infected bodily fluids.

Dr Kamwanga's mother says his experience has helped dispel some of the misinformation she had heard about Ebola

The current outbreak is caused by the Bundibugyo species of the virus for which there is no approved vaccine or treatment.

"The treatment given to Ebola patients is symptomatic - meaning that doctors treat the symptoms the patient presents," says Kamwanga, explaining that in his case it was severe vomiting and diarrhoea.

In some patients these symptoms can lead to organ failure and then some develop internal and external bleeding.

Kamwanga lost a lot of fluid and struggled to stay hydrated - he was terrified.

"I was afraid for myself. Every day, patients were dying right before my eyes. But I stayed positive," he explains.

Over 13 days lying in his hospital bed, he tried to keep his spirits up by watching movies and TV shows on his phone, reading and scrolling through TikTok.

The day he learned he had recovered, Kamwanga felt immense relief. He thanked the healthcare workers who had cared for him.

However, even a certificate showing he was Ebola-free did not initially dispel the unease those around him felt after he was discharged.

What is Ebola and why is stopping the latest outbreak so difficult?

'I buried my parents one day after the other' - Ebola mourners learn how to grieve safely

"We welcomed him home warmly. But because of our fear, we didn't get close to him any more," Ndungo, his mother, tells the BBC.

For 21 days - the incubation period of the virus - the doctor lived separately in another part of the family home, which was crowded at the time with at least 18 people living there, including several children and people displaced by recent fighting in the region.

He would only see them from afar when he went to pick up his meal, while other family members kept their distance.

Neighbour Kavugho Sifa shared these concerns.

"We were really afraid, but we prayed to God to protect him at the treatment centre. With God's help and the doctors' efforts, we saw him return in good health," she tells the BBC.

They all agree the experience changed their perception of Ebola.

Some do not believe the disease even exists - Ndungo says that witnessing Ebola first-hand helped to dispel some of that misinformation.

After his recovery, Kamwanga threw himself into community outreach - sharing his story as a way of combatting rumours and mistrust.

"I started writing my book - The Price of Saving Lives - one week after recovering and leaving the hospital," he says. "However, it is not finished yet. What I currently use during my awareness-raising activities is the draft, and it is already making a significant impact."

During these sessions, he also distributes photos taken while he was sick to help convince residents that Ebola is real.

But in Butembo, this mission remains difficult. Kamwanga explains that some people view awareness campaigns as a way for humanitarian organisations to profit from donor funding.

Such mistrust, which is also levelled at healthcare professionals, is often fuelled by poverty and trauma related to years of armed conflict, he says.

"Ebola has come alongside the war," he says, explaining that some even interpret it as a way for those in authority to threaten a vulnerable population "to finish off those whom the war failed to exterminate".

He also believes that the number of deaths observed during epidemics - compared to recoveries, which are sometimes less visible - reinforce fear and suspicion.

But putting himself on the front line also comes with risks - and Kamwanga says he receives death threats on his phone.

Some residents accuse him of being paid to spread information about Ebola. He also reports that outreach workers have been attacked by members of the community.

He feels the authorities do not work effectively to address all these problems: "Our government is taking things lightly."

He agrees too with the recent report by MÃ©decins Sans FrontiÃ¨res (MSF) that Ebola funds are going missing.

"The money allocated to the Ebola response is being misappropriated by the government. We do not have enough protective equipment," the medic says.

The BBC has asked the Congolese government for comment.

Despite the intimidation and the obstacles he and other medics face, he remains determined - his goal is to help put an end to the disease.

"Should we give up the fight against Ebola? No. We continue to do what we can with the resources available to us, which are unfortunately very limited."

#64. Megaspeed, the company linked to the downfall of Nvidia boss, says it’s a ‘legitimate and lawful business’

Company insists Alice Huang, who is accused of being a Chinese spy, left business in late 2025

Elon Musk joins 165,000 others calling for Nvidia to bring back Jensen Huang

Megaspeed International, the company linked to the downfall of the Nvidia chief, Jensen Huang, says it is a “legitimate and lawful business” and has announced that its director, who allegedly had an affair with Jensen Huang, left the business in late 2025.

The Singapore-based cloud computing company on the weekend said it was “aware of recent media reports containing assertions about our business, supply chain and global operations that are not supported by verifiable evidence”.

Megaspeed said it had “retained external legal counsel to conduct a thorough internal investigation”.

Jensen Huang resigned as chief executive of the chip manufacturer Nvidia after reports of extramarital affairs with Alice Huang and two company employees.

US intelligence agencies believe the tech chief, whose products have been crucial to the AI boom, had been targeted by Alice Huang, the former director of Megaspeed International.

US officials have alleged Alice Huang may have been acting as a Chinese intelligence asset, passing commercial information to Beijing, the New York Times has reported.

In the past three years, Megaspeed has been one of Nvidia’s biggest foreign customers, buying nearly US$5bn (A$7.4bn, £3.8bn) in AI chips and advanced hardware.

A US senator asked the commerce department to investigate whether Megaspeed had diverted Nvidia chips to China.

Megaspeed said on Sunday it would not comment on Alice Huang’s “personal and private matters”.

“Alice Huang officially left Megaspeed effective August 30, 2025 and currently has no role with the company,” it said in a statement.

The global facades of Megaspeed International form a puzzling corporate picture, and involve a lottery retailer in rural Australia, a dilapidated storefront in Kuala Lumpur and a near-empty office in Singapore.

The company on Sunday said: “Megaspeed designs and maintains its global operational and supply-chain control frameworks to comply with all applicable laws and regulatory regimes, including United States export control requirements and local jurisdiction rules in every market where we operate.”

Short seller Culper Research in May quoted a Megaspeed employee as saying Alice Huang was “still our boss”, in a report accusing Nvidia of having a “China problem”.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

On Thursday, Singaporean authorities said Alice Huang had been barred from re-entering the city state after a 2025 investigation into the company’s “suspected breaches of our domestic laws”.

The ministry of home affairs said it had held Alice Huang’s passport from June 2025 until February. Police believed she had given them “inaccurate” information, to downplay her role at the company, but released her due to insufficient evidence.

She was issued a stern warning and barred from re-entering the country. Taiwan has also barred her from re-entry, its authorities have said.

Singapore’s initial investigation related to Megaspeed’s potential “false declarations” over the purchase of computer servers.

On Friday, Malaysia said it was investigating a subsidiary of Megaspeed International over the possible diversion of advanced AI chips to other countries.

Megaspeed has previously said there was no evidence of illegal chip transfers.

Its registered Sydney office is home to numerous business and had no Megaspeed signage, mailbox or physical presence when the Guardian visited last week. No one had heard of Megaspeed Australia or its listed director, 29-year-old Kai Kang.

Reuters visited a news agency and lottery retailer, in Seymour, Victoria, an hour’s drive north of Melbourne, where a woman, who identified herself as Kang’s mother, said Kang was based in Shanghai and did not know anything about the Singaporean company.

Kang has not been accused of any wrongdoing. Attempts to contact him were unsuccessful.

The US attorney general, Todd Blanche, said last week that the Justice Department had not concluded whether Alice Huang was a Chinese spy.

Asked whether she was a spy, the US president, Donald Trump, said: “I don’t know if that’s been determined yet.”

Beijing rejected the allegations as unsubstantiated. Associated Press was unable to reach Alice Huang for comment. The Guardian has sought comment from Alice Huang.

Megaspeed began as the overseas arm of a Chinese video game developer and was incorporated in Singapore in 2015 as 7Road International. It adopted its current name in March 2023, when all its shares were transferred to Huang Le Limited, a British Virgin Islands company. Alice Huang is also known by her Chinese name, Huang Le.

By November 2023, she was no longer listed as a Megaspeed director, and all her shares had been transferred to an entity owned by Singaporean businessman Tan Yong Pong, also known as James Tan.

The company’s finances have also attracted attention. Singapore corporate records show its total assets rose roughly 90-fold from end-2023 to 2024, reaching almost US$3bn (A$4.4bn, £2.3bn).

#62. Canada could work with Chinese automakers if US trade talks fail, says Carney

Ahead of Apec summit, PM says ‘Canada is going to move forward either way’ when asked if Chinese investment was leverage in US negotiations

The prime minister of Canada, Mark Carney, said the country could turn to Chinese automakers if trade negotiations with the US president, Donald Trump, failed to preserve an integrated North American auto industry.

Carney’s comments in Cambodia on Monday came ahead of a planned meeting with Chinese leader Xi Jinping this week in Shenzhen, China, where auto manufacturing is expected to be discussed. The two leaders reached an agreement in January allowing tens of thousands of Chinese electric vehicles into Canada at reduced tariffs.

“We have the best auto workers in the world, and we want them fully deployed, full opportunity, in an integrated North American market,” Carney told reporters in Cambodia. “That market has become less integrated because of US trade actions.”

He said on Monday that negotiations with the US remained his priority, but their outcome could determine whether Canada pursues alternatives involving European, Chinese or Japanese automakers. A day earlier in Manila, in the Philippines, Carney said any trade agreement with Washington must leave Canada with a viable auto industry.

Asked whether potential Chinese investment was leverage in US negotiations, Carney replied: “It’s not leverage. But action, reaction.

“If they [Canada’s auto workers] are not fully utilized in a North American context, that provides other opportunities,” Carney said. “Canada is going to move forward either way.”

The Detroit Three – Ford, General Motors and Stellantis – operate factories across Canada, the US and Mexico, with parts sometimes crossing borders multiple times before a vehicle is completed. Trump has repeatedly pushed automakers to move production to the US from Canada.

Carney said any Chinese investment would have to involve substantial manufacturing in Canada rather than simply assembling imported parts. He said it would need proper labor standards, manufacturing facilities and domestic supply chains that expand over time, including Canadian critical minerals used in electric vehicles.

“It’s not just about kit cars,” he said. “There has to be a manufacturing aspect involved.”

Under a January agreement, Canada reduced its 100% tariff on Chinese electric vehicles to 6.1%, initially allowing up to 49,000 vehicles annually, with the cap increasing over time. In exchange, China agreed to lower tariffs on Canadian agricultural products, including canola.

Carney said at the time that he expected the agreement to attract Chinese investment in Canada’s auto industry and create jobs.

The deal drew criticism from Ontario premier Doug Ford, whose province is the center of Canada’s auto industry. Ford warned that allowing Chinese electric vehicles into Canada could threaten Canadian jobs and jeopardize access to the US market.

Trump’s tariffs have disrupted Canada’s auto industry, which relies heavily on cross-border production. Canada recently threatened to sue Stellantis after the automaker shifted planned Jeep Compass production from Brampton, Ontario, to Illinois and agreed to sell its idled Canadian assembly plant.

Carney said Saturday that he had spoken at length with Trump and senior US officials and that the two countries were making progress toward a possible trade agreement, although differences remained.

#61. Judo champion shakes up French presidential race with surprise candidacy

Five-times Olympic champion Teddy Riner is one of France's most popular athletes

Apart from an overwhelming urge to pin one's opponent to the floor of the house, there is no obvious connection between politics and the world of top-level judo.

That has not stopped France's much-garlanded world heavyweight champion Teddy Riner from throwing his hat into the ring for next April's presidential race.

The Guadeloupe-born 37-year-old may have no experience of public office, no party behind him, and as yet no manifesto. All so far is a slogan La France qu'on aime (The France we love), a declaration on social media, and a campaign visit to a hospital in the Seine-Saint-Denis suburb of northern Paris.

But it would be a foolhardy pundit who would totally write off his chances.

Riner is, after all, one of the most popular figures in modern-day France, regularly topping the polls for embodying the nation's "values". He is a proven winner, with five Olympic golds and 11 world championships under his belt, as well as a successful career in business.

More than 28 million people have watched his election broadcast on Instagram and X, shot with proper presidential decorum against a backdrop of books and a French flag. Some 22% told an Ifop poll within 24 hours they "could" vote for him.

Teddy Riner's video has attracted 14.5 million views on Instagram alone

Actors and television personalities have become presidents in other countries. The ex-leader of Liberia â George Weah â was formerly a professional footballer.

So why not a judoka in the ÃlysÃ©e Palace?

"Anyone who thinks that being out of the political in-crowd disqualifies Riner from running for the ÃlysÃ©e would be very wrong," says Guillaume Tabard of Le Figaro.

"But [Riner] too would be wrong if he thinks not belonging to the in-crowd is all it takes."

For now the whole of the French political world has decided to take the Riner candidacy seriously â or at least to pretend to. That is partly because he is such a popular personality that mocking his political hopes could easily rebound badly.

But it is also because the state of French politics â outside of the burgeoning forces of the hard left and hard right â is one of total disarray.

The centre-ground is floundering, with no candidate yet emerging who can inspire anything like the sense of mission of hard-right leader Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc MÃ©lenchon on the hard left, who for now look set to face off in the final round of the presidential election in May.

So when a popular figure like Riner enters the race - proclaiming himself "neither left, nor right", wanting to "unite the country" and "put money back in people's pockets", railing equally against "racists" and "yobs who scare people off our streets" â politicians have to take note.

Hard-right leader Marine Le Pen currently leads the polls in the race to be France's next president

It is perfectly possible, probable even, that Riner's campaign will fizzle out after a brief period of hype, and that the judoka will after all be a joker in the pack. But it is also possible that it won't, in which case the pre-election arithmetic will need a hasty readjustment: and it won't be the centrists who are smiling.

Thus far, Riner has shown himself a master of the codes required for this kind of out-of-nowhere candidacy, the first of which is to press home the message: "Don't Worry! I'm NOT a Politician!"

In this day and age in France, anyone who smacks of the "system" is automatically downgraded in voters' eyes, so it is important to emphasise that you are only running for office because, one, you have experienced real life (unlike those loafers in Paris); and two, you have discovered that there are solutions out there for the country's many problems. Solutions that are invisible to those same blinkered apparatchiks.

All this Riner was able to express in last Monday's social media broadcast by using sophisticated rhetorical devices.

"People say I am a fool for wanting to run for the presidency," he said.

"Am I a fool? Well, if refusing to see France sink under the weight of debt; refusing to see mothers and fathers grit their teeth every time they do their weekly shop, and tremble with fear when their kids go out on the streetâ¦ if all that means being a fool, then yes call me a fool."

It was a promising oratorial start for the sportsman, and certainly attracted the viewers.

Are student protests being manipulated ahead of France's presidential election?

France's Marine Le Pen, fighting court conviction and running for presidency

But if he is serious â and Riner says he never does anything half-heartedly - that's not how he became an Olympic champion â then the hard work begins now.

What does Riner actually want to do? Who will be on his team? Where does he position himself on the all-consuming matters of the day: pension-reform, investment in education, the cost of living, public debt, the rise of populism or Russia's war in Ukraine?

In short, how does he intend to transform his undeniable popularity as a sporting figure into a political mandate?

Protesters have for weeks taken to the streets in France over wages, the cost of living and public services

Broadly, Riner appears to be on the right on economic matters and law and order, but being black and of Caribbean background he is also very strong on racism. When he says he is anti-extremism, he is referring mainly to Marine Le Pen.

In other words his pitch is somewhere in the centre, and against the extremes.

The trouble is, this is already an extremely crowded area, with important figures like ex-prime ministers Edouard Philippe and Gabriel Attal signally failing to take off.

Currently there are a dozen or so candidates in the area between centre right and centre left, stretching from the Socialist-backed RaphaÃ«l Glucksman to the Republicans' Bruno Retailleau on the right.

The latest possible addition is Christine Lagarde, who steps down as head of the European Central Bank next February.

President Emmanuel Macron is among the few politicians who appears genuinely pleased by Riner's decision to run

The addition of yet another contender will dilute their polling numbers even further, making a second-round qualification for MÃ©lenchon more likely.

So no wonder the leader of France Unbowed (LFI) seemed so happy about Riner's decision to run. "We know how it goes: neither right nor left means not left," MÃ©lenchon told reporters on Friday.

The only other politician who seems genuinely pleased, rather than putting on a fixed smile, is President Emmanuel Macron, who said Riner was a "great champion" and that politics "did not belong to the professionals".

That could be explained by the president's cool relations with his former prime ministers, Philippe and Attal, both of whom are doing all they can to distance themselves from their unpopular one-time boss.

Riner said he viewed Macron's praise as a "poisoned chalice", telling reporters on Saturday that he was "not the president's envoy, I'm not a Macronist".

Politics and judo have very little in common, but of the two, as Riner will soon discover, the more vicious is not the one that is fought out hand-to-hand after mutual acknowledgement on the tatami mat.

In Japanese, judo actually means "the gentle way".

If he starts looking like a serious candidate, Riner must know his life will be turned upside down, not just his physical form.

In politics you do not bow in respect to your opponent. You give him a metaphorical headbutt.

#58. Trump’s Asia Trip Will Bring Him to the Table With Xi and Putin: What to Know

President Trump will also meet with regional leaders, including Australia’s prime minister, who said “we want to see a de-escalation in the conflicts.”

President Trump sets off this week for meetings in Asia that will culminate in a summit with two strongmen he has often praised: President Xi Jinping of China and President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia.

It will be the first time the three men, who command the world’s largest nuclear arsenals, will have sat down together. Mr. Trump plans to discuss the reduction of nuclear weapons in the world and the war against Iran, according to a White House spokeswoman. He is not expected to bring up Russia’s war with Ukraine.

Mr. Trump will travel first to Manila, then to southern China, and will meet with regional leaders confronting an array of uncertainties. Those include the course of the war between the United States and Iran, high energy prices and mounting concern over the risks of artificial intelligence.

For Mr. Trump, the trip will be a pivot to foreign policy and relationships after American voters delivered a swift rebuke to his party in the midterm elections. His trip also comes as his administration faces scrutiny over a scandal involving the former chief executive of Nvidia, the tech giant, which has raised questions about Chinese espionage and American tech exports.

The highlight of Mr. Trump’s trip is expected to be his meeting with Mr. Xi and Mr. Putin on Thursday in China.

The prospect of their summit has drawn comparisons to the 1945 conference in the Crimean city of Yalta, where the leaders of the United States, the Soviet Union and Britain met to decide the shape of postwar Europe.

#57. Town rebuilt after devastating floods becomes showcase for Libyan strongman's rule

Whole areas of Derna have been entirely rebuilt

"It was joy when I moved in. I could finally relax," says Abdulwahab Damdoum, surveying his luxurious new apartment overlooking the Mediterranean Sea, in the eastern Libyan city of Derna.

Just three years ago he was clinging in terror to the roof of a house as the world's deadliest flood this century surged around him.

Now the 27-year-old port worker, his wife and two young daughters have been given a new, free, 300-sq-m home in a showcase development commissioned by a son of Khalifa Haftar, the warlord who controls eastern Libya.

It is part of a rapid $2bn (Â£1.5bn) rebuilding of Derna that the powerful Haftar family hope will serve as a model for development throughout Libya, increasing their influence across a country currently split between east and west, with rival governments in charge.

The money comes from the state coffers of the oil-rich country, allocated by its parliament. But there are no published records of how it has been spent, raising questions about accountability and possible corruption.

Damdoum, like many flood survivors, simply thanks the head of the Development and Reconstruction Fund, Belkacem Haftar, for what has been achieved since the early hours of 11 September 2023, when lashing rain from Storm Daniel burst two dams above Derna, sending a wall of water 7m (23 ft) high cascading down onto the sleeping city.

The exact death toll will probably never be known. Some estimates have been as high as 6,000, though 4,000 now seems a more likely figure. Many bodies are still unidentified.

Abdulwahab Damdoum lost his father, brother and sister in the flood

In a city of about 100,000 people, that means almost everybody lost someone they loved. The 14 relatives and neighbours on the roof with Damdoum â including his father, brother and sister - all drowned.

He survived, but his home was destroyed, and he had to find temporary accommodation. "It was rent, rent, rent," he says. "Now, no-one can take this [new flat] from me. I thank Engineer Belkacem. God sent this person who picked the city up from disaster."

Portraits of Belkacem, widely known simply as "the Engineer", his younger brother Saddam, a military chief also involved in reconstruction, and their father Khalifa are on hoardings all over Derna and the region.

"Since 2011, Libya has not built big projects like this," says Hussein el-Mansory, project manager of the "2,000 Apartments" complex where Damdoum now lives. "I feel very happy and proud and I'll tell my children: 'I made this.'"

The complex includes a new mosque, hospital, shopping mall and schools. New roads, bridges have also been built in Derna. But while many survivors have been rehoused, some are still waiting.

Among them is Izz al-Nasr Ibzeou, a marine science lecturer at the university. His wife Salima, her mother Mouna, and all the couple's seven children, aged between one and 11, drowned.

His family home was ruined, but the authorities have not yet fully restored it because Ibzeou also has an undamaged apartment in another part of town.

He returns every day to his old house, now an empty shell, to see his children's possessions, which he has kept in dust-covered piles.

"This is my son Moez's bike. He played with it every day. And this is my daughter Fawzia's dress. She wore it whenever she went to a wedding," Ibzeou says. "Even if I decide not to come to remember them, my feet will bring me here of their own accord."

To this day, he has not found their bodies. In Derna's vast new flood cemetery, most graves are marked only by numbers, not names, because the process of matching survivors' DNA with corpses is still not completed.

"I go to the cemetery and try to guess where my family are," he says. "This has burned my heart too much. Thank God, I'm patient, but I'm still waiting just to read the Qur'an [at their graves] and say hello to them."

Izz al-Nasr Ibzeou visits his former home every day, to remember his children

Belkacem Haftar has now extended the work of his reconstruction fund to Libya's second city, Benghazi, and other parts of Libya his father controls.

Libya's parliament, based in the east, last year allocated him a further sum of nearly $11bn, to be spent over three years, in addition to the $2bn it had already given him specifically for Derna.

Building in Benghazi has included new skyscrapers, hospitals, housing developments and a refurbished football stadium that last year hosted a friendly between Inter Milan and AtlÃ©tico Madrid. Officials have talked in the past about turning the city into a version of "Dubai on the Mediterranean."

Restoring Derna - Libyaâs 'Jasmine City'

Tim Whewell reports on how Derna, famous for its fragrant jasmine flowers, has been rebuilt

If you're in the UK, you can watch Tim's film on Global Eye on BBC 2 on Monday at 19:00.

Outside the UK, you can watch on the BBC World Service YouTube channel, external from Tuesday.

Belkacem tells the BBC he has accomplished more than any previous Libyan administration.

"Libya went through the Gaddafi era, 40 years of neglect and poverty," he said. "The interim governments [post-Gaddafi, based in Tripoli] wasted a lot of money without providing anything the people could touch. I have never made promises to the people. But I have provided them with what they need."

International human rights organisations have repeatedly accused the authorities in eastern Libya and Libyan Arab Armed Forces headed by Khalifa Haftar of serious abuses, including secret executions, arbitrary detentions and forced disappearances.

The Haftars and the Benghazi-based government deny the allegations. Belkacem told the BBC there was complete freedom of speech in eastern Libya.

Posters of Khalifa Haftar and his sons are a common sight in Derna

Critics also say the Haftars have a top-down approach to development, with little public participation in planning decisions, and no transparency.

"There is absolutely zero accountability. We've never seen any financial statements of the Reconstruction Fund, for even the legal on-the-table disbursements of funds," says Claudia Gazzini from the International Crisis Group (ICG) think-tank.

Belkacem says the fund is independently audited and that all contracts are drawn up to high international standards. Dismissing concerns about transparency, he says: "What we've done in Derna was almost impossible. What we care about is the reality, what we do on the ground."

He agreed to show the BBC the audits. But he has not provided them, despite repeated requests.

Jalel Harchaoui, a Libya expert affiliated to the Royal United Services Institute think-tank in London, who has talked to some of the foreign partners awarded construction contracts. He says they are usually obliged to work alongside a private Libyan company linked indirectly to the Haftar family or other powerful political figures.

He says: "What those foreign companies say off the record, is that they do the overwhelming majority of the work and the Libyan partner is often almost an empty shell, in a context where the money is split very often on a 50-50 basis, which indicates that a lot of those facilities and bridges and towers are overcharged, twice as expensive.

"It's a form of overpricing within a context that is utterly opaque, and this is not some fairy coming from the sky that funds these reconstruction efforts. Everything is funded using the public wealth of Libya."

Belkacem Haftar denies the suggestion of corruption in reconstruction projects.

He has said the reconstruction funding was processed through the Central Bank of Libya, which operates on behalf of both the UN-recognised government in the capital Tripoli, in the west of the country, and the government in the eastern city of Benghazi.

"The Haftar authorities used Derna as a showcase for what they were pitching to the rest of Libya," says Gazzini from the ICG.

Belkacem Haftar, the son of strongman Khalifa Haftar, denies the suggestion of corruption in reconstruction projects

International efforts to reunite the country have included a proposal by Donald Trump's envoy, Massad Boulos, for a power-sharing deal with Saddam Haftar as Libya's president.

In Derna, there is no plan to rebuild the broken dams, so another disaster on the same scale is unlikely. But there is still a possibility of future flooding.

Gazzini says the authorities failed to take up offers of advice from international experts on ways to reduce the risk, for example by creating flood plains along the river which flows through the town.

"I would have expected a holistic project about how you manage the water flow," Gazzini says.

"But that did not happen. The Libyan side responded more to people's needs, was more interested in having a quick solution to the destruction of the city."

#56. Trump to embark on Asia trip in apparent foreign policy push before end of term

Experts say US president may lean toward global affairs after domestic influence curtailed in midterm elections

Donald Trump is embarking on his first overseas trip since his Republican party’s thumping midterm repudiation by US voters, in what may herald a renewed focus on foreign policy for the remainder of his presidency.

The US president will meet his Chinese and Russian counterparts, Xi Jinping and Vladimir Putin, for their first trilateral summit, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) forum in the Chinese city of Shenzhen. Before that, he will land in Manila in the Philippines on Tuesday for the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (Asean) summit.

At the Asean summit, Trump is expected to sign a trade agreement with Thailand, push deals with other countries and lobby fellow leaders to favour the US over China on artificial intelligence.

He will also take part in the first leaders’ meeting of the Quad partnership – consisting of the US, Japan, India and Australia – since he returned to the White House in January 2025.

White House officials said the president would be accompanied to Apec by Elon Musk, the Tesla and SpaceX entrepreneur who spent almost $500m (£380m) unsuccessfully supporting Republican candidates in this month’s midterms. The reason for Musk’s presence has not been explained.

The China leg of the trip will take place amid the continuing fallout from the Nvidia spying scandal, which prompted the company’s chief executive, Jensen Huang, to resign amid revelations he had had an affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Trump is under pressure to raise the matter with Xi when they hold a bilateral meeting on the sidelines of Apec.

The US president has said he does not plan to bring up the subject; administration officials insist he will not do so and that the meeting will be dedicated to implementing a trade agreement between the two superpowers.

Huang persuaded the Trump administration to lift restrictions on the sale of Nvidia’s AI chips to China that had been imposed during Joe Biden’s presidency.

Mao Ning, a Chinese foreign ministry spokesperson, told reporters on Monday that at the trilateral summit, Xi, Trump and Putin “will exchange views in depth on safeguarding world peace and promoting strategic stability in major-power relations, which is of groundbreaking significance”.

Some analysts have characterised the meeting as a “big three” gathering echoing the 1945 Yalta meeting of Winston Churchill, Franklin D Roosevelt and Joseph Stalin that in effect carved up postwar Europe, between a communist-ruled east and a democratic, capitalist west.

Such a prospect has alarmed Ukraine, which fears that Putin – who initially suggested the trilateral meeting – will use it to persuade Trump and Xi to agree to a settlement of the war with Russia on Moscow’s terms.

However, Trump has said Ukraine will not be up for debate at the meeting and that its main purpose will be to discuss ending the US’s war with Iran and “denuclearisation” – shorthand for a deal among the three powers to limit nuclear arms.

That is believed to be unpalatable to China, which has been increasing its nuclear arsenal, and has signalled an unwillingness to enter into reduction agreements until it is closer to parity with the US and Russia.

Our US morning briefing breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

Analysts say the main beneficiary of the three-way meeting is likely to be Putin, whose standing will be boosted by being treated on an equal footing by two other leaders whose countries are, in reality, much more powerful.

Expectations of a substantial agreement are low.

Lyle Goldstein, an international affairs fellow and China specialist at Brown University in the US, said the Trump-Xi-Putin meeting would not have a substantive result. “There will be no ‘Yalta’, as many have feared,” he said.

Jonathan Czin, the Michael Armacost chair in foreign policy studies at the Brookings Institution, said Trump’s lame duck status would deter Xi and Putin from trying to reach long-term arrangements with the US.

“Both men know Trump’s time is expiring even more quickly than his leverage – and that he will be irrelevant in a mere 26 months [when his term expires],” Czin told an online Brookings forum. “Why would either man make a deal with a leader whose time and leverage are waning? They will only do it if they think they can pilfer more from Trump diplomatically than they will be able to gain from the next American president.”

Nevertheless, Goldstein – who took soundings on Chinese expectations for the summit in a visit to Shenzhen last week – predicted Trump would follow the pattern of previous presidents by turning increasingly to global affairs while he faces the prospect of his influence on the domestic front being curtailed after the Democrats’ victories in the House of Representatives and the Senate.

“The president has been badly damaged by the election and will have limited prospects for the next two years,” he said. “That may cause him to be more active in foreign policy since he can act in that domain with fewer constraints. It would not surprise me if Trump is ready to go all-out to make some further splash in foreign policy.”

With the risks of reigniting the war with Iran high and the Russia-Ukraine war still resistant to a diplomatic resolution despite White House efforts, Goldstein suggested Trump could attempt “a dramatic foreign policy flourish in Asia”, such as an agreement with Kim Jong-un, the North Korean dictator with whom he has frequently boasted of having a close relationship. Kim is not expected to attend the Apec gathering, although there have been rumours he may appear.

#53. Russia's mega-rich are already imagining a future after Putin

Russia's mega-rich are already imagining a future after Putin

Hours after launching his full-scale invasion of Ukraine on 24 February 2022, Vladimir Putin gathered Russia's richest men in the Kremlin's cavernous Yekaterininsky Hall.

More than three dozen of them sat huddled along a wall, wearing face masks because of the Covid pandemic. One Russian newspaper reporter described them as "pale and sleep-deprived". On entering, Putin sat down at the opposite side of the hall, creating a vast space between him and the businessmen. As usual, he was wearing no mask.

Using his typically coded language, he said he expected them to support the war.

"I would like to call on you to show understanding of what is going on," he said, "and work together with the government to find the tools which would support production, the economy, jobs."

In the Kremlin-published video of the meeting, none of the invited billionaires said a word.

That silence has become a feature of the Russian super-rich. Over the decades of Putin's rule, they have learnt that the price of piping up could be high. In 2003, oil magnate Mikhail Khodorkovsky, Russia's richest man at the time, who was funding a number of opposition groups, accused Putin of presiding over rampant corruption. He spent the next 10 years behind bars, convicted of tax evasion, fraud and embezzlement - a warning to other would-be critics. He denied the charges.

The silence of Russian businessmen at a Kremlin summit with Putin in 2022 perhaps demonstrated their fear of the president

And even if any of the tycoons objected to the war, they have tended to keep their thoughts to themselves.

But even in that silence, there are murmurings: some members of Russia's once-mighty business elite seem to be imagining a Russia after Putin. They are wondering if, once the president has left office or died, the country might look more like it did in the 1990s, when the oligarchs enjoyed the fruits of power - or even if a healthier politics may take root.

The journalist Arkady Ostrovsky, a prominent Kremlin-watcher at The Economist newspaper who recently spent hours interviewing one of Russia's richest men, says he feels that a "historic, political shift is happening" in terms of how the ultra-rich view the future of the country.

The collapse of the Soviet Union in 1991 gave birth to a new, powerful force on the domestic scene: people who took advantage of the end of communism and became extraordinarily rich, and then wanted to control politicians for their own benefit. They were dubbed "the oligarchs", from Ancient Greek for the "few who rule".â¯ They had money, which back then meant power.

This era of the "wild 1990s" was when the so-called "new Russians" burst onto the scene. Huge state companies came up for grabs, and men with the right connections became millionaires - even billionaires - almost overnight. Corruption was rife, cronyism rampant, and contract killings frequent.

But almost immediately after Putin was first elected as president in 2000, he subjugated the oligarchs. He had little interest in sharing power with anyone else. He could afford to stand up to the billionaires because Russia's economy was getting back on its feet, and the central government was getting stronger.

Some oligarchs developed a distinctive dress sense in the 1990s, as later satirised by this St Petersburg artist

Bill Browder, a financier who used to be the biggest foreign investor in Russia, and was banned from the country in 2005 after campaigning against corruption, says the Russian tycoons cowered and wilted in front of Putin - for example at the Davos economic forum, in the pre-war years when they were still welcomed there.

"I can remember seeing oligarchs walking around like they own the place, the most arrogant and self-confident individuals. And then Putin showed up, and it was just remarkable to see how these supposedly alpha-male-on-steroids individuals turned into absolutely subordinate, almost desperate schoolchildren," he tells the BBC.

Since the outbreak of war in 2022, the Kremlin has kept Russia's rich businessmen in line with a ruthless policy of carrot and stick. Critics of the war have seen their assets seized, while loyalists have been rewarded with lavish government war contracts: today there are more Russian billionaires than ever (155 in total, according to Forbes magazine). â¯Their collective wealth has reached a new high of nearly $700bn (Â£525bn).

But even for those tycoons who have stayed loyal and been rewarded, all of that money doesn't seem to be making them happy. Some are growing restless.

Their billions do not go as far as they used to: domestically, they cannot buy much political influence.

"They have no cards, they have no game," exiled politician and analyst Dmitry Nekrasov says. "They're rich because they've got something to do with Putin, not the other way round."

And outside of Russia, they are ostracised because of the Western sanctions imposed on Russia after its full-scale invasion.

"They're treated like low-level criminals. They can't fly their private jets. Their yachts can't park in all the best harbours in Europe. It must be very awful and humiliating for them," says Browder, the avowed Putin critic.

And so, quietly, some of their minds are turning to what happens after Putin leaves the scene. That's what I'm told by well-placed analysts, who are immersed in the world of these tycoons.

There's no immediate prospect of the president's departure, and there's no reason to think he's in bad health. But Putin, who turned 74 last month, has served in the Kremlin for a quarter of a century; people in Moscow know that his exit will come one day. When asked by Reuters last year if he intends to serve until 2036, when his constitutional term technically ends, Putin replied: "Only God knows if we have enough health."

One prominent oligarch of the 1990s was Boris Berezovsky, who fell out with the Kremlin and lived his later years in the UK

If Putin resigned or died in office, the prime minister, Mikhail Mishustin, would take over. But Mishustin has not exhibited any presidential ambitions (that's probably a key reason he was appointed). That would create something of a power vacuum.

In that scenario, Russia-watchers I have spoken with envisage a few potential scenarios.

Some in the West, and no doubt within Russia, hope that democracy might flourish in a post-Putin Russia, with government led by the people, rather than by a new cadre of oligarchs or a single autocrat. But this seems like the least likely path, analysts say. Key democratic institutions have been eroded or destroyed over Putin's rule. "Russia is so used to violence, it has grown unaccustomed to standard democratic procedures," says Alexandra Prokopenko, an exiled Russian journalist and analyst.

Another path is more autocracy. A different figure could replace Putin and continue his culture of repression. But Prokopenko thinks this wouldn't be another strongman dictatorship - at least not right away. These things take time, she says: at first, Putin's successor would be weaker than him, with fewer resources.

And then there's the third option: tycoons re-assert their power and Russia returns to something resembling a 1990s-style system of oligarchy. Dmitry Nekrasov, a former Kremlin adviser and opposition politician who now lives in exile, says under this scenario, the country would be governed by a handful of powerful elite clans in alliance with business interests.

This last option, of course, is the one that many tycoons want. "They'd like to return to a time when they were listened to, when they were not stripped of assets and when they understood the rules of the game," says Prokopenko, who has spent a lot of time speaking to Russia's richest people.

Though their power has been seriously curtailed since the 1990s, it's still worth listening to the tycoons: given their immense wealth, it seems likely that sooner or later they will try to re-assert their political influence.

Already, some are "trying to be friends with the right sort of security officials or ministers", says Nekrasov, the exiled politician. But he says any further steps are "too dangerous to take".

Putin has kept tycoons in line with a ruthless policy of carrot and stick since Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine

He says the behaviour of the tycoons in coming years will depend in part on their finances. Those who have lots of liquid cash will be more confident to "play political games" and jockey for position, preparing for a post-Putin country. But those whose wealth is dependent on state contracts or government goodwill will feel too afraid, in case they lose everything, he says.

Prokopenko says there's already been a shift in how the businessmen talk about Putin. They used to nickname him "Nachalnik" (which translates as "boss"), as well as "Perviy" ("first"), or by his initials "VV" - all nicknames that convey a degree of respect. Now they call him "Ded", with what Prokopenko calls "a pinch of contempt". "Ded" translates as "oldie" or "granddad" - a much less reverent name.

Some rich Russians are pining after a "return of politics into people's lives", she says, with less repression, more open speech, and healthier competition between parties and politicians.

But at the moment, she says, they are too afraid to make that happen due to Russia's stifling atmosphere. Prokopenko interviewed one tycoon who physically sat on his phone during the whole conversation, because he thought it would make digital eavesdropping more difficult for secret police. "I thought that it's an absolutely Stalinistic type of fear. It's absolutely irrational," she says.

An interesting clue for what tycoons might want from a post-Putin future came from Andrey Melnichenko, who is known as the world's fertiliser king and is currently Russia's eighth richest man.

Earlier this year, he surprised observers by giving an interview to The Economist - a rare thing for a rich Russian to do, especially to a publication from a country the Kremlin considers unfriendly. They also published an essay written by him.

In parts of the interview, Melnichenko repeated Kremlin talking points. He said that Ukraine is a battlefield between Russia and the West, and he thinks the West ignored Russia's security concerns and threatens its sovereignty.

But then it got more interesting. Melnichenko did not call for Putin to go, but - and it's a big but - he argued for a change that would bring about an end to one-man rule in Russia. He said Russia needs to engage more with the world.

Prokopenko thinks the tycoon was trying to stir a public conversation. It was a "call on rich people like him to start talking", she says.

The journalist who interviewed him, Arkady Ostrovsky, tells me the pair had "tense and heated discussions" in "hotels and cafes, mostly drinking coffee, lots of coffee, sometimes smoking". He says the tycoon seemed to be "genuinely thinking" about his answers, rather than giving politician-style spin.

Nothing in Russia is simple, and most observers think the interview probably happened with some form of approval - implicit or explicit - from the Kremlin. We don't know why authorities would have been happy to authorise the unusual call for change; it's possible they were trying to show that some degree of pluralism still exists in the country.

No tycoons came out to support him. Still, the journalist feels that Melnichenko was not speaking for himself alone, but also for his fellow businessmen.

Some tycoons feel ostracised because of Western sanctions, unable to fly private jets and visit Western resorts like Saint-Jean-Cap-Ferrat in southern France, which used to be popular with rich Russians

Some of them are hoping that after Putin's departure, the country might become an "oligarchic republic", as journalist Ostrovsky puts it - something like how it looked in the 1990s, with the business elites in charge.

Based on his years of reporting on Russia, Ostrovsky says that many of the country's mega-rich currently feel a "sense of complete dead end, which this war has led Russia toâ¦ [an] absence of the future".

But even if the tycoons are thinking about the future, there's a slim prospect of them acting any time soon, it seems.

Browder, the Putin critic, says that although many of them are unhappy, they're unlikely to conspire against Putin while he is still alive. "They understand that if they were to do anything that would challenge Putin, he would kill them. And Putin is a very paranoid little man."

Mike Calvey, an American financier who used to be a key investor in Russia, agrees. "What can they do besides just go straight to the prison and lose their assets? It's easy for people who are sitting in London to say that, 'why don't you just all go stand up and go straight to prison?' That's really what would happen."

Russia's richest men used to be able to pull strings at the highest levels of power. Once Putin is gone, there is no doubt they will try to claw back their past influence. The question is: will remnants of the current regime let them?

Spain and China are cosying up - to the anger of many in the EU

Europe's youth is on the march - but does anything actually unite them?

#52. Eurovision organisers criticise Malaysian removal of LGBT+ footage in first Asian contest

Broadcaster TV3 appears to cut footage of Thai drag performers and gay Philippine jury spokesperson

Eurovision’s organisers have said they are “surprised and disappointed” that the first Eurovision Asia was altered when it was broadcast in Malaysia, apparently removing LGBTQ+ imagery and people from the show.

The European Broadcasting Union (EBU), the alliance of public broadcasters that owns Eurovision, said it would write to Voxovation, the company producing the Asian contest under licence, and ask it to establish with the Malaysian broadcaster TV3 why it happened.

Footage circulating online of the contest, which was won by the Philippines’ Carmelle, showed the first of two cuts during the voting in Saturday’s final in Bangkok, when the Philippines’ spokesperson, Vice Ganda, awarded the Philippine jury’s points.

Vice Ganda is gay and identifies as non-binary. The comedian is among the biggest stars on Philippine television, has fronted the country’s most watched daytime show, and has campaigned for LGBTQ+ acceptance at home.

Announcing a national jury’s points is one of the most visible roles in a Eurovision final. Each country’s spokesperson appears in turn to reveal how their jury has ranked the other entries.

While the international feed showed Vice Ganda announcing that the Philippine jury had given its 12 points, the maximum, to the Malaysian entrant, Aina Abdul, TV3 viewers saw only the scoreboard.

Separate footage shows part of the show’s opening number showcasing Thai culture, in which drag performers appeared on street food carts, replaced with a wide shot taken from rehearsals.

Eurovision fans accused the channel of “censorship”. The Guardian has not been able to review TV3’s broadcast independently as its livestream is no longer available online.

Martin Green, the director of the Eurovision song contest, said: “We are surprised and disappointed to learn that the broadcast of Eurovision song contest Asia was altered in Malaysia.

“We take this matter seriously and will be writing to Voxovation, the producers of the show, to seek clarification and ask them to establish with Malaysian broadcaster TV3 why this happened.

“This appears to be isolated to the Malaysian broadcast of the show, and all other transmissions were not altered.”

Voxovation said in a statement that it was aware of the concerns raised and was “looking into the matter with the broadcaster to establish the circumstances … We are working with the Malaysian broadcaster to make sure this doesn’t happen again.”

Neither the EBU nor Voxovation said whether the contest’s participation agreement required broadcasters to carry the show unedited or what the consequences would be if one did not.

The Guardian has asked Media Prima, Malaysia’s largest media group and TV3’s owner, for comment.

This is not the first time the wider Eurovision song contest has faced allegations of censorship. In May 2018 the EBU terminated its partnership with the Chinese streaming service Mango TV after it dropped the Irish and Albanian entries and blurred a rainbow flag in the audience, saying such censorship was not in line with its “values of universality and inclusivity”.

Asked before Saturday’s final how the EBU’s commitment to its values squared with the laws in Malaysia and Bangladesh, the other competing country where same-sex relations are criminalised, the EBU’s head of communications, Dave Goodman, said the contest’s values were “non-negotiable”.

Malaysia has some of the harshest anti-LGBTQ+ laws in Asia, with oral and anal sex being punishable by up to 20 years in prison.

Rights groups have warned of a hardening climate. In November last year, authorities raided a men-only spa in Kuala Lumpur and detained more than 200 men, though no charges followed. And in 2022, police raided an LGBTQ+ Halloween party in the capital and arrested 20 people.

In 2023, a festival appearance by the British band the 1975, whose singer criticised the country’s homophobic laws and kissed a male bandmate on stage, led to the event being cancelled and prompted tighter entry rules for foreign performers.

TV3 has faced pressure over LGBTQ+ visibility before. In February 2025, it apologised after male guests arrived at a red carpet event in clothing resembling women’s dress.

#50. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.
