The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#46. Xi, Trump and Putin to discuss ‘world peace’ at trilateral, says China

Beijing stops short of confirming specific agenda items, but many hope world’s three biggest nuclear powers will talk about denuclearisation

The meeting of Xi Jinping, Donald Trump and Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen this week will cover topics of “groundbreaking significance” including “safeguarding world peace”, the Chinese foreign ministry has said.

The confirmation of Thursday’s first-of-their-kind trilateral talks, on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation (Apec) summit, cements Xi’s status as a global powerbroker as he presides over events in the glittering southern tech hub of Shenzhen.

The topics up for discussion are far from clear but many – including the UN secretary general – are hoping that denuclearisation will be discussed between the world’s three largest nuclear powers.

In a call this week, China’s top diplomat, Wang Yi, and the US secretary of state, Marco Rubio, “agreed on the importance of meaningful leader-level conversations to advance goals such as peace and denuclearisation”, the US state department said.

China was the last of the three powers to confirm the trilateral meeting was happening, and its statement stopped short of confirming specific agenda items.

“[The leaders] will exchange views in-depth on safeguarding world peace and promoting strategic stability in major-power relations, which is of groundbreaking significance,” said the foreign ministry spokesperson, Mao Ning. “Stronger communication among China, Russia, and the United States, along ​with managing differences and stabilising relations, serves the interests of all three countries.”

The Kremlin said “the signficance and symbolism of this meeting are hard to overestimate”.

The UN secretary general, António Guterres, said that if the three leaders “really commit themselves to a meaningful denuclearisation, it will be a great achievement for the world”.

However, many analysts have warned to temper expectations of a major breakthrough.

Xi will “bask in the glow of China serving as the convener and agenda-setter”, wrote Brookings’ Ryan Hass in one such take. However, “symbolism may prove more enduring than the substance of this meeting”.

The three leaders would be approaching the talks with very different priorities, Xiaoyu Pu from the University of Nevada told the Guardian.

Our US morning briefing breaks down the key stories of the day, telling you what’s happening and why it matters

China has rebuffed previous attempts to become embroiled in nuclear arms talks with ​Russia and the US, which ‌have the larger stockpiles. Trump, though, has said he wants to talk about denuclearisation, as well as the US-Israeli war in Iran. China, a top economic partner of Tehran, has sought to mediate in the conflict, and says avoiding a return to hostilities is a “top priority”.

In contrast, as feared by Kyiv, Russia’s invasion of Ukraine might not come up at all, despite Putin reportedly wanting it to.

US officials say Trump is not expected to raise the issue, and the German president, Frank-Walter Steinmeier, said Xi showed “irritation” in a recent meeting when the idea of the trilateral talks covering Ukraine came up.

For Xi, “much of the political benefit” will be simply in “the ability to bring Putin and Trump to the table”, Peter Alexander, the Shanghai-based managing director at Z-Ben Advisors, told CNBC.

The Wall Street Journal reported last week that the Trump administration had pushed back against Beijing’s demands that the summit be tightly scripted, with prepared statements and not much room for discussion.

Sari Arho Havrén, from the Royal United Services Institute thinktank, wrote on X: “China dislikes surprises and wants to ensure neither Trump nor Putin steals the limelight from the host. Moreover, Xi doesn’t want to be dragged into a discussion about Ukraine or anything else he is not prepared for.”

#45. Winter Nightmare Looms Early in Ukraine as Russia Strikes Relentlessly

Even as it talks peace, Russia has opened a bombing campaign intended to strip Kyiv and other cities of heat, running water and sanitation this winter.

When American-led talks to end the war in Ukraine resumed last week in Abu Dhabi, the overriding question was whether Russia intended to give any ground after a nine-month lull in negotiations.

But Russian missiles and drones had already done the talking for Moscow, analysts said. Weeks earlier than expected, Russia has opened an intense bombing campaign against the infrastructure that keeps Ukrainians alive and functioning in the winter. Daily waves of strikes against Ukraine’s energy system have raised fears that the capital, Kyiv, a city of three million people, could become all but uninhabitable in the coming months.

In the Abu Dhabi talks, an emboldened Russia outlined hard-line demands that stood little chance of gaining acceptance by Ukraine and its European allies. That unbending stance indicated that Russia had no real interest in discussing peace now, because Moscow believes that the winter bombing campaign will strengthen its negotiating position entering the spring.

“They want to come to the table holding our energy system hostage,” President Volodymyr Zelensky of Ukraine said after a recent Russian attack.

The Abu Dhabi talks will be followed by a meeting on Thursday between President Trump, President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia and the Chinese leader, Xi Jinping, at a summit in Shenzhen, China. It was unclear whether the three leaders would discuss the war in Ukraine.

Assaulting infrastructure has become Moscow’s strategy for victory as it has failed for nearly five years to achieve its major objectives in Ukraine. While Russia has tried each winter to knock out the country’s power system and freeze its people into submission, Ukrainian cities are now in their most precarious position of the war. The grid is more fragile after years of strikes, and the Ukrainian military is struggling to protect the system because of dwindling air defenses.

#41. 'Can you believe it?': SpaceX catches part of its Starship rocket on return to Earth

SpaceX has successfully caught the top part of its huge Starship rocket on its return to Earth for the first time.

"Can you believe it?," a commentator said as large crowds cheered at the launch site in Texas.

The company had hoped to catch both the bottom and upper stages of the rocket using mechanical arms - nicknamed chopsticks - on its launch tower.

But the first catch of the rocket's booster was aborted a few minutes after the launch on Friday. The booster was diverted to the ocean, bursting into flames just above the sea.

The top part, known as the Ship, returned to Earth a few minutes behind schedule at around 15:20 local time (21:20 GMT) on Saturday, in the first-ever successful catch attempt.

#39. Two skulls likely belonging to enslaved people returned to Brazil after Harvard used them in eugenics studies

Remains believed to have belonged to Africans or Afro-Brazilians repatriated after being flown back by diplomats

Two skulls, including that of a man believed to have taken part in Brazil’s greatest slave revolt but whose remains were taken to the United States and used in racist eugenics studies at Harvard University, were repatriated on Wednesday.

The remains had been outside Brazil since the 19th century. One was taken about 190 years ago from Salvador, the capital of the state of Bahia; the other, about 160 years ago, from Rio de Janeiro – and were welcomed back in moving, subdued ceremonies in both cities.

The skulls are believed to have belonged to Africans or Afro-Brazilians in the country that received more enslaved Africans than any other during the transatlantic slave trade.

Each was brought back in a padded box inside a reinforced suitcase, carried on flights by Brazilian diplomats after a four-year campaign to recover the remains from Harvard’s Peabody Museum of Archaeology and Ethnology in the US.

Brazilian researchers from local universities will now try to learn more about who the two individuals were.

The skulls will not be displayed in museums or have their images released, and the Brazilian government has pledged that they will be buried with funeral rites as soon as the identification process is complete.

“It’s very important to restore the dignity of these people, which was taken from them in life and in death,” said Carolina Dias dos Reis, a government official who travelled to Boston to sign the agreement with Harvard.

The existence of the skulls was revealed in 2022, and Brazilian researchers sought their repatriation from Harvard, but said they faced a drawn-out process that only moved forward after the Brazilian government became involved in late 2024.

In recent months, concerns grew that Donald Trump’s sometimes hostile behaviour towards his Brazilian counterpart, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, could derail the repatriation. But everything worked out.

“We need to recover our past as a country, however difficult and violent it was, so that we can understand what we are living through in the present: the consequences and scars of structural racism,” added Reis.

Slavery was abolished in 1888, but nearly a century and a half later, Afro-Brazilians make up the majority of the population while occupying the bottom of every socioeconomic indicator, dominating the statistics on hunger, poverty, gender-based violence and victims of homicides committed by criminals or the police.

During the repatriation ceremony in Rio de Janeiro, Ivanir dos Santos, a history professor and a cleric of the Afro-Brazilian religion Candomblé, poured a glass of water on to the ground as researchers received the suitcase.

“It was a ritual act because you cannot deal with the remains of our ancestors without paying reverence to them. This is not a research object … for us, that person is still alive in memory and as our ancestor,” he said.

It is not precisely known who the skulls belonged to. The one taken to Salvador is believed to have belonged to a man who took part in the most significant urban slave revolt in Brazilian history, in 1835, when about 600 African Muslims attempted to seize control of what was then the country’s second most important city. They were ultimately defeated by police, who killed 70 rebels.

The man died at a hospital, and a US diplomat, Gideon T Snow, is believed to have sent the skull to Boston.

The Bahia Islamic Cultural Centre, one of the most active campaigners for its repatriation, has named the man Arakunrin, meaning brother in Yoruba.

Even less is known about the other person: initially, only that they were “an individual … exhumed from the ‘streets of Rio de Janeiro’” in the 1860s. Brazilian government officials, however, said on Wednesday that Harvard researchers had told them that some features – including that the skull was smaller than that of an adult man but larger than that of a child – suggested it may have belonged to a woman.

Bruno Veras, a historian who was part of the group that closely followed the repatriation, cross-referenced diaries, letters and maps of the city and believes the skull may have been taken from a cemetery for newly arrived Africans who either did not survive the long Atlantic crossing or died soon after disembarking.

The person believed to have taken it to the US was Walter Hunnewell, a student from a wealthy Boston family. He was part of an expedition led by the Harvard professor Louis Agassiz, who was in Brazil trying to document physical types to support his racist theories.

“To steal a skull and take it to another country was violent even in the context of racist research,” said Veras.

Researchers in forensic anthropology will now try to confirm the sex of the individuals and other information through measurements of the skulls – the one in Salvador is complete, while the one in Rio is missing its jaw – and microscopic analyses. If necessary, they will seek authorisation to carry out genetic or dating tests.

“This history of slavery cannot be undone,” said Irina Feisthauer Silveira, a diplomat who carried one of the skulls. “But acknowledging the mistakes of the past with transparency is a first step towards building a more equal, just and less violent future.”

#34. U.N. Starts Uncertain Effort to Regulate Lethal Autonomous Weapons

Delegates met in Geneva to discuss guardrails for such systems even as their development grows. Russia and the United States have resisted the efforts.

Global efforts to limit the use of lethal autonomous weapons reached a crossroads this week as countries gathered to decide whether to negotiate binding restrictions on the technology, despite strong resistance from the United States and Russia.

Delegates of 128 states met at the United Nations in Geneva on Monday for a weeklong conference to determine whether to draft a treaty that would ban lethal autonomous weapons and require meaningful human control of such systems.

“The longer the international community waits, the more difficult it will become to establish meaningful safeguards — and it may be too late,” Volker Türk, the U.N. human rights chief, said before the conference.

Artificial intelligence and its military uses are developing at breakneck speed, as seen on the battlefields of Ukraine and the Middle East. That has deepened international alarm and prompted calls for action by many countries, global leaders and activist groups, even as top military powers balk at attempts to curb their ability to develop new weapons.

“Decisions affecting human life, freedom and the use of force must always remain subject to effective, informed and responsible human oversight,” Pope Leo XIV said in a statement last month that urged those at the Geneva conference to take “concrete “steps” to ban lethal autonomous weapons. Artificial intelligence, he said, “must be ‘disarmed.’”

Russia and Ukraine have already deployed fully autonomous drones to carry out attacks. Disarmament experts fear that the low cost and easy availability of such technology could lead to its rapid spread to law enforcement, nonstate armed groups and organized crime.

#32. US basketball player arrested with 34kg of cannabis at Nigerian airport

The drugs agency described the seized package as a "potent strain of cannabis"

A basketball player from the US has been arrested in Nigeria with 33.95kg (75lbs) of cannabis in his luggage at the main airport in Lagos.

The National Drug Law Enforcement Agency (NDLEA) identified the player as Greene Da'monte Khiree, 28, from the US state of Georgia, who it said had arrived from Thailand via the United Arab Emirates.

According to the agency, the suspect said he had been playing professional basketball in Thailand and was visiting to Nigeria to "build networks" for his career. He has not publicly commented on the allegations.

The agency said an examination of the suspect's phone had pointed "to his knowledge of and involvement in the drug trafficking activity".

In statement on Sunday, the agency said he was arrested on Wednesday after airport officials noticed him abandoning his two checked-in bags and attempting to leave with his carry-on bag.

The two bags, which bore his name, contained 32 packs of the drug known as "Loud", a potent strain of cannabis, the agency said.

A tracking device was also recovered from the bags, the agency added.

The International Basketball Federation (FIBA) lists a player by the name Damonte Greene, with the most recent appearance at the Phuket Elite Cup in Thailand, on 26 September.

Greene played with the Reinhardt University's basketball team in the US in the 2017-2018 season, before he went to play at the South Georgia Technical College.

He was the leading scorer with Georgia Bombers semi-professional basketball team during the 2023-24 season before he moved to Thailand.

Nigeria, once regarded primarily as a transit hub for drug trafficking, has increasingly become a producer, consumer, and distributor of illegal drugs, according to the UN.

Recent NDLEA operations have uncovered several industrial-scale methamphetamine laboratories, raising concerns about links between Nigerian drug producers and Mexican cartels seeking to manufacture and export the drug.

In September, the agency said it had dismantled a meth lab and arrested a Mexican suspected drug kingpin who was allegedly attempting to flee Nigeria through Lagos.

Methamphetamine, commonly known as meth, is a highly addictive synthetic drug that can cause severe psychosis, heart attacks or stroke.

In August, a social media influencer suspected of links to an international drug trafficking network was arrested after the authorities seized cocaine worth nearly $28m (Â£21m), which was destined for the UK.

#31. Storm Charles causes floods and flight cancellations across southern Europe

Heavy rain and strong winds have also caused disruption across British Columbia, Canada, over the last week

Storm Charles has brought destructive weather to the Iberian peninsula this week, particularly in Portugal and the far south of Spain. Heavy rain and strong south-westerly winds arrived first in the Portuguese island of Madeira, before the system headed towards the mainland on 11 November. A wind gust of 84.5mph (136 km/h) was recorded at Funchal airport, alongside rainfall totals reaching 355mm (13.97in) in 12 hours. In Lisbon, 183 flights were cancelled on Wednesday, about a third of its normal daily traffic, while Madrid Barajas airport had 155 cancellations.

After causing widespread flooding in Spain and Portugal, the system moved on a north-easterly track through southern France and then towards northern Italy. This resulted in significant flooding in parts of Cannes, some of which caused rock falls and led to the closure of roads along the French Riviera. The Italian weather service issued a red rainfall warning, its highest level, for the area extending from Tuscany to Venezia. Further heavy rain is expected to fall in Italy and the Balkans over the next few days, with the latest forecast models indicating an additional 100mm could accumulate widely, while totals close to 200mm are likely in more mountainous areas.

Successive rounds of heavy rain and strong winds have caused flooding, landslides and disruption across British Columbia, Canada through the last week or so.

Between Monday and Friday last week, severe rainfall and strong winds left more than 400,000 customers without power in British Columbia. These unsettled conditions continued into the weekend, with further heavy rain leaving 135,000 customers without power, alongside downed trees, flooded roads and landslides. Strong winds were also recorded on Sunday, with a gust of 72.1mph (116 km/h) on Herbert Island and a gust of 60mph (96 km/h) at Victoria international airport.

Heavy rain warnings were then issued on Wednesday for British Columbia’s Vancouver Island and southern coastal areas as multiple stations recorded rainfall totals in excess of 100mm. Additionally, the River Forecast Centre issued flood warnings on Wednesday across the south coast with heavy rain and high streamflow continuing to bring an increased risk of flash flooding and landslides.

A further area of low pressure is expected to bring torrential rain and strong winds across this same region over the weekend. More than 100mm is likely across central and southern parts of Vancouver Island on Saturday, with gusts in excess of 60mph likely too.

#29. Clothing factories shut in Bangladesh as workers strike over pay

Protesters in Dhaka, Bangladesh, on Friday demanding a new minimum wage

Dozens of factories in Bangladesh's main clothes-producing area have been shut by workers striking over a higher minimum wage.

Nearly 150 sites across the country have been closed in the textile hubs of Savar, Ashulia and Gazipur this weekend.

The garment industry is big business for Bangladesh, generating nearly $40bn (Â£30bn) in exports every year and making up 10% of the country's total GDP.

Workers are striking over demands for a minimum wage of 30,000 taka (Â£182) a month. The minimum monthly wage in Bangladesh is currently 12,500 taka but employees say this is not enough to deal with the cost of living crisis.

On Sunday morning workers massed on the Dhaka-Tangail highway in Gazipur to block the road. Traffic was stopped by the protests for about an hour before police stepped in.

A "No Work No Pay" provision of labour laws allows owners to suspend operations and withhold wages when production is disrupted.

Police Supt Mohammad Amjad Hossain told the BBC officers shut factories in the city after the workers walked out.

He said 13 factories were closed on Saturday night and "sixteen factories were closed during the morning unrest".

Wage protests force hundreds of Bangladesh garment factories to close

The garment industry in Bangladesh employs about four million workers making clothes for big brands including H&M, Primark and Zara.

Bangladesh is the world's second largest exporter of ready- to-wear clothes after China and it is feared that the current unrest could have a knock-on effect for international buyers if it carries on for substantial amount of time.

The Bangladesh Garment Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BGMEA) and the Bangladesh Knitwear Manufacturers and Exporters Association (BKMEA) is calling for protection for factories.

"BGMEA and BKMEA have questioned the justification of continuing to participate in wage board negotiations without any assurance that the factories will be protected," BGMEA President Mahmud Hasan Khan said.

"Garment factories that have been unable to continue production due to the ongoing unrest will remain indefinitely closed from tomorrow under... Bangladesh's labour law."

But critics say workers are being sacrificed for factories to churn out products at competitive prices.

Joly Talukder, the vice president of the Garment Workers Trade Union Centre, told BBC's Newshour that "workers have carried the costs for years" while factory owners and global retailers profit from their exploitation.

"They are asking for a wage they can survive on. How can any person of conscience stand against such a basic human demand?" she said.

On 1 November the government formed a minimum wage board to recommend a revised pay structure for workers.

The board, chaired by a senior district judge, said at its first meeting it would finalise any recommendations by the end of November.

Once it is put in place, the revised pay structure is expected to take effect from 1 December.

#27. Choking fires, deadly heat and devastating floods: 2026 in extreme weather

People around the world have contended with unprecedented weather events and their fatal effects caused by the climate catastrophe

After the world’s hottest summer since records began in 1850, the death toll from extreme weather in 2026 is already in the high tens of thousands. That is a conservative estimate and is certain to rise much higher.

As Cop31 continues and scientists warn that climate breakdown may be accelerating, the Guardian looks back at a year of droughts, fires and floods as a reminder that, unless the world takes urgent action to cut emissions, this disastrous year will be one of the best of the rest of our lives.

Extreme heat fuels wildfires in Australia As temperatures jump nearly 20C (68F) above average, Victoria and other southern states register new heat records, including 50C in Port Augusta and the tiny opal mining town of Andamooka. Melbourne hospitals report a 25% increase in heat-related illnesses, while authorities in many areas struggle with wildfires. Scientists say the supercharged climate has made the heatwave five times more likely. Heatwaves already cause more deaths in Australia than all other natural hazards combined.

Mudslides kill 72 in Brazil after record rain The city of Juiz de Fora in Minas Gerais records its wettest ever month, triggering landslides that sweep away hundreds of homes in poor communities on unstable hillsides. Floods also disrupt Rio de Janeiro and São Paulo. More than 10,000 people are displaced.

Devastating floods across much of the world In Ethiopia, at least 131 people are killed in floods and mudslides that bury entire villages. Eighty die in Kenya and 18 in Tanzania. The Arabian peninsula is battered by fierce winds and heavy downpours that flood the streets of the desert kingdom. Hailstones the size of tennis balls fall in Oman and lightning storms leave cities in the UAE inundated with water. Many areas report record rainfall. At least 30 die in Afghanistan as heavy rains trigger landslides. Hong Kong is soaked in twice the usual monthly precipitation. Flood waters in southern Russia submerge homes and leave more than 300,000 without electricity. Huge tracts of farmland are left underwater in Argentina and Chile.

World’s energy imbalance accelerates rapidly The World Meteorological Organization reveals an alarming buildup of energy in Earth’s system. More than 90% is stored in the oceans, where warming has more than doubled over the past two decades compared with the average over the previous 45 years. This makes weather more extreme and threatens health and food supplies. António Guterres, the UN secretary general, says: “The global climate is in a state of emergency. Planet Earth is being pushed beyond its limits. Every key climate indicator is flashing red.” Guterres urges the world to cut fossil fuel emissions as rapidly as possible to minimise future risks. However, CO2 in the atmosphere continues to rise.

Super typhoon tears through Micronesia, Guam and Saipan At peak intensity on 12 April, Sinlaku registers a wind speed of 295km/h (183mph) – roughly the same as a Shinkansen bullet train and the joint highest recorded by a storm in the western Pacific basin so early in the year. It is also the deadliest in decades, killing 12, leaving 43,000 without power, forcing mass evacuation and causing $1.5bn (£1.1bn) in damages. This will remain one of the most powerful storms of 2026, superseded only by Super Typhoon Champi that will strike Taiwan and Japan in October.

Hundreds of millions endure deadly heat in south Asia At least 47 heat-related deaths are reported in southern Asia as temperatures soar above 45C in several Indian and Bangladeshi cities, and hit a peak of 52C in Pakistan’s southern province of Sindh. Scientists warn that the climate crisis is exposing hundreds of millions of people in south Asia to longer, deadlier pre-monsoon heat.

Ocean heat enters uncharted territory Sea surface temperature graphs begin to jag upwards and away from all previous highs on 21 June, starting a trend that will worsen over the rest of the year. This alarms scientists because oceans absorb more than 90% of the excess energy in Earth’s system. If the seas start to release that heat then the land and air will get warmer more quickly. This coincides with the emergence of the latest El Niño, which is confirmed on 2 June.

Arctic and Antarctic swelter in unprecedented warmth Instead of the usual winter snow and ice, scientists at the Esperanza base on the Trinity peninsula of Antarctica are astonished to see rain and dirt outside their windows after an eight-day heatwave that peaked with a record temperature of 15C on 6 June. At the other end of the planet, the Arctic experiences its warmest ever June. Beliy Island, Siberia, registers 28.3C on 25 June, the highest temperature ever recorded that far north in the Eurasian Arctic.

West African deluge takes heavy toll Relentless rains claim almost 100 lives in west Africa from May to June. Record downpours of up to 140mm (5.5in) in a single day are made five times more likely and 23% more intense by the human-caused climate crisis. Floods and mudslides kill 59 in Côte d’Ivoire, 35 in Ghana and five in Togo.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

Fires choke Canada and Europe Almost 1,000 wildfires sweep across Canada, charring 3m hectares (7.4m acres) and sending a haze of smoke as far as New York. On 15 July Toronto registers the world’s worst air quality as a result of the haze. Smoke kills 4,000 people in Canada each year, almost four times as many as at the start of the century. Meanwhile, in Europe, prolonged droughts create tinderbox conditions for wildfires in Greece, Portugal, Italy, Norway, Germany, Spain and France. They burn 686,000 hectares in Europe, twice the average of the past 20 years.

Extreme weather disrupts US 250th birthday festivities and the World Cup Half of the US population is under a heat alert before 4 July independence celebrations, disrupting Donald Trump’s Great American State Fair on the National Mall in Washington and other events. Dozens of heat-related deaths are reported and thousands experience power cuts.

At least nine World Cup games are played in potentially dangerous heat and humidity, while fierce storms delay three matches by more than an hour, including Mexico v England.

Nepal glacier collapse kills at least 1,300 Lhende Khola river valley communities are swept away after a 2 sq km (0.8 sq miles) slab of rock and glacier ice falls 1,400 metres (4,600ft) in the Himalayas, triggering a flood of water and debris. More than 5,000 people are missing and 8,600 need medical attention. Scientists say unusually warm temperatures and long-term permafrost degradation owing to the climate crisis were among the likely causes of the catastrophe.

Europe’s murderous heat forces reactor shutdown The UK and France are among a host of nations that swelter through the warmest summer (June to August) in European history. The heat kills an estimated 50,000 people in cities across the continent. Two-thirds of those excess deaths are attributable to the climate crisis. Many more are likely to have died in rural areas. As droughts spread, Romania is forced to shut down its only nuclear power station because the cooling system could not be replenished by low water levels in the Danube River.

Record heat and deadly storms lash Asia Hundreds are killed and millions evacuated as the world’s most populous continent experiences its hottest September and severe storms. Typhoon Dujuan kills at least 10 people in Japan, with violent winds of 144km/h and 832mm of rain in only 48 hours on the island of Izu Oshima. Three die in a mudslide in eastern China during Typhoon Saudel. At least 102 are killed in India and Nepal amid rain that is nine times the norm in some places. Eight die and half a million are affected by flooding in Bangkok, Thailand.

Governments warned to brace for peak El Niño The UN disaster relief agency urges countries to prepare for an unprecedented “wrecking ball” El Niño that is forecast to peak around the end of the year. UK authorities advise households to stockpile a few days of basic supplies. Climate scientists project more than 450,000 people globally will die by February as a result of the additional heat brought by the super El Niño.

Protests in Indonesia after deadly fires Demonstrators in Jakarta protest against Indonesia’s worst fire season in a decade, which has scorched almost 2m hectares of forest – more than twice as big an area as last year. The choking haze is estimated to have killed 3,560 people in one month alone.

Roads turn to rivers in Chile After the heaviest October rainfall in 76 years, the Chilean government declares the Santiago district of Las Condes a “disaster zone”, with more than 1,000 people evacuated from their homes, some by helicopter.

Drought and fire in the Amazon rainforest Months of drought leave some of the world’s mightiest rivers, including the Rio Negro, Madeira and Tapajós, close to record low levels, curtailing the water transport of essential goods. This coincides with a sharp rise in fire activity in the central and eastern Amazon region, releasing so much smoke that the city of Manaus registers the second-worst air quality in the world on 12 October.

2026 is forecast to be one of the hottest years in history

Global temperatures tie an all-time record of 2.04C above preindustrial levels, which was set in 2023. This year is forecast to be one of the four warmest years in history, along with 2023, 2024 and 2025. Sea surface temperatures are so high that the climate scientist Eliot Jacobson calculates the odds of being this far above the norm at 33tn to one. Meanwhile, the amount of CO2 in the atmosphere reaches a peak not seen in 2m years. On the plus side, global fossil fuel emissions are likely to fall this year thanks to a shift away from oil and gas owing to high prices after the US-Israeli attack on Iran, and ever cheaper renewable alternatives.

#25. Ashes of exiled Nobel Laureate Miguel Ángel Asturias returned to Guatemala

The urn was placed in soil brought from different parts of Guatemala in a ceremony honouring the long-exiled poet, author and diplomat

The remains of writer and diplomat Miguel Ãngel Asturias have been buried in his native Guatemala 52 years after he died in exile.

His ashes were brought to Guatemala from Paris, where he had lived before his death from cancer in the Spanish capital, Madrid, in 1974.

Asturias was awarded the Nobel Prize for Literature in 1967 for his vivid writings, which drew on the experiences of indigenous Guatemalans.

The urn containing his ashes was placed in soil gathered from different parts of Guatemala at a cultural centre named after him in Guatemala City.

His son, whose name is also Miguel Ãngel Asturias, said at the ceremony on Sunday: "I can tell you, old man, that you are with us, you are in your homeland, you will sleep beneath your own skies."

Asturias, who was born in 1899, first moved to Paris in the 1920s to study ethnology.

Influenced by the surrealists, he wrote his first major work, Legends of Guatemala, a retelling of Maya origin legends and mythological tales, while in France.

Subsequent works, such as the 1946 novel The President and his Banana Trilogy, denounced the terrors of dictatorship in Latin America and the exploitation indigenous workers suffered on banana plantations.

Arguably his most famous novel was Men of Maize, published in 1949, which explores the struggles of indigenous communities as the world around them quickly changes and modernises.

Nobel Peace Prize laureate Rigoberta MenchÃº (centre) attended a ceremony to welcome the ashes of Asturias back to Guatemala alongside the writer's son (right)

Between 1944 and 1954, a period known as "Guatemala's 10 Years of Spring", he served in several diplomatic posts in Central and South America and continued writing.

But when the democratically elected Guatemalan President Jacobo Ãrbenz GuzmÃ¡n was deposed in a CIA-backed coup in 1954, Asturias was stripped of his Guatemalan citizenship, his books were banned and he had to go into exile.

At Sunday's burial ceremony, Asturias's son said that the writer had "always wanted to be in Guatemala" but "political conditions did not allow him to".

The family only agreed to have the ashes moved from PÃ¨re Lachaise cemetery in Paris to Guatemala after the current progressive president of Guatemala, Bernardo ArÃ©valo, was elected in 2023.

ArÃ©valo praised Asturias at the interment ceremony as "our voice in the world".

"He deserved to rest in the land he named and reinvented in his pages," said ArÃ©valo, whose father Juan JosÃ© appointed Asturias to the diplomatic service when the older ArÃ©valo served as president during the 10 Year Spring.

#24. ‘The blood of these brothers cries out’: Pope Leo demands justice for people killed protecting the Amazon

Pontiff delivers forceful speech in Peru as he travels from high in the Andes into the Amazon

Pope Leo XIV has demanded justice for environmental defenders killed for protecting the Amazon and backed efforts by Indigenous peoples to stop their lands from being ravaged by logging and illegal mining.

On the penultimate day of his South American tour, Leo on Sunday travelled into the Peruvian Amazon and channelled one of Pope Francis’s core teachings that links care for the most vulnerable with care for God’s creation.

“The cry of the Earth and the cry of the poor are inseparable,” Leo told Indigenous representatives and missionaries gathered along the Ucayali River in the north-eastern city of Pucallpa.

He said he came to Pucallpa to support them in their search for justice and peace and referred to what environmental groups estimate have been 57 murders of environmental defenders between 2010 and 2024.

“The blood of these brothers cries out to God and demands justice,” Leo said to applause from the crowd, many of whom were wearing traditional feathered headwear and face markings of the region.

In addition to deforestation, illegal gold mining has become an increasingly lucrative business in the Amazon, devastating forests and polluting rivers. Illegal mining and coca production has also driven deforestation.

Leo addressed the combination of threats in one of his most forceful speeches to date, drawing on his years of experience as a Peruvian bishop and Francis’s constant preaching about the environment and the plight of Indigenous groups.

During the encounter and a subsequent mass in Pucallpa, he demanded a different model of economic development that respects the Earth and the Indigenous peoples who care for it, rather than the “irresponsible exploitation” by outside actors.

“Very little has been done – and very slowly at that – while the means to exploit and pollute creation have accelerated,” he said at mass. “In the face of these evils, let us not bury the Lord’s gifts, for he gives us the strength to repair and heal the suffering caused by an insatiable thirst for domination.”

In Peru, most murders of environmental defenders go unpunished; only a few have resulted in convictions.

The most recent conviction came in the case of Kichwa leader Quinto Inuma Alvarado, who was murdered in 2023 when he was ambushed by hitmen on a river in the San Martín region, the Amazonian territory he was defending.

Leo also referred to a notorious rise in sexual violence against Indigenous Awajún and Wampis schoolgirls in the northern Amazon, and blasted the “impunity” and “corruption” surrounding them.

The Awajún and Wampis Women’s Council has recorded 370 cases of sexual violence in the Condorcanqui province between January and July.

Julio Cusurichi, an Indigenous leader, handed Leo a letter during his visit to Pucallpa, asking him to help protect the rights of uncontacted Indigenous relatives.

“The Peruvian state is weakening the mechanisms that should safeguard their territories,” said the letter from the Indigenous rights organisation Aidesep, on which Cusurichi is a board member.

“We cannot allow economic interests to take precedence over the lives and rights of these vulnerable peoples. Therefore, we ask Pope Leo XIV to raise his voice and support this urgent call for their defence.”

Cardinal Pedro Barreto praised the pope’s decision to meet with Indigenous peoples. Barreto, a former Peruvian archbishop who helped win a multimillion-dollar pollution payout for an Andean town, said: “[Leo] is well aware of all the historical suffering … corruption, illegal mining, deforestation, and the contempt shown by politicians.”

Luis E Fernandez, co-founder of Cincia, a scientific research centre in Peru, also hailed the pontiff’s power to raise awareness. “Pope Leo’s recognition of pollution from illegal mining brings much-needed attention to a crisis affecting Indigenous and riverine communities across the Amazon,” he said.

Fernandez visited the Vatican in October and presented Leo with scientific evidence detailing the impact of illegal gold mining and associated mercury poisoning.

Leo’s last full day in Peru was also his most ambitious, crisscrossing the nation to minister to communities high in the Andes mountains. Peru’s air force released footage of the pope inside a helicopter and said it was “an unforgettable memory” to take Leo around the country.

His first stop was the mountain city of Cusco, where huge crowds greeted him.

“It is all worth it,” said Margarida Salomon, 63, who said she came from Sucre in Bolivia to see the pontiff, spending about $500 to take a bus and sleep in a tent. “I am still getting to know this pope, but the fact he is here in the Andes means a lot.”

The visit to Cusco, 3,400m (more than 11,000ft) above sea level, tested Leo’s stamina as he continues recovering from lung cancer surgery last month. Leo sat in the chair of his popemobile during part of the ride around Cusco, but it wasn’t clear if that was to preserve his energy or to steady himself on the uneven streets.

In general, the 71-year-old Leo has seemed unfazed by the toll of the three-nation South American trip, the longest of his pontificate.

#23. Bahrain Convicts Dozens of Shiite Clerics in Sweeping Terrorism Trial

The defendants were accused of colluding with Iran. Rights groups said the case was marred by torture allegations and raised concerns about religious persecution.

Bahrain sentenced dozens of Shiite Muslim clerics and other religious figures to life in prison on terrorism charges on Monday, accusing them of colluding with Iran against the Bahraini government.

The convictions underscored rising sectarian and political tensions in Bahrain, a Gulf Arab country, amid the U.S.-Israeli war on Shiite-led Iran. Human rights groups had denounced the trial, saying that it was marred by allegations of torture and coerced confessions.

A lawyer for several of the defendants, speaking on condition of anonymity out of fear of retribution from Bahrain’s authoritarian government, said that all of them had pleaded not guilty. He described the trial as farcical, saying that lawyers had been deprived of basic information about the charges against their clients.

When asked about those claims, the Bahraini government said in a statement that all of the defendants had been “afforded their full legal rights and safeguards, including the right of defense,” and that complaints of mistreatment would be referred for investigation.

“This case was about criminal conduct, not faith or belief,” the government added.

The Bahraini authorities had accused the defendants of forming a “terrorist group” linked to the Islamic Revolutionary Guards Corps of Iran. According to a statement from the Bahraini public prosecution department, published by the state news agency, the defendants had worked to undermine the Bahraini government, and the group’s leaders had received “assignments and instructions” from Iranian officials.

“They had also sought to change the system of government by force and unlawful means through incitement to commit terrorist acts, acts of rioting and sabotage,” the statement added.

#22. Mexican drug lord Miguel Ángel Félix Gallardo dies age 80

Miguel Ãngel FÃ©lix Gallardo pictured in the attorney general's office detention centre in Mexico City in 1989

Notorious Mexican drug lord Miguel Ãngel FÃ©lix Gallardo has died in hospital aged 80.

FÃ©lix Gallardo was widely considered one of the biggest drug smugglers in history and is credited with building an alliance between criminal groups in Colombia and his own Mexican cartel.

Known as The Godfather or the "Boss of Bosses", FÃ©lix Gallardo was the head of the all-powerful Guadalajara Cartel, which reigned over Mexican drug trafficking at the height of the cocaine boom of the 1980s.

At the time, it created a new modus operandi in the illegal drug trade which endures to this day. He had been in state custody since 1989.

The name Miguel Ãngel FÃ©lix Gallardo will echo through the annals of drug-trafficking history more than any other except perhaps Pablo Escobar's.

He led the organisation which revolutionised drug smuggling in the region by entering into partnership with the Colombian cartels, particularly Escobar's MedellÃ­n Cartel, to combine production in the Andes with Mexican transportation across the border into the United States.

At one point an estimated 80% of all cocaine that entered the US came from the MedellÃ­n Cartel. FÃ©lix Gallardo and his organisation played a major role in smuggling it in.

At its height, the Guadalajara Cartel under FÃ©lix Gallardo exerted huge influence over Mexican politics and law enforcement, controlling entire police networks and state governorships.

In 1985, the net started to tighten around the leaders of the cartel following the torture and murder of a DEA agent, Enrique "Kiki" Camarena.

Camarena had angered them after he had discovered and then destroyed a vast marijuana plantation in Chihuahua, which belonged to the criminal group.

His murder sparked one of the biggest manhunts the US government has ever launched in North America.

In 1989, FÃ©lix Gallardo was captured and sentenced to decades in prison. In ill health, he was granted house arrest in 2022 but technical problems with electronic ankle monitoring prevented his transfer. He died in hospital in his native Guadalajara.

Mexican prison police secured the hospital where Miguel Ãngel FÃ©lix Gallardo was admitted earlier this week

In the 1980s, Joaquin "El Chapo" GuzmÃ¡n was introduced to FÃ©lix Gallardo, who put him in charge of handling logistics for his drug organisation.

From the subsequent break-up of FÃ©lix Gallardo's organisation after his arrest, the Sinaloa Cartel emerged, led by GuzmÃ¡n, now another man synonymous with large-scale drug trafficking.

In 2019, GuzmÃ¡n was sentenced to life in prison for charges including drug trafficking and money laundering following one of the most high-profile trials in recent US history.

FÃ©lix Gallardo's life has been portrayed in a number of television programmes including the Netflix series Narcos: Mexico. The show depicted his rise to power as well as his relationship with GuzmÃ¡n.

#21. Delaware hit by rare November tornado

Meanwhile, severe thunderstorms bring hailstones the size of baseballs in Argentina

The National Weather Service in the US has confirmed that an EF1 tornado touched down south-east of Newark in the Salem Woods neighbourhood, Delaware, on Thursday. The tornado reached 160 metres wide, had a path length of 4.4 miles (7.1km) and reached estimated peak winds of up to 100mph (161km/h). There were no injuries or fatalities but windows and roofs were damaged on several homes, a large hotel sign fell on to a number of cars, and a semi truck overturned on I95. Although Delaware often experiences a few tornadoes every year, this is only the second time the state has had tornado activity in November since 1950, according to the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration. The only other November tornado recorded was in 1989.

Severe storms have also resulted in tornadoes in Argentina as well as large hailstones. A round of severe thunderstorms moved through the central Córdoba province on Friday, bringing hailstones up to the size of a baseball (about 7.5cm in diameter). Parts of the province recorded up to 73mm of rainfall in just six hours early on Saturday. Such storms are common at this time of year. Water vapour from the Amazon meeting the Andes quickly condenses, resulting in massive cloud formations often associated with heavy downpours, lightning, tornadoes and large hailstones.

India’s meteorological department has released an alert for a depression located in the Bay of Bengal with the potential to develop into a cyclone. A low-pressure system formed during the early hours of Friday before developing into a depression on Sunday. The department’s forecast is for the depression to intensify into a cyclonic storm by Tuesday before gradually drifting north-west towards eastern India, with the expectation it will make landfall on Thursday. If the storm were to develop it would become the third of the season and be named Akvan. There is a “watch” level warning for much of the eastern coast on Wednesday and Thursday, with parts of the Andhra Pradesh and Odisha states on an “alert” level.

#20. Australian MP 'full of gratitude' upon leaving hospital after alleged car attack

Independent MP Helen Haines has been released from hospital after an alleged attack last month

Australian politician Helen Haines has said she is "full of gratitude" upon being discharged from hospital, four weeks after she was allegedly attacked with a car and left a double amputee.

The independent MP was struck outside her electorate office in Wangaratta, north-east of Melbourne, on October 19, an incident which horrified Australia.

Brendan Nugent, 43, has been charged with dangerous driving causing serious injury, intentionally causing serious injury and the federal offence of causing harm to a public official. He has yet to enter pleas.

In her first public comments since the attack, Haines, 65, said she had moved into the rehabilitation stage of her recovery.

"This is a really positive moment, and I am full of gratitude for the expert and compassionate care I received at the Alfred and am receiving in this next stage," Haines said.

"I appreciate the thousands of warm wishes and supportive messages from people across Indi, throughout Australia and around the world. They are giving me strength as I focus on this next phase of healing."

She added that she had been "overwhelmed" by the generosity of well wishers who had donated to Save the Children in her honour.

Haines, known for supporting stronger climate action and helping establish a national anti-corruption commission, entered parliament in 2019 as the Member for Indi after working as a nurse and midwife.

Victoria Police has said its investigation, with support from the Australian Federal Police, remains ongoing and a motive is yet to be established.

Nugent's father said he and his wife had been long-time friends of Haines, and Nugent's lawyer has said his client has mental health concerns.

The incident sparked a reckoning in the country more broadly over the tone of public debate.

Though a motive is still unclear, politicians, law enforcement, and experts have said heated public discourse is heightening the threat of political violence in the country.

Prime Minister Anthony Albanese said he had been in regular contact with Haines and her "extraordinary" family.

"All our thoughts and prayers continue to be with Helen for her recovery," he said in a statement.

"We continue to provide support to Helen, her family, community and staff as she undertakes her rehabilitation."

#19. Collision of two trains in Egypt kills at least seven

President orders prompt investigation after one train derails in collision in al-Ayyat about 50km from Cairo

Seven people were killed and at least 125 injured when two trains collided on Sunday south of the Egyptian capital, Cairo, officials said.

The crash occurred in al-Ayyat district in Giza province, about 50km (30 miles) south of Cairo, when a train travelling to Fayoum province crashed into the rear of a train heading to the southern province of Aswan, according to the transport ministry.

An inspection indicated that the Cairo-Aswan train had been stationary on the same track, officials said.

The health ministry gave the casualty toll, adding more than 70 of the injured were discharged after receiving treatment.

Train derailments and crashes are common in Egypt, where the railway system has a history of mismanagement and poor maintenance of equipment. In recent years the government has announced modernisation initiatives to improve its railways.

Ayman Gamal, a resident of Kafr Girza in al-Ayyat where the crash occurred, said people rushed to the scene, pulled out survivors and called ambulances.

Gamal said he saw about 80 of the injured being taken by ambulances to hospital. Some passengers were trapped inside one of the carriages of the stationary train and died, he said.

Egypt’s president, Abdel Fatah al-Sisi, said in a social media post that he had ordered a prompt investigation.

Outside al-Wasta central hospital in Beni Suef province, where some of the injured were taken for treatment, survivors said passengers were in shock and struggling to breathe because of all the dust that was kicked up. Nearby villagers eventually got them out, they said.

Farha Gamal, a survivor who was inside the Fayyoum train, said she saw some of the passengers who appeared dead and others were struggling to find their way out of the car. “There were other cars that were completely mangled,” she said in an interview.

Emergency teams and cranes were dispatched to the crash site to clear the wreckage and restore train services to their normal schedule as soon as possible, the Egyptian railway authority said.

Last September, a train driver and his assistant were injured after two freight trains collided in the same district.

Egypt’s deadliest train crash was in 2002 when more than 300 people were killed after a fire broke out on an overnight train journeying from Cairo to southern Egypt.

#17. Two mass shootings in South Africa leave 17 dead

Gun violence remains a persistent problem in South Africa, where about 60 people are murdered every day, according to police (file photo)

South African police are investigating the murders of 17 people in two separate shootings on Sunday night.

Eight men were fatally shot in a minibus in KwaZulu-Natal province. Police declared them dead when they arrived at the scene. Hours later, nine people were found shot dead inside a shack in western Pretoria.

Police say the two incidents are unrelated. The motive for the shootings has not yet been established.

South Africa is struggling to contain violent crime, including gang-related killings. The country has one of the world's highest murder rates.

Police say they responded to reports of a shooting â outside Pietermaritzburg town in KwaZulu-Natal and found a white seven-seater vehicle riddled with bullet holes.

Inside were eight men with multiple gunshot wounds.

"It has not been established how the incident happened, and police are collecting pieces of evidence to determine the possible motive of the killings and the identity of the suspects," police spokesperson Robert Netshiunda said.

The police have launched a manhunt for the suspects.

In a separate shooting in the Jeffsville informal settlement in Atteridgeville, west of Pretoria, police found the bodies of six men and three women.

They are believed to have been killed inside a four-room shack between 22:00 and 23:00 on Sunday. The bodies were scattered across the rooms, with several spent cartridges discovered outside the shack.

Community members initially mistook the gunshots for thunder and lightning.

"The provincial commissioner of police in Gauteng [province, which includes Pretoria] has mobilized the necessary resources, including the crime intelligence and the deputy provincial commissioner tracking team to search for the suspect," police spokesperson Brigadier Brenda Muridili said.

It's not the first mass shooting in the area. In January, five men were shot dead outside a local shop in broad daylight.

Illegal firearms are widespread in South Africa, where gun violence remains a persistent problem, increasingly involving mass shootings.

In September, 28 people were killed in two separate cases.

Eighteen people were killed when eight gunmen stormed a bar near Johannesburg and opened fire on 25 September. Fourteen others were injured.

In a separate shooting the following day, 10 people were killed outside an entertainment venue in a township near Cape Town. A further 11 were injured.

Much of the violence has been linked to turf wars between gangs involved in drug trafficking and illegal mining.

In February, President Cyril Ramaphosa described organised crime as "the most immediate threat to our democracy, our society and our economic development".

Earlier this year, the military was deployed alongside police to five provinces with the highest murder rates, including Gauteng, in an effort to tackle armed violence.

According to the latest police statistics, 5,427 people were murdered nationwide between April and June 2026, an average of about 60 killings a day. This was a 6% decline compared with the same period a year earlier.

#16. Estonia to close Russia border crossings after attempted drone factory arson attack

The Estonian government says it will shut the crossings after accusing Russia of directing an alleged sabotage attack on a drone manufacturer

Estonia has said it will close border crossings into Russia from 1 December after accusing Moscow of being behind an attempted arson attack at a drone factory.

Prime Minister Kristen Michal said the break-in on Saturday at a facility north of the capital Tallinn was part of what he called Russia's "broader hybrid war against Europe".

The alleged attackers were arrested, Michal said, with local media reporting the suspects are Latvian.

They are said to have broken into the factory and attempted to set fire to it using flammable liquids.

On Monday, Moscow said the accusation was "baseless" and accused the Estonian government of "building a wall" between the neighbouring countries.

The facility targeted is operated by Threod Systems and produces attack and reconnaissance drones, with Ukraine and Nato member states listed as customers on its website.

Foreign minister Margus Tsahkna said on Sunday that "closing the border crossings will reduce Russia's ability to conduct hostile activities in Estonia".

Michal said "extensive damage was averted thanks to heightened security measures" at the factory, while the head of Estonia's internal security service labelled the incident attempted Russian sabotage.

The decision to close border crossings is expected to be finalised next week, according to local media.

Estonia shares a border of around 220 miles (338 km) with Russia that spans both sea and land. Three road crossings are in operation but are already subject to restrictions.

One of them is located at Narva, Estonia's third biggest city. It sits on the western bank of the River Narva that flows into the Baltic Sea; on the eastern bank is Russia. Closures will complicate life for families which live either side of the border.

Kremlin spokesman Dimitry Peskov said: "Estonia is building a wall. Many countries, as history demonstrates, have built walls that have brought nothing good to those who erect such walls."

Russia has been repeatedly accused by European leaders of targeting national infrastructure and resources destined for Ukraine since Russia launched its full-scale invasion in 2022.

Baltic region leaders have repeatedly accused Russia of hybrid attacks and raised fears about the Kremlin's territorial ambitions.

Other major backers of Ukraine in Europe, including France, Germany, the UK and Poland, have also said Russia has orchestrated attacks on their soil in recent months.

Last year, six men were jailed in the UK over an arson attack linked to Russia at a London warehouse where aid bound for Ukraine was being stored.

#15. Gérard Depardieu drops appeal against sexual assault conviction

GÃ©rard Depardieu appeared at a courthouse in Paris on Monday for his appeal trial that was due to start

French film star GÃ©rard Depardieu has dropped his appeal against a conviction for sexual assault, admitting that his behaviour towards women had often been offensive and offering a "deep and sincere" apology.

On the day that the appeal was due to start in a Paris court, the 77-year-old actor's unexpected mea culpa marks a dramatic reversal of his previous denials of sexual impropriety.

"Justice was pronounced. I was found guilty. I understand and bow to this convictionâ¦ I have therefore decided to abandon the appeal," he wrote.

It means he is now guilty in law of abusing two women during a film shoot in Paris five years ago, and his 18-month suspended prison term stands.

"Victory is ours, and we welcome it with head held high," said one of the two women, named only as AmÃ©lie, who was at the court.

"There is huge relief because the fight is over. The giant of film has fallen," said the other woman in the case, who is known in court by the false name Sarah.

In his statement, Depardieu appeared to have undergone a major shift from his earlier stance of unapologetic intransigence.

"For a long time on film shoots I hid myself behind provocation and smut. I thought that the remarks I made, and certain gestures that I made, were of no consequence.

"I see now that there is a border between impertinence and offence. Never, ever did I intend to hurt the women with whom I came into contact. But I see now that intention has no bearing on the hurt that people feel.

"Cases before the courts are damaging. Everything got out of handâ¦ including my defence. Many people have suffered in this story. I have not the power to go back in time. I have come to understand that I belonged to a world that has disappeared."

The reference to his defence "getting out of hand" follows widespread criticism of the aggressive line taken by Depardieu's lawyer during the original trial in March last year.

Depardieu's lawyers David-Olivier Kaminski (L) and Delphine Meillet (R) were at the courthouse on Monday for the opening day of their client's appeal trial in Paris

His new lawyers, Delphine Meillet and David-Olivier Kaminski, said that with his admission of guilt Depardieu wanted to "move on", adding that "basically, as he turns 78 years old, he is saying to himself that life is more beautiful than 10 days of court hearings".

In the original trial, Depardieu was said to have touched the two women on intimate parts of the body during the making of Les Volets Verts, which is a film about an ageing actor coming to terms with his declining powers.

He himself admitted to using vulgar language but denied being "a metro-train groper". However, he also said he did not consider touching a woman's buttocks to be an act of "sexual aggression".

The trial was the first time the star of films like Green Card and Cyrano de Bergerac appeared in court on sexual assault charges. Several other women have made similar allegations in the media.

A decision on whether an alleged rape case can come to trial is expected this month.

#14. Lewis Hamilton visits Egypt’s Rafah crossing and Gaza aid facilities

Seven-time F1 world champion tours border area and logistics warehouses operated by Egyptian Red Crescent

Lewis Hamilton has visited Egypt’s Rafah border crossing with the Gaza Strip to meet organisations sending humanitarian aid to the territory.

The seven-time Formula One world champion has spoken out about conflict and humanitarian crises in the region, and has called for peace. He has frequently posted on social media about children killed in Israeli airstrikes in Gaza and called for a ceasefire.

“I think we need to make sure we’re pushing to free Congo, free Sudan, free Syria, free Palestine. I think there’s a lot of peace that I’m hoping we get to at some stage,” Hamilton said in October.

A ceasefire agreement reached last year has halted major combat operations in Gaza, but the amount of aid reaching the territory continues to fall far short of the agreed-upon minimum of 600 trucks a day.

Israeli forces have continued to advance, expanding their control from 53% of the territory to at least 60%, an independent commission of UN experts said last month.

Airstrikes also continue, and Israeli fire along the advancing front has killed at least 196 Palestinians, including 18 women, 32 boys and 11 girls, the commission said.

Hamilton toured Egypt’s Rafah border crossing with Gaza before visiting logistics warehouses operated by the Egyptian Red Crescent, which facilitates the entry of aid to Gaza.

At the crossing, Hamilton greeted aid workers with “Assalamu alaikum” a widely used Arabic greeting meaning “peace be upon you.” He shook hands and briefly spoke to workers and officials.

Khaled Megawer, the governor of North Sinai, where Rafah is located, said the visit was intended to help Hamilton learn about Egypt’s efforts to send aid to Gaza.

The visit comes after Hamilton made his 400th race start on Sunday at the São Paulo Grand Prix at Interlagos.

The first black F1 driver, he made his debut with McLaren in 2007. He is only the second F1 driver to start 400 races after his former teammate Fernando Alonso, who achieved the milestone in 2024.

#12. Pope Endures Grueling Travel in South America Weeks After Surgery

When doctors operated on Pope Leo XIV’s lung in October, some wondered if he’d postpone a long-planned trip to South America. It’s gone well so far.

Motoko Rich, the Rome bureau chief, has been traveling with Pope Leo XIV through Uruguay, Argentina and Peru.

Flying with Pope Leo XIV over the Andes, halfway through the pontiff’s 12-day trip to South America, the journalists traveling at the back of the plane suspected something was amiss.

They had expected the pope to come out from behind the business-class curtain and take questions, as he had on previous papal flights. Instead, Vatican officials abruptly told the press corps that the pope would not be speaking with them on the nearly five-hour flight from Argentina to Peru.

Was the pope, who had been diagnosed with lung cancer less than a month earlier, feeling ill? Was he too exhausted to talk? Was he taking a nap?

“It’s evident he’s well,” said Maria Montserrat Alvarado, who heads the Vatican’s communications department, when asked why the pope wasn’t taking questions. “He’s running circles around us.”

It was perhaps inevitable that Leo’s every action on this journey, so soon after surgery to remove a lobe of his right lung, would be scrutinized for signs of fatigue or illness, particularly as the Vatican is not known for its transparency.

The three-country trip, which began in Uruguay and concludes in Peru on Monday, involves a grueling schedule of 12 flights, two helicopter rides, nine Masses, 36 speeches and numerous meetings with local bishops and community members.

#11. UK and allies call on Israel to allow rapid humanitarian access to Gaza

A heavy storm earlier this month damaged tents and caused widespread flooding

The UK, Australia, Canada and Italy have issued a joint statement urging Israel to "allow immediate, rapid and unimpeded humanitarian access throughout Gaza", where they say the situation on the ground is "dire".

The foreign ministers warn recent floods have brought "further suffering" to the war-torn territory, which recently had its first winter storm.

The statement says 150,000 tents and other vital supplies are being held at the border.

The Israeli military authority which controls the Gaza crossings has said it is co-ordinating with the US-led Board of Peace and the UN on a winter plan, and that 93,000 tents and 8,600 aid trucks with winter supplies had entered the territory.

It added that reconstruction "can only begin after Hamas fully disarms".

The UN Office for the Co-ordination of Humanitarian Affairs said the widespread flooding and a landslide led to a number of deaths, thousands of damaged shelters and the suspension of critical services.

The statement from the British, Australian, Canadian and Italian foreign ministers says, external: "This situation is entirely reversible - and there is still time to act before the winter fully sets in."

"The crossings must be opened, and the tents allowed in. Dual-use items necessary for humanitarian operations and early recovery must be granted entry before it is too late."

The European Union issued a similar statement on Saturday, external, reiterating its "grave concern" over what it called the "persistent and devastating humanitarian crisis".

The EU also urged Israel to comply with international law.

The war in Gaza was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, in which about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Israel responded by launching a military campaign in Gaza, during which more than 74,690 people have been killed, according to the territory's Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

Israeli air strikes and other attacks have continued to take place almost every day despite the ceasefire agreed in October last year.

#10. Raye, Rosalia and Dolly Parton recognised in Grammy nominations

Raye's latest record This Album May Contain Hope also won the Mercury Prize last month

British singer Raye leads the charge at this year's Grammy Awards with 11 nominations, while Dolly Parton has been recognised with two posthumous nods.

Raye is nominated for her latest Mercury-winning record This Album May Contain Hope and its lead single Where Is My Husband!, which is up for both song and record of the year.

Other nominees include RosalÃ­a, Ella Langley, Olivia Dean, Ariana Grande, Bruno Mars, Taylor Swift, Olivia Rodrigo, Miranda Lambert and Phoebe Bridgers.

Parton's two nominations, both in the best country duo/group performance category, come three months after the country music star died aged 80.

Parton is recognised for Butterfly, her collaboration with the Zac Brown Band, and Light of a Clear Blue Morning, alongside an all-star line-up including her goddaughter Miley Cyrus.

The Grammy Awards 2027 will take place on Sunday 7 February in Los Angeles.

Dolly Parton scored two nominations, three months after her death aged 80

Madonna's album Confessions II, a sequel to 2005's Confessions on a Dancefloor, was absent from the "big four" categories - including album, record and song of the year.

That will be seen by many as the Grammys continuing their tradition of snubbing the US singer, but the album did make it into the best dance category.

RosalÃ­a's nominations comes after she won big at last weeks' Latin Grammy Awards

There are five new categories this year, including best Latin song and best Asian pop music performance.

They have been added as part of the Academy's attempts to make the event "more inclusive" and represent a wider range of music genres.

However, critics fear that the new specialist categories will result in genres such as K-pop being under-represented in the major categories, and acts including BTS and Stray Kids have said they will boycott this year's ceremony.

Instead, the nominees for the first Asian pop music performance included Babymetal and Lisa from Blackpink.

Songs and albums had to be released between 31 August 2025 and 28 August 2026 to qualify for this year's event.

Around 16,000 voting members of the Recording Academy are eligible to vote for the winners.

#9. Zimbabwean author Simbarashe Kundizeza cleared of using AI to write prize-winning story

After a month-long investigation it was concluded that Kundizeza’s story The Blue Fighter, which won the £10,000 Caine prize, was ‘human-written’

The Zimbabwean author Simbarashe Kundizeza has been cleared of using AI to write a prize-winning short story, after the prestigious Caine prize for African writers concluded that his entry was “human-written” after a month-long investigation.

Kundizeza’s story The Blue Fighter, which charts the declining fortunes of a family after the father invests in fish tanks, was announced as the winner of the £10,000 prize on 6 October.

Five days later, the Atlantic published an article titled “Yet another literary prize winner seems to be AI”, in which the author and journalist Vauhini Vara said the AI-detection software Pangram, which itself uses AI, had deemed the story to be almost completely artificially generated.

The Caine prize chair, Ellah Wakatama, said in a statement: “From our detailed investigation, we feel the evidence is clear: The Blue Fighter was human-written. We now look forward to awarding and celebrating Simbarashe Steyn Kundizeza … before the end of the year.”

She said: “When concerns were raised, we have a duty as an organisation to look at them carefully and fairly,” adding that the prize had taken a “human-centred” approach to its investigation.

“Our review included interviews with the author, the 2026 judging panel and The Shallow Tales Review, where the story was first published,” Wakatama said. “It also included independent and expert-led metadata analysis of drafts written by the author.”

Kundizeza, who had strongly denied using generative AI to write the story, said he was “relieved” that the allegations had been dismissed after the investigation. “It was not necessary in the first place, I am happy to put all this behind me and continue with my writing journey,” he said.

Vara’s article prompted outrage online, with many claiming that Black writers, and Africans in particular, were being unfairly singled out by allegations of the use of AI.

Associated Press contributed to this report
