#8. Man sentenced over 'horrific' attempted murder of three children

In July Riad Bouchaker was found guilty of the eight charges he faced

A 52-year-old man has been sentenced to life in prison for the attempted murder of three children outside a primary school in Dublin's Parnell Square in November 2023.

Riad Bouchaker, of no fixed address, was also found guilty of assault causing serious harm to creche worker Leanne Flynn, and of assaulting two other children and a teenager.

On Monday, Judge Tony Hunt imposed the overall sentence of life imprisonment in Dublin's Central Criminal Court.

Rioting across many parts of Dublin following the attack led to the deployment of the most riot police in Irish history.

Bouchaker was originally from Algeria but had lived in Ireland for 25 years prior to the attack, and he had Irish citizenship.

He was assisted by an Arabic interpreter and a speech-and-language expert during the trial, who were present in court for the sentencing.

The attack took place outside a primary school on Parnell Square East

The attack took place outside Gaelscoil CholÃ¡iste Mhuire in Parnell Square, shortly after 13:40 local time on 23 November.

A group of 13 children, aged five and six, were lined up on Parnell Square waiting to go to their afterschool provision. Childcare worker Leanne Flynn was helping a little boy zip up his jacket when Bouchaker pulled his knife on the children.

A five-year-old girl was left with a lifelong brain injury after being stabbed in the heart.

Now eight, she is non-verbal and can only communicate by blinking, uses a wheelchair and has to be fed through a tube in her stomach as she cannot swallow safely by herself.

The judge said the child will never live independently and that her life, and the lives of her family, had been permanently altered.

He also noted that Flynn suffered life-threatening injuries, including a collapsed lung and the loss of a spleen, as well as profound psychological consequences and her services not being available to children.

The judge said she had acted fearlessly to protect the children and hoped she could return to work in the future, but said the possibility of such remains an unanswered question.

Speaking outside court Garda (Irish police) Det Supt Colm Murphy acknowledged the actions of those "who immediately reacted the to the situation as it unfolded, in particular to Leanne Flynn â her actions on the day saved lives".

Murphy, who was the senior investigating officer in charge added: "To those who intervened and stopped the attack again, their actions saved lives".

He also gave praise to medical personnel on the ground and hospital staff, saying that they too "saved lives".

Murphy also acknowledged the principal of Gaelscoil CholÃ¡iste Mhuire, "for the way he and the staff at the school reacted on the day", and also to the parents who had to endure such a traumatic incident".

Judge Hunt said this was an unprecedented, highly unusual case involving a sudden, ferocious, sustained attack on young children and their childcare worker.

He said he had to mark the exceptional gravity of the offending against very young children and their carer, with a lethal weapon leading to consequences of the utmost gravity.

He said the circumstances were uniquely horrific and marked a profound assault on the most fundamental values protected by the criminal law.

The judge said the children happened to be selected because they were there and he was not yet sure what inference could be drawn from how Bouchaker found himself at their collection point.

At an earlier hearing, the court heard he received brain surgery in 2021 and a portion of his skull was removed, leaving a part of his brain vulnerable.

He suffered a head injury when members of the public intervened during the Parnell Square attack, after which he received hospital treatment for a month.

His defence team, which the judge ordered cannot be named due to safety concerns, said Bouchaker's cognitive function, memory, concentration, communication and decision-making were affected as a result of the impairment.

The most riot police in Irish state history were deployed to deal with the rioting following the attack

The attack was followed by riots across much of Dublin.

The most riot police in Irish state history were deployed to deal with the unrest.

Within hours of the attack, rioters destroyed 11 police vehicles, while 13 shops were badly damaged and more were looted during clashes with riot police.

Three buses and a tram were also destroyed and several police officers were injured during three hours of sustained violence.

Ireland's police chief at the time, Drew Harris blamed the rioting on a "lunatic, hooligan faction driven by a far-right ideology".

#7. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

History was made Thursday when he was indicted by a New York grand jury. It’s the first time that a current or former US president has been criminally charged.

The Manhattan district attorney’s office has been investigating Trump’s alleged role in a hush money payment scheme and cover-up involving adult film star Stormy Daniels that dates to the 2016 presidential campaign.

Trump’s attorney said he plans to file “substantial” legal challenges. The former president has repeatedly denied wrongdoing in the matter and accused Democrats of targeting him politically.

While Trump couldn’t see his admirers as he entered a side door on 56th Street, his arrival came with more fanfare and security than at any other time in his post presidency.

Trump’s mood was described by an adviser as “defiant and focused,” as he watched expansive television coverage while flying back to his native New York City from Florida.

Special counsel prosecutors have secured evidence including daily notes, texts, emails and photographs and are focused on cataloging how Donald Trump handled classified records around the Mar-a-Lago resort and those who may have witnessed the former president with them, according to multiple sources familiar with the investigation.

The new details come amid signs the Justice Department is taking steps that are typically near the end of an investigation.

The recent investigative activity before a federal grand jury in Washington, DC, also includes subpoenaing witnesses in March and April who had previously spoken to investigators, the sources said. While the FBI interviewed many aides and workers at Mar-a-Lago nearly a year ago voluntarily, grand jury appearances are transcribed and under oath — an indication the prosecutors are locking in witness testimony.

The focus of both the mishandling of records and obstruction of justice probes has remained on the actions of the former president, the sources say. That includes prosecutors pursuing evidence of Trump’s intent to keep classified records after he left the White House, plus his knowledge that the records remained in his possession after the Justice Department demanded their return last May.

Witnesses are being questioned about what they saw in Trump’s private residential and work areas within the club, some of the sources said.

Trump is “one of the most famous people in the world, and he is a former president, presidential candidate, and now he’s a defendant. We haven’t seen that. It’s hard to kind of process that,” Borger said to CNN’s Dana Bash.

Borger also highlighted how Trump’s day in court is something he’s avoided for decades and that it will be a grievance he uses to rile up his supporters.

And I think we’re going to see that play out over and over again, and we’ll see it play out in court with his attorney,” she said.

Lawyers for former President Donald Trump told a New York judge on Monday that they oppose the request by media outlets to broadcast Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

“We submit that the media request should be denied because it will create a circus-like atmosphere at the arraignment, raise unique security concerns, and is inconsistent with President Trump’s presumption of innocence,” the Trump team said in a letter to New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchant.

Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Among the Trump lawyers signing the new letter was Todd Blanche, who, CNN reported, recently joined the former president’s legal team.

Meanwhile, the Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request, and said that it was deferring to the judge to decide how best to manage his court room.

However, the district attorney’s office wrote that it would “be a defensible exercise of the Court’s discretion to exclude or restrict videography, photography, and radio coverage of the arraignment in the interest of avoiding potential prejudice to the defendant, maintaining an orderly proceeding, assuring the safety of the participants in the proceeding, or for other reasons within the Court’s broad authority to manage and control these proceedings.”

But the prosecutors also noted that there “does not appear to be a categorical prohibition on cameras during an arraignment” under existing New York statutes and case law.

While the district attorney’s office stopped short of opposing the request, its lawyer pointed out to Merchan that a similar request for audio-video broadcasting was made for the 2021 arraignment in the tax fraud case against Trump Organization and its CFO Allen Weisselberg.

The judge responded to that 2021 request by “allowing a limited number of still photographs to be taken prior to the commencement of proceedings,” the prosecutors noted Monday.

CNN is one of the outlets requesting camera access for Tuesday’s arraignment.

#6. Airstrikes on Iran Missile Sites May Shed Light on Justification for Dams

Environmentalists have long puzzled over a wide network of water projects lacking any obvious justification. Then their areas were targeted in airstrikes.

Environmentalists have long been mystified by the significant proliferation of dams and water transfer projects like pipelines and tunnels across Iran, especially in regions that had no apparent need for them.

Some analysts think they now know the answer after U.S. and Israeli airstrikes on concealed Iranian missile sites this year targeted many of the same areas. These analysts have concluded that the water projects may have been developed to support the country’s military buildup.

Given the secrecy surrounding Iran’s missile program, any connection between it and Iran’s ambitious, decades-old initiative to develop water infrastructure remains speculative. But some experts find the evidence highly suggestive.

Nima Shokri, an expert on water security, and his team at the Hamburg University of Technology, in Germany, had for years tracked an unusually high number of dams across Iran — at least 533 of them, among the most of any country in the region.

“It was always puzzling to me,” he said. “Why were they building so many dams?”

In the spring, Dr. Shokri was perusing news about the war in his home country when he came across a map plotting airstrikes that had targeted suspected secret Iranian missile sites. The pattern — a semicircle of dots from north to south — nearly matched that of his own map marking the locations of dams.

His old question, he said, was superseded by a new one: “Why does it seem that wherever we have dams, there is a missile strike close by?”

#5. Five EU countries expected to announce migrant deportation centre in Rwanda

Dinghys in the Mediterranean (file pic) are often overcrowded

Five EU countries are expected to announce the creation of a migrant deportation centre in the landlocked African country Rwanda, according to diplomatic sources, part of a broader push to boost deportations and curb irregular migration.

Germany, Denmark, Austria, Greece and the Netherlands said in September they were working towards a deal with a non-EU country to host "return hubs".

In August, Rwanda said it had begun "preliminary discussions" with several European states.

It is understood the scheme would only apply to asylum seekers who had exhausted legal avenues to remain in the EU, unlike a scrapped British deal that envisaged sending people to Rwanda to have their claims processed.

Legal challenges meant the UK deal never took off, and it was eventually junked by the incoming Labour government in 2024.

The five EU countries say the plan to send migrants abroad is in line with EU and international law, but human rights bodies say it could violate migrant rights.

The deal follows a recent EU reform that allowed for the creation of hubs outside of the bloc.

Details are expected to be announced on Monday in Vienna. Diplomatic sources told the AFP news agency the hub in the east-central African nation is expected to initially run as a pilot, hosting about 100 people, with each of the five nations possibly sending about 20 migrants.

The EU countries reportedly expressed hope that a centre would be running in 2027. The United Nations' International Organisation for Migration (IOM) could play an ancillary role.

"IOM has a long history of providing assistance to migrants in situations of transit, including in complex contexts," an IOM spokesperson said, adding the agency was in discussions with some EU countries "on plans for the establishment of return hubs or similar mechanisms".

With elections approaching in several EU nations, administrations have been under pressure to show they can control migration.

Concerns around security, struggling economies and disillusionment with governing parties have all had a major role in shaping attitudes towards people leaving their home countries.

Human Rights Watch's senior Africa researcher Clementine de Montjoye said the choice of Rwanda would be a cause of concern as it had a "well-established record of repression of dissent, arbitrary detention, ill-treatment in detention" and support for armed rebels in the neighbouring Democratic Republic of Congo.

"Paying Kigali to host a deportation programme would not only cause suffering and harm to those deported, it would finance and provide political leverage to a government responsible for serious human rights abuses at home and abroad."

The independent EU Agency for Fundamental Rights argued any third country hosting a return hub should "respect fundamental rights", including that people should not be sent to a country where they face serious risk of persecution.

Unaccompanied children should not be sent to a return hub, the agency said.

According to the European Border and Coast Guard Agency, there were almost 110,000 irregular border crossings into the EU in the first 10 months of 2026, which represents a 29% decrease compared to the previous year.

In October, about 18,100 crossings were detected, which is about 6% less than October 2025 but 22% more than in September "largely because of a sharp rise in arrivals on the Western African route to the Canary Islands".

#4. Elon Musk joins 'Bring Jensen Back' campaign to reinstate the ex-Nvidia boss

SpaceX boss Elon Musk and Nvidia's former chief executive Jensen Huang

Allies of Jensen Huang are trying to rally support for the former Nvidia boss to get his job back after resigning last week over a scandal involving extramarital affairs, one involving an alleged Chinese spy.

SpaceX boss Elon Musk and Tim Sweeney, chief executive of Fortnite-maker Epic Games, are among those backing a campaign to get the AI chip giant's board to "Bring Jensen Back".

Huang resigned as chief executive of Nvidia after the emergence of two affairs, one of whom was with a woman suspected of spying for China and another allegedly with one of his employees.

It has created a crisis for one of the world's most valuable firms which is a key part of the AI industry and its huge expansion.

An open letter calling for Nvidia's board to give Huang his job back while an internal probe is conducted has received more than 175,000 signatures, including Musk and Sweeney, according to a website named "Stand with Jensen".

The campaign group said it is made up of current and former Nvidia employees, partners, shareholders and supporters.

"We write with gratitude for Jensen Huang and with a clear demand to the board: bring Jensen back while an independent investigation establishes the facts, unless and until you can provide compelling evidence that his removal is in NVIDIA's best interests," the letter said.

Allies of the ex-Nvidia boss wrote they were "concerned that Jensen was removed without meaningful due process".

Huang had been expected to weather the storm following the revelation of an affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy.

However, he resigned last Thursday after a second extra-marital relationship emerged, this time allegedly with an executive at Nvidia who reported to him.

The scandal-hit tech boss is now discussing a possible return to Nvidia with confidants, according to the Financial Times., external

The newspaper quoted one ally saying that a "war room" of Silicon Valley executives was being convened to lobby for the former Nvidia boss's reinstatement.

A representative for Jensen Huang declined to comment on the report.

The so-called war room also wants to get support for Huang from the White House.

While President Donald Trump recently spoke in glowing terms about Huang, the scandal has created tensions between the US Treasury and Commerce departments over shipments of high-end Nvidia chips to Alice Huang's former firm, Megaspeed.

It has raised questions about the possibility that the chips may have ended up in China despite US export restrictions.

The former Nvidia boss has agreed to testify in Congress, including before the House Select Committee on China.

The BBC understands that the likelihood of Huang being reinstated is low while the Nvidia's internal investigations are ongoing.

The FBI is also investigating the affair between the former Nvidia boss and Alice Huang, according to Trump.

Musk claimed that "critical deals" involving Nvidia's technology would be lost following Huang's resignation.

"Jensen drove supply chain brilliantly at Nvidia but many of those deals were a handshake with Jensen, not a written contract.

"Without Jensen, at least some of those critical deals will be lost," he posted on X., external

He later added: "If Jensen gave me his word about compute delivery or I asked for components to be accelerated, he always came through."

If Huang was reinstated, it would face scrutiny from Washington. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has called for Nvidia to hire its next chief executive from outside the company.

Shareholders in Nvidia also want Huang back at Nvidia's helm.

In an open letter to the board, Brad Gerstner, founder of Altimeter Capital and Atreides Management boss Gavin Baker called for management to leave "all options on the table, including the potential return of Jensen Huang".

Lord Sarfraz, a member of the UK's House of Lords, has also written to Nvidia's new chair, Harvey Jones, urging the firm's directors to give "serious consideration" to Huang's return following the investigation.

For the moment, Nvidia's chief financial officer Colette Kress is leading the firm and will present the company's results on Tuesday.

#3. Relocation of Cape Town baboons to sanctuary ignites heated protests

Demonstrators tried to block vehicle carrying the baboons, whose capture authorities say is needed to protect both primate and human

The relocation of a troop of baboons in Cape Town to a wildlife sanctuary has triggered furious protests by local people opposed to their capture, which officials argue is necessary to stop human-wildlife conflict in the city.

The 44-member Waterfall troop (named after the area they mainly inhabit) was captured in the southern seaside suburb of Simon’s Town on Friday and Saturday. A second, smaller Seaforth troop is also due to be moved.

The growth in both baboon and human populations in Cape Town in recent years has led to increased conflict and more baboons dying as a result. Meanwhile, a heated debate has raged between people who believe that baboons and humans can coexist and those who argue that baboons in urban areas are in danger and in some cases need to be relocated. Some animal rights activists have compared authorities’ baboon management to apartheid.

During Friday’s relocation, outraged protesters tried to stop vehicles taking the baboons away. One person sprayed a fire extinguisher, according to the Daily Maverick, a local news outlet.

After the confrontation, the local Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals branch urged those opposed to the baboons’ capture not to try to stop the vehicles. “Capture and transport are already stressful for the baboons,” it said in a statement. “Delaying their arrival prolongs their time in crates and risks adding to that stress – this is not fair on the baboons.”

It said it remained opposed to the baboon’s relocation, adding: “Our presence did not imply support … our inspectors are present because the animals need someone looking out for them.”

On Sunday, dozens of people sat outside the wildlife centre in a silent protest, some tying flowers to the fence. Baboons from a different troop also gathered at the boundary, calling to the newly captive animals, the Daily Maverick reported.

“We stand together for the Seaforth and Waterfall troops, for their freedom, their dignity and their right to live as wild animals,” Mandy Neal, the administrator of the Save Kogelfontein Baboons Facebook group, posted on Monday, calling for weekly Sunday silent protests. “ Our message is simple: CAPTIVITY IS NOT CONSERVATION.”

The City of Cape Town said in a statement on Sunday that the relocation had been successful, with the baboons being monitored 24/7 after being checked by vets and the females receiving contraceptive implants.

It said: “The Waterfall troop has been relocated due to the severity and persistence of human-baboon conflict in the Simon’s Town urban environment, the limited available natural low-lying habitat, the increasing risk to the baboons’ welfare, and public safety concerns … [and] as a very last resort after other interventions were evaluated and deemed ineffective, and as an alternative to culling.”

Prof Justin O’Riain, a behavioural ecologist at the University of Cape Town, said the baboon management plan had undergone two years of public consultations. “It has thus been disappointing watching members of the public and select NGOs, many which did not engage in the stakeholder process, now compromising the safety and welfare of the baboons and people through violent actions directed at staff and baboons in transit.”

#2. Yemeni forces retreat to defend port city from Houthis

Pro-government forces said troops were being moved to defend the interim capital, Aden (file photo)

Yemeni pro-government forces backed by Saudi Arabia say they are redeploying troops to defend the country's interim capital, Aden, following weeks of intensified fighting with the Iran-backed Houthi movement.

The Southern Armed Forces said it was withdrawing from an area overlooking the strategically important Bab al-Mandab Strait, at the southern end of the Red Sea, to reinforce positions around Aden and neighbouring areas.

Houthi-run media say the movement has taken control of the evacuated territory. Saudi Arabia has not commented.

The shift ends a weeks-long offensive to try to secure the area near the key shipping route which the Houthis seized in a surprise offensive in September.

Pro-government forces said they had "secured" the strait in early October as they sought to retake crucial ground from the Houthis. The Iran-backed fighters said they had captured areas including Perim Island at the mouth of the strait and the strategic port city of Mokha a month prior.

But the Saudi-backed Southern Armed Forces fighting on behalf of Yemen's internationally recognised government said on Sunday they were withdrawing to reinforce troops elsewhere, with the southern city of Aden a "priority".

It said troops were being withdrawn "from Dhubab Airport and the Bab al-Mandab area" with the aim of "strengthening the front lines and addressing existing threats".

"This step is driven by the priority of securing the capital, Aden, and neighbouring governorates, reinforcing the front lines and addressing existing threats."

Watch: Why is the Bab al-Mandab Strait so important?

The Houthis said they posed no threat to international shipping passing through the waterway when they captured territory overlooking it, but that they would continue to target vessels from Saudi Arabia.

However, the seizure contributed to a surge in global oil prices because Saudi Arabia had been relying on the Red Sea for oil exports since the US and Israel's war with Iran effectively closed the Strait of Hormuz.

A UN Human Rights spokesperson, Ravina Shamdasani, urged all parties to protect civilians and civilian infrastructure.

She noted that since the escalation of fighting in August, more than 60% of documented civilian casualties were women and children.

The Houthis have repeatedly attacked Aden in recent weeks, including an assault on 15 October that targeted the presidential palace, killing five and injuring 16.

Saudi Arabia responded with hundreds of air strikes, while the Houthis launched hundreds of drones and missiles at its cities and infrastructure.

The UN has estimated that nearly 330,000 people have been displaced in Yemen as the fighting has since escalated. Hundreds of people are reported to have been killed.

Yemeni military says it has 'secured' Red Sea waterway

Yemenis flee across Red Sea as Houthis and Saudi-backed forces escalate war

Yemen has been devastated by a civil war that began in 2014, when the Houthis ousted the government from its capital, Sanaa.

The conflict escalated in 2015 after a Saudi-led coalition of Arab states intervened in an attempt to restore the government's rule.

A four-year informal ceasefire began to unravel in July, when the Houthis announced a "maritime embargo" against Saudi Arabia and began missile and drone attacks on Saudi airports, oil facilities and tankers in the Red Sea.

The Houthis said they were retaliating for a Saudi blockade of ports and airports in their territory, as well as an air strike on Sanaa airport that they blamed on the kingdom.

#1. US basketball player arrested with 75lb of marijuana at Nigerian airport, say authorities

Drug smuggling carries life sentence in Nigeria

An American basketball player has been arrested in Nigeria for allegedly attempting to smuggle 33.95kg (75lb) of marijuana into the country.

Nigeria’s National Drug Law Enforcement Agency (NDLEA) said a 28-year-old player it named as Greene Da’monte Khiree from the state of Georgia was arrested at Lagos’s main airport last Wednesday after he arrived on a flight from Thailand via the United Arab Emirates.

The NDLEA said the player told them he had come to Nigeria to “build networks” for his basketball career. The agency said evidence on his phone suggested he had “knowledge of and involvement in the drug trafficking activity”. The NDLEA said it detained the player when he attempted to leave the airport with his hand luggage after abandoning two checked-in bags. The agency said those two bags were found to contain 32 parcels of marijuana, and a tracking device.

Nigeria has strict drug laws, and trafficking carries a sentence of life in prison.

A basketball player by the name of Damonte Greene is listed on the roster of the Pattani Raptors in the Thai Premier Men’s League. Greene, a Georgia native, played college basketball for Reinhardt University and South Georgia Technical College. He played semi-professional basketball in the US before representing a number of teams in Thailand.

WNBA star Brittney Griner and former Utah State player Jarred Shaw were imprisoned in Russia and Indonesia respectively for marijuana offenses. Griner was released in December 2022, while Shaw remains in prison.
